### [1] [Geometry Parameterisation via a Structural Modal Basis for Aerodynamic Shape Optimisation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05568)
*Alwin Wang,Robert Carrese,Luca Brown,Joel Collins,Pier Marzocca*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A structural modal parameterisation method (MPM) is introduced for aerodynamic shape optimisation in tightly constrained design spaces, using a tunable pseudo-structure's eigenmodes to parameterise geometry with concise, constraint-aware design variables.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Aircraft outer mould lines are heavily constrained by payload, manufacturability, and low-observability requirements, leaving only a small, highly constrained aerodynamic design space that is difficult to represent with a concise and physically meaningful set of design variables.

Method: The method builds a tunable pseudo-structure and solves its eigenvalue problem to derive a modal basis. A selected subset of modes then parameterises the geometry. Boundary conditions, stiffness distribution, density, thickness, and added masses serve as intentional basis-design variables that shape the admissible deformation space prior to optimisation.

Result: The design coordinates are flow-solver independent, making the approach broadly applicable. It achieves a practical balance of parameter conciseness, robust constraint awareness, and geometric flexibility for tightly constrained aerodynamic shape optimisation.

Conclusion: MPM provides an effective parameterisation strategy for heavily constrained aerodynamic design problems where traditional design variable representations fall short.

Abstract: Aircraft outer mould lines are often heavily constrained early by payload requirements, manufacturability limits, and low-observability considerations. The remaining aerodynamic design space is typically small, highly constrained, and difficult to represent using a concise and physically meaningful set of design variables. A structural modal parameterisation method (MPM) is introduced for such problems. The method constructs a tunable pseudo-structure and solves its eigenvalue problem to obtain a modal basis, from which a selected subset of modes parameterises the geometry. Boundary conditions, stiffness distribution, density, thickness, and added masses are treated as intentional basis-design variables that shape the admissible deformation space prior to optimisation. The resulting design coordinates are independent of any specific flow solver, making the approach broadly applicable. For tightly constrained aerodynamic shape optimisation problems, it provides a practical balance of parameter conciseness, robust constraint awareness, and geometric flexibility.

</details>


### [2] [Efficient $C^1$ Bernstein Quasi-Trefftz Discretization for Heterogeneous High-Frequency Helmholtz Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05849)
*Shelvean Kapita*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper develops an efficient C^1 Bernstein quasi-Trefftz method for heterogeneous high-frequency Helmholtz problems on unstructured triangular meshes, reducing local polynomial spaces to trace-sized dimensions via Helmholtz residual constraints and demonstrating high-order accuracy with substantial computational cost reductions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-frequency Helmholtz problems on heterogeneous media require methods that can resolve oscillatory solutions without excessive degrees of freedom. Traditional conforming polynomial approximations suffer from large local dimensions (quadratic growth in polynomial degree), and handling variable coefficients alongside Trefftz-type accuracy poses significant challenges.

Method: The method imposes strong C^1 continuity on local macro-patches via Bernstein-Bézier smoothness relations, then compresses the conforming polynomial space by enforcing projected Helmholtz residual constraints. On two-triangle patches this reduces local dimension to 2p+1. Variable matrix-valued coefficients are treated through local polynomial projection. A graph-residual formulation couples reduced patch spaces into a sparse global system. The implementation uses explicit C^1 continuation, batched coefficient projection, batched residual construction, and stable local kernel extraction via QR factorization.

Result: Numerical experiments on heterogeneous unstructured meshes demonstrate high-order accuracy, roundoff-level C^1 conformity, robust high-frequency resolution, and substantial reductions in both local and global computational cost. The method handles turning-point and penetrable-scattering examples with flexibility.

Conclusion: The proposed quasi-Trefftz approach effectively combines C^1 smoothness constraints with residual-based space reduction to deliver an efficient, high-order method for heterogeneous high-frequency Helmholtz problems, with trace-sized local spaces and clear separation of coefficient-approximation and discretization errors.

Abstract: We develop an efficient $C^1$ Bernstein quasi-Trefftz discretization for heterogeneous high-frequency Helmholtz problems on unstructured triangular meshes. The method first imposes strong $C^1$ continuity through Bernstein--Bézier smoothness relations on local macro-patches and then reduces the resulting conforming polynomial space by enforcing projected Helmholtz residual constraints. On two-triangle patches, this compresses the local dimension from quadratic growth in the polynomial degree to a trace-sized space of dimension $2p+1$ under the natural rank condition. Variable matrix-valued coefficients are handled through local polynomial projection, allowing coefficient-approximation effects to be separated from discretization error. A graph-residual formulation couples the reduced patch spaces and produces a sparse global system in compressed coordinates. The implementation combines explicit $C^1$ continuation, batched coefficient projection, batched residual construction, and stable local kernel extraction by QR factorization. Numerical experiments on heterogeneous unstructured meshes demonstrate high-order accuracy, roundoff-level $C^1$ conformity, robust high-frequency resolution, and substantial reductions in local and global computational cost. Turning-point and penetrable-scattering examples further illustrate the flexibility of the approach.

</details>


### [3] [High-order smoothness and high-order collocation approximation for Volterra integral equation with multiscale and nonlinear exponent kernel](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05851)
*Wenlin Qiu,Chuanwei Su,Xiangcheng Zheng*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper addresses Volterra integral equations with multiscale nonlinear exponent kernels, proposing high-order smoothing conditions to eliminate initial solution singularities, and developing a discontinuous high-order collocation method with arbitrary polynomial degree on uniform or graded meshes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors aim to extend and complement prior work by Zheng, Qiu, and Stynes by addressing both mathematical aspects (singularity removal via smoothing conditions) and numerical aspects (discontinuous high-order collocation methods) for Volterra integral equations with multiscale nonlinear kernels.

Method: The paper proposes high-order smoothing conditions enabling the elimination of initial singularities to any prescribed order, leveraging the multiscale nature of the kernel. A discontinuous high-order collocation method with arbitrary polynomial degree is then developed and analyzed on uniform or graded meshes informed by solution regularity.

Result: The proposed smoothing conditions eliminate initial singularities to any prescribed order, and the discontinuous high-order collocation method is shown to be applicable and analyzable for equations with multiscale nonlinear exponent kernels.

Conclusion: This work provides a comprehensive mathematical and numerical framework for solving Volterra integral equations with multiscale nonlinear exponent kernels, extending previous results in both theoretical and computational directions.

Abstract: We consider a Volterra integral equation with multiscale and nonlinear exponent kernel. We propose the high-order smoothing conditions, under which the initial singularity of the solutions can be eliminated up to any prescribed order. This indicates the application of the multiscale nature of the kernel on local modification of the solutions. Then a discontinuous high-order collocation method with arbitrary polynomial degree is developed and analyzed on uniform or graded meshes based on the solution regularity. This work serves as a comprehensive extension and complement to [Zheng, Qiu and Stynes, SIAM J. Numer. Anal., to appear] in both mathematical and numerical aspects.

</details>


### [4] [A structure-preserving implicit-explicit method for a hyperbolic approximation of fourth-order PDEs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05939)
*Rahul Barthwal,Rahuldev Ghorai*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: Introduces a structure-preserving IMEX numerical method for a first-order hyperbolic relaxation system approximating general fourth-order PDEs, with proven energy consistency, positivity preservation, and relaxation-parameter-independent stability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a numerical scheme that preserves key physical structures (energy consistency and positivity) while maintaining stability in asymptotic regimes for hyperbolic approximations of fourth-order PDEs, such as thin film equations.

Method: Employs an implicit-explicit (IMEX) splitting between stiff and non-stiff terms within a first-order hyperbolic relaxation framework.

Result: Rigorous proof that the scheme is energy-consistent and positivity-preserving under a CFL-type condition, and remains robustly stable in asymptotic regimes with a time-step restriction independent of relaxation parameters.

Conclusion: The scheme's theoretical properties are validated through numerical examples for thin film equations, confirming its efficacy and robustness.

Abstract: We introduce a novel structure-preserving numerical method for a first-order hyperbolic approximation system, which approximates the solutions of general fourth-order partial differential equations. By employing an implicit-explicit (IMEX) splitting between the stiff and non-stiff terms, we rigorously prove that the proposed scheme is energy-consistent and positivity-preserving under a CFL-type condition. Furthermore, we show that the method remains robustly stable in asymptotic regimes, with a time-step restriction that is entirely independent of the relaxation parameters. Finally, we present a series of numerical examples for thin film equations to validate the theoretical properties and efficacy of the scheme.

</details>


### [5] [Robust high-dimensional integration using medians of coarsely scrambled Sobol' sequences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05979)
*Ziyang Ye,Chaokun Zhu,Zexin Pan*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper combines Suzuki's coarse scrambling with the median trick for randomized quasi-Monte Carlo (RQMC) integration using scrambled Sobol' sequences. The proposed approach achieves dimension-independent error bounds for L² integrands and near-optimal high-probability convergence rates for L^p integrands and Haar wavelet-space functions, overcoming the curse of dimensionality and the O(N^{-3/2}) RMSE barrier of classical RQMC.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical RQMC methods using scrambled Sobol' sequences have error bounds that grow exponentially in dimension s and RMSE convergence rates generally no better than O(N^{-3/2}), limiting their practical applicability for high-dimensional integration problems.

Method: The method combines two recent developments: (1) Suzuki's coarse scrambling, which randomizes Sobol' sequences based on their generating base polynomials and reduces the maximal gain coefficient—with appropriate base polynomial selection, the coefficient becomes uniformly bounded in s; and (2) the median trick, which aggregates independent coarsely scrambled estimates to achieve near-optimal convergence for function classes beyond L².

Result: The method yields O(N^{-1/2}) RMSE for L² integrands with dimension-independent constants. For L^p integrands (p∈(1,2)), the median achieves O(N^{-1+1/p}) error with high probability. For Haar wavelet space functions, they prove high-probability error bounds of O(N^{-α_p-1/2+ε}), which improve to O(N^{-r-α_p-1/2+ε}) when dominating mixed derivatives of order r≥1 exist. Bounds are uniform in s under suitable ANOVA component conditions. Numerical experiments confirm predicted rates.

Conclusion: The combination of coarse scrambling with the median trick provides a robust RQMC framework that overcomes the dimension-dependent error growth and convergence barriers of classical RQMC, achieving near-optimal rates for a broad class of integrands in high dimensions.

Abstract: We study the numerical approximation of high-dimensional integrals using randomized quasi-Monte Carlo (RQMC) methods, with a focus on scrambled Sobol' sequences. While asymptotically faster than Monte Carlo, classical RQMC suffers from error bounds that grow exponentially in the dimension $s$, and its root mean squared error (RMSE) convergence rate is generally no better than $O(N^{-3/2})$. To overcome these limitations, we combine two recent developments: Suzuki's coarse scrambling and the median trick. Coarse scrambling randomizes Sobol' sequences according to their generating base polynomials and significantly reduces the maximal gain coefficient. By appropriately choosing the base polynomials, we show that the coefficient can be made uniformly bounded in $s$, yielding an $O(N^{-1/2})$ RMSE for $L^2$ integrands with a dimension-independent constant. The median trick then enables near-optimal convergence for function classes beyond $L^2$: for $L^p$ integrands with $p\in(1,2)$, the median of independent coarsely scrambled estimates achieves an error of $O(N^{-1+1/p})$ with high probability; for integrands in the Haar wavelet space $\mathcal H_{\mathrm{wav},α,s,p,q}$ with $α_p:=α-(1/p-1/2)_+>0$, we prove a high-probability error bound of $O(N^{-α_p-1/2+\varepsilon})$ for any $\varepsilon>0$; the bound improves to $O(N^{-r-α_p-1/2+\varepsilon})$ when the integrand has dominating mixed derivatives of order $r\ge1$ that belong to $\mathcal H_{\mathrm{wav},α,s,p,q}$. The latter two bounds are uniform in $s$ under suitable conditions on the ANOVA components of the integrands. Numerical experiments confirm the predicted convergence rates and demonstrate the robustness of the proposed approach.

</details>


### [6] [Quasi-uniform designs from random candidates: optimal candidate complexity and farthest-point sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05986)
*Takashi Goda,Hengjun Xu*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper studies randomized constructions of quasi-uniform designs on compact metric-measure spaces with two-sided polynomial ball-growth. Using a candidate pool of M independent points followed by farthest-point sampling (FPS), the authors prove non-asymptotic probabilistic bounds on the mesh ratio, showing M=Θ(N log N) is the sharp candidate-pool size for bounded mesh ratio. The upper bound on mesh ratio tends to 2 under supercritical oversampling, and the analysis extends to approximate FPS, doubling-dimension implementations, and nested sequences with almost sure quasi-uniformity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quasi-uniform designs (point sets that are well-spread with bounded covering-to-separation ratio) are fundamental in numerical integration, approximation, and sampling. The authors aim to establish tight probabilistic guarantees for randomized constructions via farthest-point sampling from random candidate pools, resolving the sharp trade-off between candidate pool size and achieved mesh ratio.

Method: The approach draws M independent candidate points from a metric-measure space with two-sided polynomial ball-growth, then retains the first N points of a farthest-point traversal of the candidate set. Both exact and approximate FPS are analyzed. Non-asymptotic probabilistic bounds on mesh ratio are derived via covering/separation arguments exploiting the ball-growth conditions. Lower bounds are established to show sharpness of the M=Θ(N log N) threshold, and fast implementations are analyzed for spaces of bounded doubling dimension.

Result: Key results: (1) M=O(N log(N/δ)) suffices for bounded mesh ratio with probability ≥ 1−δ; (2) if M=o(N log N), the mesh ratio diverges in probability for every selection procedure from the same candidate pool—showing sharpness; (3) if M/(N log M)→∞, the exact-FPS mesh-ratio upper bound tends to 2; (4) approximate FPS effects are quantified; (5) a nested almost surely quasi-uniform sequence is constructed from a single infinite stream, with mesh-ratio limsup equal to 2 for compact positive-volume Euclidean subsets.

Conclusion: The paper fully resolves the candidate-pool complexity for randomized quasi-uniform designs via farthest-point sampling, establishing a sharp Θ(N log N) threshold. Under supercritical oversampling, the mesh ratio can be driven arbitrarily close to the information-theoretically optimal value of 2, and the construction extends naturally to nested, streaming settings.

Abstract: We study randomized constructions of quasi-uniform designs on a compact metric-measure space satisfying two-sided polynomial ball-growth conditions. Given integers $N\le M$, we first draw $M$ independent candidate points and then retain the first $N$ points of a farthest-point traversal of the candidate set. We prove probabilistic non-asymptotic bounds on the mesh ratio and show that $M=Θ(N\log N)$ is the sharp order of the candidate-pool size required for bounded mesh ratio: a sufficiently large multiple of $N\log(N/δ)$ suffices with probability at least $1-δ$, whereas, if $M=o(N\log N)$, the mesh ratio diverges in probability for every procedure that selects $N$ points from the same independent candidate pool. If $M/(N\log M)\to\infty$, the upper bound on the mesh ratio for the exact farthest-point sampling (FPS) tends to $2$. We also quantify the effect of approximate FPS and analyze direct and fast implementations in spaces of bounded doubling dimension. Finally, using a single infinite stream of candidate points and increasing candidate budgets, we construct an almost surely quasi-uniform nested sequence; under supercritical oversampling and exact FPS, or more generally when the approximation factors tend to $1$, its mesh-ratio limit superior equals $2$ for compact positive-volume subsets of Euclidean space.

</details>


### [7] [Crouzeix--Raviart--Marini realisation of computable a priori $L^2$-error bounds on anisotropic meshes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06057)
*Hiroki Ishizaka*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper analyzes conforming P1 and lowest-order Crouzeix-Raviart (CR) finite element approximations of the Dirichlet Poisson problem, deriving computable a priori L2-error bounds on anisotropic simplicial meshes without global H2-regularity assumptions. The key innovation is using the Marini relation to construct equilibrated Raviart-Thomas fluxes and controlling errors via the Marini constant (a generalized eigenvalue problem), combined with elementwise Poincaré constants.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional a priori error analysis for finite element methods typically requires global H2-regularity of the solution, which fails on anisotropic meshes and in domains with reentrant corners. The authors seek computable error bounds that do not rely on such global regularity assumptions, applicable to both conforming P1 and nonconforming CR elements.

Method: The paper considers conforming P1 and lowest-order CR approximations. For piecewise constant data, the Marini relation produces an equilibrated lowest-order Raviart-Thomas (RT) flux from the scalar CR solution. The square of the Marini constant is characterized as the largest generalized eigenvalue of a problem involving only scalar conforming and CR matrices. This constant, combined with the elementwise Poincaré constant, controls both conforming and CR errors. The CR L2 estimate uses positivity of the discrete CR-conforming gap operator rather than a nonconforming Aubin-Nitsche argument. An exact decomposition of the Marini defect separates it into the CR-conforming energy gap and an explicit geometric term without aspect-ratio factors.

Result: On algebraically graded L-shaped mesh families, the analysis shows that grading parameter q>3/2 yields a Marini constant κ_{M,h}=O(h), leading to computable O(h^2) L2-error bounds. The approach provides guaranteed, computable error estimates that work on anisotropic meshes without global regularity assumptions.

Conclusion: The paper establishes computable a priori L2-error bounds for both conforming P1 and CR elements on anisotropic simplicial meshes, using the Marini relation and generalized eigenvalue computations. The derived estimates hold without global H2-regularity and achieve optimal O(h^2) convergence rates on suitably graded L-shaped meshes.

Abstract: We consider conforming $\Pone$ and lowest-order Crouzeix--Raviart (CR) approximations of the Dirichlet Poisson problem in two and three dimensions. We obtain computable a priori $L^2$-error bounds on anisotropic simplicial meshes without a global $H^2$-regularity assumption. For piecewise constant data, the Marini relation gives an equilibrated lowest-order Raviart--Thomas (RT) flux from the scalar CR solution. The square of the resulting Marini constant is the largest generalised eigenvalue of a problem involving only scalar conforming and CR matrices. Combined with the elementwise Poincaré constant, it controls both the conforming and CR errors. The CR $L^2$ estimate uses positivity of the discrete CR--conforming gap operator rather than a nonconforming Aubin--Nitsche argument. We also derive an exact decomposition of the Marini defect into the CR--conforming energy gap and an explicit geometric term with no aspect-ratio factor. On the algebraically graded L-shaped mesh family considered here, $q>3/2$ yields $κ_{M,h}=O(h)$ and computable $O(h^2)$ $L^2$-operator bounds.

</details>


### [8] [Polytopic symmetric interior-penalty approximation of elliptic SPDEs driven by spatial white noise](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06082)
*Tristan Pryer*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper analyzes symmetric interior-penalty discontinuous Galerkin (SIPG) approximations for elliptic SPDEs driven by spatial Gaussian white noise on polytopic meshes. The authors prove sharp convergence rates of h^(2-d/2) for polynomial degree k≥1, yielding order h in 2D and h^(1/2) in 3D, valid even on polytopic meshes with arbitrarily many/small faces and non-convex/reentrant elements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The low regularity of the exact random field (driven by spatial white noise) precludes the standard SIP consistency argument used for deterministic problems. A new analytical framework is needed to establish convergence rates for DG approximations of stochastic elliptic PDEs on general polytopic meshes, especially given the interest in polygonal and non-convex element geometry.

Method: The approach restricts the same continuum white-noise functional canonically onto the discontinuous polynomial space, then separates the total error into two components: (1) a stochastic forcing-restriction error and (2) a deterministic discretisation error. The analysis leverages the discontinuous structure for exact element-local sampling, and treats polytopic elements including admissible non-convex and reentrant-corner geometries, where a finite-rank singular resolvent analysis handles angle-dependent regularity loss.

Result: For every fixed polynomial degree k≥1, a sharp two-sided estimate is proved: ‖Y−Y_h‖_{L²(Ξ;L²(Ω))} ≃ h^(2−d/2). This gives convergence rates of h in 2D and h^(1/2) in 3D, with a finite-dimensional lower bound confirming optimality. These rates are retained even for polytopic elements with arbitrarily many/small faces, non-convex elements, and (in 2D) polygons with a single reentrant corner. Numerical experiments confirm predicted convergence on irregular polygonal, non-convex polytopic, and reentrant domains.

Conclusion: The sharp first-order stochastic convergence rate is retained despite deterministic approximation challenges on complex polytopic geometries. The canonical restriction framework provides a rigorous and optimal error analysis for SIPG methods applied to white-noise-driven elliptic SPDEs, with the DG structure enabling exact element-local sampling of the restricted white noise.

Abstract: We analyse symmetric interior-penalty discontinuous Galerkin approximations of elliptic stochastic partial differential equations driven by spatial Gaussian white noise on quasi-uniform polytopic meshes. The low regularity of the exact random field precludes the usual SIP consistency argument. We instead restrict the same continuum white-noise functional canonically to the discontinuous polynomial space and separate the error into stochastic forcing-restriction and deterministic discretisation components.
  For every fixed polynomial degree $k\geq1$, we prove the sharp two-sided estimate \[ \|Y-Y_h\|_{L^2(Ξ;L^2(Ω))}
  \simeq
  h^{2-d/2}, \] giving rates $h$ in two dimensions and $h^{1/2}$ in three dimensions. A finite-dimensional lower bound establishes optimality. The analysis allows polytopic elements with arbitrarily many and arbitrarily small faces, including admissible non-convex elements. In two dimensions we also treat polygons with a single reentrant corner. Although the deterministic approximation exhibits an angle-dependent loss of regularity, the singular resolvent contribution is finite rank, and the sharp first-order stochastic rate is retained.
  The discontinuous structure yields an exact element-local sampler for the restricted white noise. Numerical experiments on irregular polygonal, non-convex polytopic and reentrant domains support the predicted convergence behaviour.

</details>


### [9] [A Second Order Positivity-Preserving Scheme for the Nonlocal Cahn-Hilliard System with Variable Mobility and Logarithmic Flory-Huggins Potential](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06092)
*Yuanyi Sheng,Zhengru Zhang*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A second-order finite difference scheme for the nonlocal Cahn-Hilliard system with variable mobility and singular logarithmic potential is analyzed. The scheme uses modified Crank-Nicolson temporal discretization, explicit mobility treatment, and a nonlinear regularization term for positivity preservation. Rigorous stability, unique solvability, and optimal convergence are established via higher-order asymptotic expansions and a two-stage error strategy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The nonlocal Cahn-Hilliard system with variable mobility and singular Flory-Huggins potential is analytically and numerically challenging. Key difficulties include: (1) variable mobility causes loss of ellipticity if treated implicitly and complicates the convergence analysis; (2) the singular logarithmic potential must preserve positivity at the discrete level; (3) rigorous convergence analysis for variable mobility has remained an open analytical challenge. There is a need for an efficient, positivity-preserving, and rigorously analyzable numerical scheme.

Method: The paper proposes a second-order finite difference scheme with: (1) modified Crank-Nicolson temporal discretization; (2) explicit treatment of variable mobility to guarantee ellipticity and reduce computational cost; (3) a nonlinear regularization term to strictly enforce positivity at the discrete level. For convergence analysis, two non-standard techniques are employed: a higher-order asymptotic expansion (up to third order in time and fourth order in space) and a two-stage error strategy (rough error estimate first, then refined optimal error analysis).

Result: The scheme is proven to be uniquely solvable and stable under a modified numerical energy. Rigorous convergence analysis yields the optimal convergence rate. Numerical experiments validate the theoretical results and demonstrate robustness of the proposed scheme.

Conclusion: The proposed second-order scheme successfully handles the challenging combination of variable mobility and singular logarithmic potential in the nonlocal Cahn-Hilliard system, providing rigorous theoretical guarantees (unique solvability, stability, optimal convergence) and validated numerical performance.

Abstract: A second order finite difference scheme is analyzed for the nonlocal Cahn-Hilliard system with variable mobility and the singular Flory-Huggins logarithmic potential. The temporal discretization employs a modified Crank-Nicolson formulation, while the mobility is treated explicitly to guarantee ellipticity and reduce computational cost. To strictly enforce the positivity-preserving property at the discrete level, a nonlinear regularization term is added into the numerical scheme. Rigorous analysis shows that the scheme is uniquely solvable and stable under a modified numerical energy. The well-known analytical challenge associated with the variable mobility has to be handled carefully. To overcome this, a convergence framework is constructed based on two non-standard techniques: (1) a higher-order asymptotic expansion (extending up to the third order in time and fourth order in space) to retain a sufficiently order of accuracy; (2) a two-stage error strategy, wherein a rough error estimate first guarantees the discrete boundedness of the mobility and nonlinear terms, followed by a refined error analysis that yields the optimal convergence rate. Numerical experiments validate the theoretical results and demonstrate the robustness of the proposed scheme.

</details>


### [10] [The Levin Method for the Summation of One-dimensional and Multidimensional Infinite Series](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06152)
*David Levin*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper extends the Levin method from oscillatory integral evaluation to the summation of one-dimensional and multidimensional infinite oscillatory series by transforming the summation problem into solving a functional equation for an auxiliary function.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing numerical methods for summing infinite oscillatory series are limited, especially in multidimensional settings. The Levin method has been successful for highly oscillatory integrals, and extending it to series summation could provide a powerful new approach where few methods currently exist.

Method: The summation problem is reformulated as the solution of a functional equation involving transformed arguments of an unknown slowly varying auxiliary function, analogous to how the Levin method transforms integral evaluation into solving a first-order linear ODE via collocation.

Result: The approach is shown to be particularly attractive in the multidimensional setting, where the existing range of numerical methods is relatively limited.

Conclusion: A new extension of the Levin method is developed for summing one-dimensional and multidimensional infinite oscillatory series, offering advantages especially in multidimensional cases.

Abstract: The Levin method transforms the evaluation of a highly oscillatory integral into the solution of a first-order linear ODE for a slowly varying auxiliary function. This ODE is typically approximated by collocation, after which the integral value is recovered from the auxiliary function at the endpoints.
  The present work develops a new extension of the Levin method for the summation of one-dimensional and multidimensional infinite oscillatory series. The summation problem is transformed into the solution of a functional equation involving transformed arguments of the unknown function. The resulting approach is particularly attractive in the multidimensional setting, where the range of existing numerical methods is relatively limited.

</details>


### [11] [Fourier-cosine Tensor Trains for Density Recovery and Expectation Calculation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06214)
*Auke Schaap,Fang Fang*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper introduces two tensor-train based methods (COS-TT and COS-TT-CHF) to overcome the curse of dimensionality in the Fourier-cosine (COS) method for option pricing and density recovery. COS-TT-CHF compresses characteristic function sample tensors via tensor trains, achieving linear computational costs in dimension with only algebraically growing error, demonstrated accurate through 150 dimensions for GBM and 100 for VG models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The COS method suffers from exponential scaling in both expansion synthesis and coefficient-tensor computation as dimension increases, making it impractical for high-dimensional problems in financial applications.

Method: Two tensor-train approaches: (1) COS-TT replaces the COS coefficient tensor with a TT representation to remove exponential online synthesis cost; (2) COS-TT-CHF compresses a characteristic function sample tensor and maps it to the COS coefficient tensor via an alternative COS representation, reducing both offline and online costs to linear scaling in dimension. The method also handles nonseparable expectations of additive aggregates and closed-form TT-contraction integrals for basket options.

Result: COS-TT-CHF demonstrates accuracy through 150 dimensions for geometric Brownian motion and 100 dimensions for variance gamma models, with explicit error bounds and parameter-selection rules confirmed experimentally.

Conclusion: COS-TT-CHF reopens Fourier-based density recovery and expectation computation methods to high-dimensional problems previously considered intractable, offering a scalable and accurate approach for basket-option pricing.

Abstract: The Fourier--cosine (COS) method of Fang and Oosterlee (2008) recovers densities and computes expectations semi-analytically from the characteristic function (ch.f.). In higher dimensions, both expansion synthesis and computation of COS coefficient-tensor entries scale exponentially with dimension. We address this curse of dimensionality with two tensor-train (TT) methods. COS-TT replaces the COS coefficient tensor by a TT representation, removing the exponential cost of online synthesis but not of offline construction. COS-TT-CHF, the main contribution of this paper, instead compresses a ch.f. sample tensor and maps it to the original Fourier--cosine coefficient tensor through an alternative COS representation. Apart from the model-dependent cost of one ch.f. evaluation, its offline and online costs scale linearly with dimension, while its error grows only algebraically with dimension. COS-TT-CHF introduces frequency-domain truncation and discretization errors absent from COS-TT; we derive explicit bounds and practical parameter-selection rules to control both. As a by-product, the construction yields cosine-basis functional TTs directly from the ch.f., converging to the density under square integrability. We further reduce expectations of nonseparable functions of additive scalar aggregates, including weighted sums of dependent variables, to one-dimensional COS calculations without tensorizing the target function. For basket-option pricing, closed-form TT-contraction integrals yield a semi-analytical formula. Experiments under geometric Brownian motion (GBM) and variance gamma (VG) confirm the parameter-selection rules and demonstrate that COS-TT-CHF remains accurate through 150 dimensions for GBM and 100 for VG. COS-TT-CHF thus reopens Fourier-based methods to problems previously considered out of reach in high dimensions.

</details>


### [12] [Weighted Laplace Spaces for Spectral Measures and Rational Approximation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06243)
*Stefan Jakobsson,Alice Kozakevicius,Stig Larsson*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper introduces a weighted Laplace space H_w, an RKHS of Laplace transforms on (0,∞), and studies spectral measures in its dual space H'_w. It proves a dual-norm inequality for conforming FEM discretizations of the Dirichlet Laplacian on bounded Lipschitz domains, combines min-max monotonicity with heat-trace representations, and develops H_w-adapted rational approximation with a transfer principle for shifted symbols.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To establish rigorous norm inequalities between exact and discrete (FEM) spectral measures in a properly weighted function space, enabling uniform bounds on spectral sums and transformed observables.

Method: Combines min-max monotonicity of FEM eigenvalues with a heat-trace representation of the dual norm, introduces an RKHS of Laplace transforms (H_w), and develops rational approximation theory with a conditional transfer principle in the weighted Laplace pre-image norm.

Result: Proves the dual-norm inequality ||μ_h||_{H'_w} ≤ ||μ||_{H'_w} for FEM discretizations, yields uniform bounds for finite spectral sums and related transformed observables via dual pairing.

Conclusion: The weighted Laplace space framework provides a novel and effective tool for analyzing spectral approximation errors in FEM discretizations of the Dirichlet Laplacian.

Abstract: We introduce the weighted Laplace space $H_w$, an RKHS of Laplace transforms on $(0,\infty)$, and study spectral measures in its dual space $H'_w$. For conforming FEM discretizations of the Dirichlet Laplacian on bounded Lipschitz domains, we prove the dual-norm inequality $\|μ_h\|_{H'_w} \leq \|μ\|_{H'_w}$, where $μ= \sum_kδ_{λ_k}$ and $μ_h = \sum_k δ_{λ_{k,h}}$. The proof combines min-max monotonicity of FEM eigenvalues with a heat-trace representation of the dual norm. We then analyze $H_w$-adapted rational approximation of shifted symbols $φ(x)=(x+κ^2)^{-β}$ and give a conditional transfer principle for estimates proved in the corresponding weighted Laplace pre-image norm. Via dual pairing, the norm inequality yields uniform bounds for finite spectral sums and related transformed observables.

</details>


### [13] [Convergence rates of randomly pivoted methods for low-rank approximation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06287)
*Ryan Divan,Marc Aurèle Gilles*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper provides convergence rates for randomly pivoted Cholesky, QR, and LU algorithms that depend on the decay of the original matrix's singular values. The rates hold approximately when pivots are sampled from an approximate distribution, allowing cheaper approximations to be substituted for exact sampling. This flexibility is used to derive rates for randomly pivoted LU variants, including a sketching-based approach.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Randomly pivoted Cholesky, QR, and LU are important iterative algorithms for structured low-rank matrix approximation, but their theoretical convergence guarantees were not well understood when approximate pivot distributions are used. Understanding how convergence depends on singular value decay and how exact sampling can be relaxed to cheaper approximations is needed.

Method: The authors establish convergence rates for randomly pivoted factorization algorithms that depend on singular value decay of the original matrix. They show the rates hold (up to a constant) when pivots are drawn from approximate distributions rather than the exact one. This enables trading exact sampling for computationally cheaper approximations, and they apply this to analyze variants of randomly pivoted LU, including one that selects pivots via a sketch of the input matrix.

Result: Convergence rates are established for randomly pivoted Cholesky, QR, and LU that depend on the singular value decay of the matrix. The rates are shown to hold up to a constant factor under approximate pivot sampling, and specific rates are derived for randomly pivoted LU variants, including a sketching-based pivot selection method.

Conclusion: The work demonstrates that approximate pivot distributions are sufficient for maintaining near-optimal convergence rates in randomly pivoted factorization algorithms, providing theoretical justification for using cheaper pivot-selection strategies such as sketching in practice.

Abstract: Randomly pivoted Cholesky, QR, and LU are iterative algorithms that form structured low-rank approximations of a matrix by sampling columns, or rows and columns, from the residual. We give convergence rates for these methods that depend on the decay of the singular values of the original matrix. The rates hold, up to a constant, when pivots are drawn from an approximation to the exact distribution. This extra degree of freedom can be used to trade exact sampling for cheaper approximations. We use it to establish rates for variants of randomly pivoted LU, including one that picks pivots using a sketch of the input matrix.

</details>


### [14] [A review of weakly enforced Dirichlet boundary conditions in computational flow analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06314)
*Ming-Chen Hsu,Monu Jaiswal,Yuri Bazilevs*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This review paper examines weakly enforced Dirichlet boundary conditions as an alternative to strongly enforced conditions, which require costly refined near-wall meshes. Weak enforcement acts as a variationally consistent near-wall model, enabling accurate flow simulation on coarse meshes and serving as the enabling technology for immersogeometric analysis. The paper traces the unified mathematical development of this framework from scalar advection-diffusion to the full Navier-Stokes equations, covering incompressible and compressible flows across boundary-fitted, sliding-interface, and immersogeometric applications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Strongly enforced Dirichlet boundary conditions demand highly refined near-wall meshes to resolve steep velocity and thermal gradients, leading to prohibitive computational costs in practical flow simulations. Weakly enforced boundary conditions offer a way to alleviate this computational burden while maintaining accuracy, and they enable direct geometry-to-analysis workflows on complex geometries without mesh fitting.

Method: The paper presents a unified mathematical review of weakly enforced Dirichlet boundary conditions, tracing their development from scalar advection-diffusion equations to the full Navier-Stokes equations. It demonstrates how the weak form evaluates boundary integrals independently of the background mesh, allowing controlled slip at solid walls and enabling immersogeometric analysis on complex geometries (CAD models, point clouds, photogrammetric reconstructions, and segmented medical images).

Result: Weak enforcement recovers accurate flow quantities on coarse boundary-layer meshes across both incompressible and compressible regimes by allowing controlled slip at the solid wall. The approach proves versatile and robust across traditional boundary-fitted, sliding-interface, and advanced immersogeometric applications.

Conclusion: Weakly enforced Dirichlet boundary conditions represent a unified framework that is both variationally consistent as a near-wall model and technically enabling for immersogeometric analysis, providing a versatile solution for high-fidelity flow simulation across diverse flow regimes and geometry representations.

Abstract: Strongly enforced Dirichlet boundary conditions require highly refined near-wall meshes to resolve steep velocity and thermal gradients. This introduces high computational costs, especially for practical flow simulations. Weakly enforced boundary conditions alleviate this burden by acting as a variationally consistent near-wall model. By allowing a controlled slip at the solid wall, weak enforcement recovers accurate flow quantities on coarse boundary-layer meshes across both incompressible and compressible regimes. Furthermore, weak boundary conditions serve as the fundamental enabling technology for immersogeometric analysis. Because the weak operator evaluates boundary integrals independently of the background mesh, high-fidelity flow analysis can be performed directly on complex geometries without fitting a mesh to the surface. This capability has facilitated direct geometry-to-analysis workflows for boundary-representation CAD models, raw point clouds, photogrammetric reconstructions, and segmented medical images. This review examines the unified mathematical development of the weak boundary condition framework from scalar advection-diffusion equations to the full Navier-Stokes equations, illustrating its versatility and robustness across incompressible and compressible flows, whether in traditional boundary-fitted, sliding-interface, or advanced immersogeometric applications.

</details>


### [15] [Rough variational principles and applications to adjoint systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06337)
*Ruiao Hu,Melvin Leok*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper develops a Type-II variational principle for geometric rough paths with split boundary conditions, deriving rough Hamilton's equations, conservation laws, and Hamilton-Jacobi equations. It constructs a rough Galerkin discretization that yields symplectic flows equivalent to Rough Symplectic Partitioned Runge-Kutta (RSPRK) methods, validated numerically for adjoint sensitivity analysis and gradient computation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To extend Type-II variational principles and symplectic structure-preserving discretization to the setting of rough paths, enabling pathwise conservation laws and stable adjoint sensitivity analysis in non-smooth (rough) dynamical systems.

Method: The authors formulate a Type-II variational principle for geometric rough paths with split boundary conditions suited to adjoint systems. They derive rough Hamilton's equations and conservation laws, specialize to rough adjoint systems, and construct a rough Galerkin discretization that produces symplectic flows with discrete conservation law analogues, shown equivalent to RSPRK methods.

Result: The rough Galerkin discretization is proven equivalent to RSPRK methods, with convergence and naturality properties established. Numerical experiments confirm predicted convergence rates and show RSPRK methods preserve adjoint conservation laws to machine precision, yielding more accurate and stable gradients than non-symplectic methods.

Conclusion: The framework provides a principled, structure-preserving approach to rough variational problems and adjoint sensitivity analysis, offering discrete symplectic integrators that outperform non-symplectic alternatives in optimization accuracy and stability.

Abstract: We consider a Type-II variational principle driven by geometric rough path with split boundary conditions naturally suited to adjoint systems. From this rough variational principle we derive rough Hamilton's equations, establish their pathwise conservation laws and associated Hamilton--Jacobi equation. We then specialise the framework to rough adjoint systems, obtaining pathwise conservation and quasi-conservation laws that underpin adjoint sensitivity analysis with respect to initial conditions and parameters. On the discrete side, we construct a rough Galerkin discretisation of the rough Type-II variational principle and show that it generates a symplectic flow with discrete analogues of the continuous conservation laws. We establish it's equivalence to a class of Rough Symplectic Partitioned Runge--Kutta (RSPRK) methods and analyse its convergence and naturality properties. Lastly, we perform numerical experiments to validate the predicted convergence rates and demonstrate that RSPRK methods preserve the adjoint conservation laws to machine precision, yielding more accurate and stable gradients in optimisation problems than non-symplectic alternatives.

</details>


### [16] [A counterexample to vertex interpolation by planar $C^1$ cubic splines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06364)
*Junkai Qiu*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper constructs a specific triangulation with 41 vertices and 56 triangles that disproves Alfeld's conjecture on vertex interpolation for cubic C¹ splines. The authors derive an explicit linear relation with integer coefficients among vertex values using Bernstein-Bézier smoothness equations, without requiring matrix rank computations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Alfeld's conjecture on vertex interpolation for cubic C¹ splines over triangulations is a long-standing open problem. The authors aim to disprove this conjecture by constructing a concrete counterexample triangulation where vertex data do not always admit a continuously differentiable piecewise polynomial interpolant of degree at most three.

Method: The authors construct a nondegenerate conforming straight-line triangulation with a tree-structured interior graph (eight arms of length two). They derive an explicit linear relation with integer coefficients among vertex values by forming a weighted sum of Bernstein-Bézier smoothness equations. Rational coordinates and weights are used to verify the relation, avoiding matrix rank computations. Separately, they compute in exact arithmetic the rank (40) of the vertex evaluation map and the dimension (107) of the spline space.

Result: The constructed triangulation admits a linear relation with integer coefficients among cubic C¹ spline vertex values, meaning some vertex data cannot be interpolated. This disproves Alfeld's conjecture. The vertex evaluation map has rank 40 and the spline space has dimension 107, attaining the classical dimension lower bound.

Conclusion: Alfeld's conjecture on vertex interpolation is false. The counterexample triangulation demonstrates this via an explicit linear relation derived from Bernstein-Bézier smoothness conditions, provable without rank computations, while still attaining the classical dimension lower bound for the spline space.

Abstract: We construct a nondegenerate conforming straight-line triangulation of a polygonal disk on which some vertex data admit no continuously differentiable piecewise polynomial interpolant of total degree at most three. The triangulation has 41 vertices and 56 triangles, and the graph induced by its interior vertices is a tree with eight arms of length two. Every cubic $C^1$ spline on this triangulation satisfies an explicit linear relation with integer coefficients among its vertex values. We derive the relation by a weighted sum of Bernstein--Bézier smoothness equations and verify it using rational coordinates and weights. This disproves Alfeld's conjecture on vertex interpolation. The nonexistence proof does not require a matrix rank computation. A separate computation in exact arithmetic gives rank 40 for the vertex evaluation map and dimension 107 for the spline space, attaining the classical dimension lower bound for this triangulation.

</details>


### [17] [Sharp approximation rates for shallow ReLU neuralnetworks on critical Besov classes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06418)
*Yupeng Wang*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper determines the sharp algebraic rate of finite n-term approximation from the normalized ridge dictionary generated by ReLU^k on a bounded Lipschitz domain, under a fixed outer ℓ¹ coefficient budget. For the unit ball of the critical Besov space B_{p,q}^{k+d/p}, the optimal algebraic exponent is min{(k-m+d/2)/(d-1), (k-m+d/2+1/p-1/2)/d}, with two-sided estimates in three regimes and associated logarithmic factors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To establish the fundamental approximation-theoretic limits of dictionary approximation (ReLU^k ridge functions) for functions in critical Besov spaces, with a constrained coefficient budget independent of n, yielding sharp algebraic rates.

Method: Upper bounds combine critical wavelet sparsity, localized Fourier–Radon representations, and stable allocation of directions and biases. Lower bounds derive from two distinct obstructions: radial ridge approximation on the direction sphere and Gevrey localization in the joint direction–bias space.

Result: Two-sided estimates matching the sharp exponent min{(k-m+d/2)/(d-1), (k-m+d/2+1/p-1/2)/d} in three regimes, with logarithmic factors identified at and beyond the transition. The representation exponent exceeds the degree-limited exponent for fixed-degree isotropic finite elements under parameter counting.

Conclusion: The critical smoothness k+d/p is exactly the endpoint where Besov regularity yields a uniform shallow-network variation bound without additional scale decay. Ridge-dictionary representations outperform fixed-degree isotropic finite elements, though this concerns best approximation rather than training or computational complexity.

Abstract: Let $\mathbb D$ be the normalized ridge dictionary generated by $\operatorname{ReLU}^k$ on a bounded Lipschitz domain $Ω\subset\mathbb R^d$. We determine the sharp algebraic rate of finite $n$-term approximation from $\mathbb D$ when the outer $\ell^1$ coefficient budget is independent of $n$. More precisely, let $d\ge3$, $k\in\mathbb N_+$, $0\le m\le k$, $0<p<1$, and $0<q\le1$. For the unit ball of the critical Besov space $B_{p,q}^{k+d/p}(Ω)$, measured in $H^m(Ω)$, the optimal algebraic exponent is \[
  \min\left\{\frac{k-m+d/2}{d-1},
  \frac{k-m+d/2+1/p-1/2}{d}\right\}. \] We prove two-sided estimates in the three regimes determined by the two branches of this minimum and give the corresponding logarithmic factors at and beyond the transition. The estimates coincide without logarithmic loss in the strict angular regime and at the transition when $q\le[1/2+(k-m+d/2)/(d-1)]^{-1}$. The upper estimate combines critical wavelet sparsity, localized Fourier--Radon representations, and a stable allocation of directions and biases. The lower estimates arise from two distinct obstructions, namely radial ridge approximation on the direction sphere and Gevrey localization in the joint direction--bias space. The critical smoothness is exactly the endpoint at which Besov regularity provides a uniform shallow-network variation bound without additional scale decay. The resulting representation exponent is strictly larger than the degree-limited exponent for fixed-degree isotropic finite elements under a parameter-count comparison. This last statement concerns best approximation, not training or computational complexity.

</details>


### [18] [High-Order Structure-Preserving SBP Finite Difference Methods for the Vlasov-Maxwell System on Matrix-Free GPUs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06452)
*Robin Dymér,Ken Mattsson,Murtazo Nazarov*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper presents a high-order, stable summation-by-parts (SBP) finite difference method for solving the Vlasov-Maxwell system in 2D2V phase space, stabilized with high-order upwind SBP operators and explicit Runge-Kutta time integration, with a GPU-optimized matrix-free implementation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Central SBP operators for advection terms are unstable when the solution becomes non-smooth and develops fine-scale filamentary structures, which is typical in high-dimensional Vlasov-Maxwell simulations. A stable high-order method is needed.

Method: High-order upwind SBP operators are used to stabilize the advection terms instead of central SBP operators. High-order explicit Runge-Kutta methods handle time integration. A matrix-free implementation is developed for modern GPU architectures.

Result: The fully discrete scheme exactly conserves mass and preserves momentum up to truncation error. Accuracy, robustness, and performance are demonstrated on a range of challenging benchmark problems.

Conclusion: The proposed upwind SBP finite difference method provides a stable, accurate, and efficient approach for solving the Vlasov-Maxwell system in 2D2V phase space, suitable for GPU-based high-performance computing.

Abstract: In this paper, we present a high-order, stable summation-by-parts (SBP) finite difference method for solving the Vlasov-Maxwell system in a 2D2V phase space. Central SBP operators for the advection terms are not stable when the solution becomes non-smooth and fine-scale filamentary structures develop, as is typical in high-dimensional Vlasov-Maxwell simulations. To address this issue, the method is stabilized using high-order upwind SBP operators. High-order explicit Runge-Kutta methods are employed for time integration. We prove that the fully discrete scheme exactly conserves mass and preserves momentum up to truncation error. Furthermore, we present a matrix-free implementation of the method on modern GPU architectures. A range of challenging benchmark problems is solved to demonstrate the accuracy, robustness, and performance of the proposed scheme.

</details>


### [19] [Rate of convergence of a fully discrete structure-preserving midpoint scheme for the stochastic Landau--Lifshitz--Gilbert equation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06459)
*Agus L. Soenjaya*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper presents the first convergence-rate analysis for a fully discrete, structure-preserving finite element scheme solving the stochastic Landau-Lifshitz-Gilbert (sLLG) equation with colored multiplicative Stratonovich noise on a bounded interval. The scheme preserves the unit-length constraint exactly at nodes, achieving first-order spatial and γ-order temporal convergence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The sLLG equation is a strongly nonlinear stochastic PDE with a non-convex constraint arising in micromagnetics, yet no convergence-rate results existed for fully discrete structure-preserving finite element schemes solving it.

Method: The method uses continuous piecewise affine finite elements with mass lumping and midpoint time discretization to preserve the unit-length constraint exactly at nodes. Analysis exploits geometric structure and stochastic midpoint discretization, establishing uniform higher-moment stability and error estimates under regularity assumptions.

Result: For any γ∈(0,1/2), first-order spatial convergence and temporal convergence of order γ are proven in the natural discrete energy norm, locally in mean square on events of arbitrarily large probability and consequently in probability.

Conclusion: This is the first convergence-rate result for a fully discrete structure-preserving finite element scheme solving the stochastic Landau-Lifshitz-Gilbert equation.

Abstract: The stochastic Landau--Lifshitz--Gilbert (sLLG) equation is a strongly nonlinear stochastic PDE with a non-convex pointwise constraint arising in the theory of micromagnetics. We analyse a fully discrete, structure-preserving finite element approximation of the sLLG equation with coloured multiplicative Stratonovich noise on a bounded interval. The method utilises continuous piecewise affine finite elements, mass lumping, and midpoint time discretisation to preserve the unit-length constraint exactly at the finite element nodes. Under suitable regularity assumptions on the initial data and the noise, we establish uniform higher-moment stability and develop an error analysis for the scheme. The analysis exploits the geometric structure of the equation and the stochastic midpoint discretisation. For every $γ\in(0,\frac12)$, we prove first-order spatial convergence and temporal convergence of order $γ$ in the natural discrete energy norm, locally in mean square on events of arbitrarily large probability and, consequently, in probability. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first convergence-rate result for a fully discrete structure-preserving finite element scheme solving the stochastic Landau--Lifshitz--Gilbert equation.

</details>


### [20] [Improved Inclusion-Exclusion Eigenvalue Localization Sets for Matrices and Matrix Polynomials](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06538)
*Ljiljana Cvetkovic,Christina Michailidou,Irena Prodanovic*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper introduces a new family of inclusion-exclusion eigenvalue localization sets built on the S-strict diagonal dominance (S-SDD) framework and CKV localization regions. For any nonempty subset S of indices, novel exclusion regions are constructed, and the resulting localization sets are shown to contain all eigenvalues of a complex matrix. A refined set is obtained by intersecting over all subsets S. The framework generalizes and unifies existing results (notably Gershgorin and Dashnic-Zusmanovich inclusion-exclusion sets as special cases) and is extended to matrix polynomials.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Eigenvalue localization regions can be significantly sharpened by excluding subsets guaranteed to contain no eigenvalues (inclusion-exclusion sets). Existing results like Gershgorin and Dashnic-Zusmanovich inclusion-exclusion sets are limited in generality, and there is a need for a unified framework that yields sharper localization regions.

Method: The authors construct novel exclusion regions based on the S-strict diagonal dominance framework and CKV localization regions for arbitrary nonempty index subsets S. They derive S-SDD inclusion-exclusion sets, prove all eigenvalues lie in these regions, define a refined set by intersecting over all subsets S, and extend the theory to matrix polynomials.

Result: The proposed framework unifies and extends existing results, recovering Gershgorin and Dashnic-Zusmanovich inclusion-exclusion sets as special cases. The new localization set is shown to be generally sharper than both. Numerical examples confirm the improvement over existing localization sets, including for polynomial eigenvalue problems.

Conclusion: The S-SDD-based inclusion-exclusion localization framework provides a general, unifying construction that yields sharper eigenvalue inclusion regions than previously known approaches, with successful extensions to matrix polynomials.

Abstract: Recent developments have shown that eigenvalue localization regions can often be significantly sharpened by identifying and excluding subsets that are guaranteed to contain no eigenvalues, leading to the concept of inclusion-exclusion localization sets. In this paper, we introduce a new family of inclusion-exclusion eigenvalue localization sets based on the S-strict diagonal dominance framework and the corresponding CKV localization regions. For an arbitrary nonempty subset S of the index set, we construct novel exclusion regions and derive the associated S-SDD inclusion-exclusion localization sets. We prove that every eigenvalue of a complex matrix belongs to the proposed regions and define a refined localization set obtained by intersecting the corresponding inclusion-exclusion regions over all nonempty subsets S. The proposed framework unifies and extends several existing results. In particular, we show that the classical Gershgorin inclusion-exclusion localization set and the Dashnic-Zusmanovich inclusion-exclusion localization set arise naturally as special cases of our construction. Consequently, the new localization set provides, in general, a sharper eigenvalue inclusion region than both of these previously known localization regions. The theory is further extended to matrix polynomials, yielding corresponding inclusion-exclusion localization results for polynomial eigenvalue problems. Several numerical examples illustrate the effectiveness of the proposed approach and demonstrate the improvement achieved over existing localization sets.

</details>


### [21] [Introductory Notes on Learning$^2$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06546)
*Sai Siddharth,Maniarasu Ravi*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper introduces Learning², a representation-level framework that couples a primary representation to a second representation through a known physical transformation, restricting the hypothesis space for learning physical system evolution. It is instantiated through EuLaNet, an Eulerian-Lagrangian framework for fluid dynamics that derives induced flow maps and material transport quantities from the velocity state, providing an ante-hoc physically interpretable consistency criterion beyond state-level agreement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard machine learning methods that predict physical system evolution from data only learn the system state at each time step, leaving temporal and dynamical structure to be resolved within an overly broad hypothesis space. This lack of structure can allow solutions that satisfy the primary state representation but are physically inconsistent or non-physical in their dynamical consequences.

Method: Learning² couples a primary representation to a secondary representation via a known physical transformation, creating a cross-representation constraint that narrows the effective hypothesis space (H_L² ⊆ H). The framework is instantiated as EuLaNet, which takes the Eulerian velocity field u(x,t) and computes the induced Lagrangian flow map X(a,t) via Ẋ(a,t)=u(X(a,t),t), from which material transport and finite-time deformation are derived. EuLaNet is designed as a model-independent representation module, separating physical constraints from downstream learning architectures.

Result: The paper provides a formalization of the effective hypothesis space H_L² ⊆ H and defines conditions under which a consequence representation provides discriminative constraints on candidate solutions. EuLaNet demonstrates the framework in fluid dynamics, delivering a second consistency criterion (dynamical consequences) beyond state-level agreement, with the implementation open-sourced.

Conclusion: Learning² offers an ante-hoc (before-the-fact) mechanism for physically interpretable constraint in scientific learning, rather than relying on post-hoc verification. The framework provides a basis for developing and evaluating broader classes of Learning² architectures across various scientific domains.

Abstract: Although machine learning can be used to predict the evolution of physical systems from data, a formulation that learns only the system state at each time leaves the temporal and dynamical structure of the solution to be resolved within a broad hypothesis space. We introduce Learning$^2$, a representation-level framework that structures this space by coupling a primary representation to a second representation through a known physical transformation. The resulting cross-representation constraint restricts the effective hypothesis space and provides an ante-hoc, physically interpretable criterion for excluding solutions that satisfy the primary representation alone. We instantiate Learning$^2$ through EuLaNet, an Eulerian--Lagrangian representation for fluid dynamics. Given the velocity state $u(\mathbf{x},t)$, EuLaNet constructs its induced Lagrangian flow map $X(\mathbf{a},t)$ through $\dot{X}(\mathbf{a},t)=u(X(\mathbf{a},t),t)$, from which material transport and finite-time deformation are derived. The resulting representation couples the predicted state to the dynamical consequences it induces, providing a second consistency criterion beyond state-level agreement.
  We formalize this construction through an effective hypothesis space $\mathcal{H}_{L^2}\subseteq\mathcal{H}$ and define the conditions under which a consequence representation provides discriminative constraints on candidate solutions. EuLaNet is implemented as a model-independent representation module, separating the physical constraint from the downstream learning architecture. This construction provides an ante-hoc mechanism for physically interpretable constraint in scientific learning and offers a basis for developing and evaluating broader classes of Learning$^2$ architectures. The implementation is open-sourced to support the development and extension of the architecture across scientific domains.

</details>


### [22] [Halley's Method for Rectangular Matrix Variables and the Matrix Schwarzian Derivative](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06592)
*Shintaro Yoshizawa*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper generalizes Halley's cubic convergence method to matrix gradient fields via a matrix Schwarzian derivative interpreted through information geometry (α-connections), proving local cubic convergence without restrictive assumptions and validating with a matrix-free algorithm.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To extend the classical connection between Halley's method and the Schwarzian derivative (originally for scalar functions) to the matrix setting, where gradient fields F=∇φ on R^{m×n} require a more sophisticated mathematical framework.

Method: Constructs a matrix Schwarzian derivative operator using matrix gradient fields and information geometry (α-connections), simplifying prior work by Palmore (1994). The operator is derived square-root-free from the third Fréchet derivative of the Newton map. A main theorem proves local cubic convergence with an explicit error constant, without requiring self-adjointness, commutativity, or gradient-field assumptions. A matrix-free algorithm using only Hessian-vector products is developed for validation.

Result: Local cubic convergence is proven with an explicit error constant. The method is contrasted with a power-Newton family (which fails in naive matrix extension) and matrix Laguerre family (which requires an operator square root). A case study on the Oja-type system shows convergence depends on target eigenvalue gaps, cubic gains grow with ill-conditioning, and 70-digit tests confirm theoretical orders with a working-precision accuracy budget.

Conclusion: Coupled formulations excel primarily when sequential deflation is inapplicable, indicating the practical scope of the proposed matrix Halley-type method within matrix computation contexts.

Abstract: Alefeld (1981) recast Halley's cubic convergence as Newton's method on $g=f/\sqrt{f'}$, linked to the Schwarzian derivative. We generalize this to matrix gradient fields $F=\nablaφ$ ($X\in\R^{m\times n}$) via a matrix Schwarzian derivative interpreted through information geometry ($α$-connections). Simplifying Palmore (1994), we construct this operator square-root-free derivative from the third Fréchet derivative of the Newton map. Our main theorem proves local cubic convergence with an explicit error constant, without self-adjointness, commutativity, or gradient-field assumptions. A matrix-free algorithm (Hessian-vector products only) validates the theory. We contrast this with a power-Newton family (whose naive matrix extension fails) and the matrix Laguerre family (which requires an operator square root). A case study on the Oja-type system $\dot X=AXB-XBX^TAX$ reveals that convergence depends on target eigenvalue gaps, cubic gains grow with ill-conditioning, and 70-digit tests confirm exact theoretical orders alongside a working-precision accuracy budget. Finally, we note coupled formulations excel primarily when sequential deflation is inapplicable.

</details>


### [23] [Dilation-Covariant Hankel Pencils for Multiscale Recovery of Sparse Mellin Spectra](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06661)
*Zhiliang Deng,Xiaomei Yang*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper develops a sparse Mellin spectral recovery method using geometric sampling at multiple scales. Dilation covariance directly induces Hankel structure, incommensurate sampling scales eliminate logarithmic aliasing for complex exponents, and a minimal Hankel pencil enables regionally certified spectral recovery via contour winding with explicit error bounds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sparse Mellin spectral recovery suffers from logarithmic aliasing at a single sampling scale, and existing approaches rely on indirect reduction to exponential-sum models. There is a need for a direct recovery framework with improved identifiability and certified resolution guarantees.

Method: The method exploits dilation covariance to construct Hankel matrices directly from geometric samples. Incommensurate sampling scales are used at multiple dilation rates to eliminate aliasing for complex exponents. Recovery proceeds via a minimal Hankel pencil whose generalized eigenvalues carry spectral information; contour winding over the pencil counts spectral nodes regionally, with Rouché-type bounds providing certification for isolated nodes and unresolved clusters under noise.

Result: Numerical experiments demonstrate that adding an auxiliary sampling scale improves both identifiability and resolution. Certified contour counts remain reliable even beyond the regime where accurate pointwise recovery is possible. The contour margin shows an explicit degeneration rate as the sampling ratio approaches one.

Conclusion: Multi-scale geometric sampling with minimal Hankel pencils provides a robust and certifiable framework for sparse Mellin spectral recovery, overcoming single-scale aliasing and extending reliable recovery guarantees beyond pointwise accuracy limits.

Abstract: We study sparse Mellin spectral recovery from geometric samples. Dilation covariance is shown to generate the Hankel structure directly, rather than only after reduction to a classical exponential-sum model. For complex exponents, incommensurate sampling scales remove the logarithmic aliasing that persists at a single scale. A second recovery channel is obtained from the minimal Hankel pencil: contour winding counts spectral nodes regionally, while Rouché-type bounds certify isolated nodes and unresolved clusters under noise. The associated contour margin has an explicit degeneration rate as the sampling ratio approaches one. Numerical experiments show that the auxiliary scale improves both identifiability and resolution, and that certified contour counts can remain reliable beyond the regime of accurate pointwise recovery.

</details>


### [24] [A discontinuous Galerkin approximation of the Dean--Kawasaki equation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06681)
*Kamran Arora,Federico Cornalba,Tony Shardlow*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper introduces an arbitrary-order discontinuous Galerkin method for the Dean–Kawasaki equation (a singular SPDE for density fluctuations of many diffusing particles), with a general noise-discretization procedure preserving cross-variation structure at the discrete level. It proves weak error estimates of order O(h^p) against the underlying particle system, plus exponentially small corrections in the regime Nh^d ≫ 1, and supports the analysis with numerical experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The Dean–Kawasaki equation is a highly singular SPDE arising from density fluctuations of large numbers of diffusing particles, and accurate numerical discretization of such multiplicative divergence-form noise while preserving the discrete cross-variation structure is a fundamental open challenge.

Method: The authors develop an explicit, elementwise construction for discretising multiplicative divergence-form noise on finite element spaces that preserves cross-variation structure at the discrete level (applicable to both continuous and discontinuous spaces, and extendable to general mobilities), then embed it within an arbitrary-order discontinuous Galerkin spatial discretization.

Result: Weak error estimates of order O(h^p) are proven between the semi-discrete scheme's fluctuations and the underlying particle system, with an exponentially small correction in the regime Nh^d ≫ 1 arising from lack of positivity preservation. The method is locally and globally conservative on unstructured simplicial meshes, and quantitative numerical experiments confirm the theory.

Conclusion: The proposed dG method provides a rigorously justified numerical framework for the Dean–Kawasaki equation, with experiments additionally demonstrating robustness beyond the theoretical scope (indicator observables, potentials, convection-dominated regimes, reflective boundaries).

Abstract: We introduce and analyse an arbitrary order spatial discontinuous Galerkin (dG) method for the Dean--Kawasaki equation, a highly singular SPDE modelling density fluctuations of $N$ diffusing particles in the regime of large particle number $N \gg 1$. Our starting point is a general procedure for discretising multiplicative, divergence-form noise on finite element spaces whilst preserving its cross-variation structure at the discrete level; the construction is explicit, elementwise, applies to continuous and discontinuous spaces alike, and extends to general mobilities. Using it, we prove weak error estimates of order $O(h^p)$ between fluctuations of the semi-discrete scheme and those of the underlying particle system, together with a correction that is exponentially small in the scaling regime $Nh^d \gg 1$ and arises because the scheme does not preserve positivity. The resulting method is locally and globally conservative and applies on unstructured simplicial meshes. Quantitative numerical experiments support the analysis, and further experiments illustrate the method beyond the scope of the theory: indicator function observables, external and interaction potentials, convection-dominated regimes and reflecting boundary conditions.

</details>


### [25] [Invariant-region-preserving high-order schemes for a model of reactive sedimentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06846)
*Juan Barajas-Calonge,Julio Careaga,Luis-Miguel Villada*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper develops a high-order finite volume scheme (using MUSCL and CWENO3 reconstructions with SSPRK time integration) to solve a system of convection-diffusion-reaction equations modeling reactive sedimentation of biological and inert solids with liquid substrates, applied to secondary settling tanks in wastewater treatment. The scheme preserves invariant-region properties in zero-diffusion cases and is validated through denitrification simulations and convergence tests.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reactive sedimentation processes in secondary settling tanks (SSTs) of water resource recovery facilities (WRRFs) require accurate numerical simulation for control and optimization. The governing equations are complex nonlinear strongly degenerate parabolic convection-diffusion-reaction systems, and existing low-order schemes may lack the accuracy needed for reliable predictions while also risking non-physical numerical solutions.

Method: The model consists of a nonlinear strongly degenerate parabolic PDE for total solids concentration coupled with transport-reaction equations for solid components and substrates. A high-order finite volume scheme is developed using two polynomial reconstruction approaches: a second-order MUSCL scheme and a third-order CWENO3 scheme with limiters. Time discretization employs SSPRK schemes of second and third order. High-order approximations are also proposed for diffusion terms. In the zero-diffusion case, invariant-region preservation is proven.

Result: Simulations of denitrification in secondary settling tanks under continuously operated conditions in WRRFs demonstrate the model and discretization performance. Convergence tests confirm the designed order of accuracy and the invariant-region preservation property of the schemes.
