Conclusion: The proposed high-order finite volume schemes provide accurate and physically consistent numerical solutions for reactive sedimentation models, with the invariant-region property ensuring physically relevant results at each time step, making them suitable for simulation and control of SSTs in wastewater treatment facilities.

Abstract: Reactive sedimentation of mixtures composed by biological and inert solid matter including liquid substrates is modelled by a system of convection-diffusion-reaction equations. In this process, the small solid particles dispersed in the viscous fluid settle due to gravity while simultaneous chemical reactions take place between solid and liquid components (substrates). Among the applications of reactive sedimentation processes, the principal use is in the simulation and control of secondary settling tanks (SSTs) in water resource recovery facilities (WRRFs). The governing equations include a nonlinear strongly degenerate parabolic equation for the total concentration of solids, and transport equations including reaction terms for the solid components and substrates. To accurately approximate the model equations, a high-order finite volume scheme is developed using polynomial reconstructions. Two reconstruction methods are used, a second-order monotonic upstream-centered scheme (MUSCL), and a third-order central weighted essentially non-oscillatory (CWENO3) method. In addition, for the time discretization, we employ strong stability preserving Runge-Kutta (SSPRK) schemes of second- and third-order. The CWENO3 scheme is complemented with limiters, and in the case of zero diffusion, the designed high-order schemes are shown to preserve an invariant-region property. This property is particularly relevant as it ensures that physically relevant numerical solutions are obtained at each time iteration. In addition, high-order approximations for the diffusion terms are proposed to approximate the complete model equations. Simulations of denitrification in SSTs under a continuously operated regime in WRRFs demonstrate the performance of the model and its discretization. Finally, convergence tests show the order of accuracy of the developed scheme and the invariant-region preservation.

</details>


### [26] [A Generalized Finite Difference-Based Fragile Points Method for Heat Conduction Problems in Non-Homogeneous Media](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06891)
*Smriti,Sundararajan Natarajan,Abhinash Malla*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper presents an enhanced Fragile Points Method (FPM) formulation for modeling implicit interfaces in 2D differential equations with non-homogeneous materials, eliminating the need for specialized numerical integration while using discontinuous, point-based polynomial trial functions with numerical flux corrections to handle interface discontinuities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a truly meshless approach for efficiently solving interface problems in non-homogeneous materials without requiring specialized numerical integration techniques, while providing a systematic mathematical foundation for interface problems that conventional Galerkin methods struggle with due to continuity requirements.

Method: The method employs simple, local, point-based polynomial trial and test functions constructed via generalized finite difference (GFD), which are inherently discontinuous and bypass continuity requirements of standard Galerkin frameworks. Natural handling of discontinuities in primary and secondary variables is achieved through FPM's discontinuous shape functions, and numerical flux corrections inspired by the discontinuous Galerkin (DG) method are incorporated to address the resulting inconsistency from discontinuities.

Result: The proposed method is validated through several benchmark problems, demonstrating its efficiency and robustness in handling implicit interfaces in 2D differential equations.

Conclusion: The enhanced FPM provides an efficient, robust, and systematic meshless framework for solving interface problems in non-homogeneous materials without the need for specialized numerical integration, leveraging DG-inspired numerical flux corrections to achieve consistency.

Abstract: This paper presents an enhanced formulation of the Fragile Points Method (FPM), a truly meshless approach for efficiently modeling implicit interfaces in two-dimensional differential equations involving non-homogeneous materials. The proposed framework eliminates the need for specialized numerical integration techniques and provides a systematic mathematical foundation for solving interface problems. Discontinuities in both primary and secondary variables across interfaces are naturally handled through the inherently discontinuous shape functions of FPM. Unlike conventional Galerkin methods, FPM employs simple, local, point-based polynomial trial and test functions constructed via a generalized finite difference approach. These discontinuous functions bypass the continuity requirements of standard Galerkin frameworks. To address the resulting inconsistency due to discontinuities, we incorporate numerical flux corrections inspired by the discontinuous Galerkin method. The proposed method is validated through several benchmark problems, demonstrating its efficiency and robustness.

</details>


### [27] [Data Assimilation with Sparse Observations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06901)
*Nan Jiang,William Layton,Nanda Nechingal Raghunathan,Troy Yang*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper analyzes nudging-based data assimilation under non-ideal conditions (moderate parameters, sparse/infrequent observations), proving that assimilation always strictly decreases errors and strictly increases the (finite) predictability horizon compared to no assimilation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous results showed nudging yields exponentially decaying errors and infinite predictability only under ideal conditions (large parameters, dense/frequent observations). This paper addresses the realistic complementary case where such ideal conditions cannot be met, asking whether nudging still provides benefit.

Method: The authors prove analytically, under conditions of moderate assimilation parameters and sparse/infrequent observations, that assimilation with any data and any positive parameter strictly reduces errors and strictly increases the now-finite predictability horizon.

Result: It is proven that even in the non-ideal regime (moderate parameters, sparse and infrequent data), nudging-based assimilation strictly improves forecast accuracy by reducing errors and extending the predictability horizon, though the horizon is finite rather than infinite.

Conclusion: Nudging data assimilation provides guaranteed, strictly monotonic improvements in error and predictability even when ideal parameter/data conditions cannot be achieved, making it a robust method in practical settings.

Abstract: Data assimilation by nudging (also called CDA) yields exponentially decaying errors and an infinite predictability horizon if the method parameter is large enough and the observations are frequent enough in time and dense enough in space. We consider the complementary case of moderate parameters and sparse and infrequent observations. We prove that assimilation with any data and any(positive) parameter strictly decreases errors and strictly increases the (now finite) predictability horizon.

</details>


### [28] [Null--Space--Free 6D Spectral Embedding with Local Rayleigh Quotient Recovery for 3D Quasiperiodic Maxwell's Eigenproblems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06965)
*Teng-Chao Sun,Tiexiang Li,Wen-Wei Lin,Xing-Long Lyu*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper develops a numerical framework for solving 3D quasiperiodic Maxwell eigenvalue problems via a 6D periodic embedding, using projected Bloch-Fourier discretization with explicit orthonormal bases to remove the gradient-type kernel exactly, an inverse Lanczos method with bounded-condition CG inner solves, and a separated multi-center Taylor expansion for efficient physical-space reconstruction on a Yee grid.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Solving Maxwell eigenvalue problems in quasiperiodic media is challenging because quasiperiodicity lacks the direct periodicity of Bloch theory. Embedding them in a higher-dimensional periodic space enables Bloch-Fourier methods, but introduces a large gradient-type kernel and expensive inner-outer solves. The paper aims to eliminate these computational bottlenecks and enable practical physical-space mode recovery.

Method: The method embeds the 3D quasiperiodic problem into 6D periodic space and applies a projected Bloch-Fourier discretization, producing a structured generalized eigenvalue problem. Explicit orthonormal bases for longitudinal/transverse subspaces remove the kernel exactly, reducing the problem to a null-space-free standard eigenvalue problem. An explicit inverse representation enables an inverse Lanczos method whose inner computation is a Hermitian positive definite CG solve with condition number bounded by that of the mass matrix. For physical-space recovery, a separated multi-center Taylor expansion reconstructs 6D Fourier eigenvectors on a 3D Yee grid, avoiding the dense Fourier-to-grid phase matrix. Local weighted Rayleigh quotients serve as independent validation, with their mass-weighted expectation proven equal to the cropped Yee Rayleigh quotient under a partition-of-unity condition.

Result: Numerical experiments confirm the accuracy and computational effectiveness of the framework, including successful physical-space recovery of 3D quasiperiodic Maxwell modes. A residual estimate quantifies the impact of inner CG solves on inverse Ritz pairs, and the independent physical-space validation via local weighted Rayleigh quotients confirms reliability.

Conclusion: The proposed framework provides an efficient and accurate approach for 3D quasiperiodic Maxwell eigenvalue problems, eliminating the kernel via explicit bases, avoiding nested linear solves via explicit inverse representation, and enabling practical dense-free physical-space reconstruction—thus making large-scale quasiperiodic photonic computations feasible.

Abstract: We develop a numerical framework for three-dimensional quasiperiodic Maxwell eigenvalue problems obtained through a six-dimensional periodic embedding. A projected Bloch--Fourier discretization yields a structured generalized eigenvalue problem with a large gradient-type kernel. Explicit orthonormal bases for the longitudinal and transverse subspaces remove this kernel exactly and reduce the original generalized eigenvalue problem to a null-space-free standard eigenvalue problem containing only the positive spectrum. An explicit inverse representation of the reduced operator avoids nested inner--outer linear solves and leads to an inverse Lanczos method whose main inner computation is a Hermitian positive definite conjugate-gradient solve with condition number bounded by that of the mass matrix; a residual estimate quantifies the effect of inner solves on the inverse Ritz pairs. To recover the computed modes in physical space, the six-dimensional Fourier eigenvectors are reconstructed on a three-dimensional Yee grid by a separated multi-center Taylor expansion, which avoids the dense Fourier-to-grid phase matrix and remains practical when direct dense reconstruction becomes prohibitively expensive. Local weighted Rayleigh quotients provide an independent physical-space validation, and their mass-weighted expectation is proved to equal the cropped Yee Rayleigh quotient under a partition-of-unity condition. Numerical experiments confirm the accuracy and computational effectiveness of the proposed framework and its physical-space recovery of three-dimensional quasiperiodic Maxwell modes.

</details>


### [29] [Uniform Inf-Sup Norm Equivalence and Robust Operator Preconditioning for Stokes Flow in tight domains with Periodic Pillars](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06999)
*Qi Xin,Yan Xie,Chen-song Zhang,Shihua Gong,Jinchao Xu*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The authors prove that for Stokes saddle-point systems on periodic pillar-array geometries (as in DLD devices), the divergence-induced inf-sup norm is uniformly equivalent to an L²+σ_εH¹ K-functional norm at the pore scale, with constants independent of period, pillar count, and mesh size. This equivalence identifies the problem with a Brinkman model at homogenized permeability and enables a block preconditioner (via operator preconditioning) built from standard algebraic-multigrid solves, achieving iteration counts essentially independent of mesh size and pillar density.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Stokes flow computations in microfluidic/DLD devices with dense pillar arrays suffer from ill-conditioning: the inf-sup constant decays as m⁻¹ (m = pillar count across device), the pressure Schur complement becomes severely ill-conditioned, and standard block solvers slow down in proportion to pillar density—a practical bottleneck as m reaches hundreds or thousands.

Method: Identify the correct pressure norm induced by the divergence operator on periodic pillar geometries. Prove that in the proportional-hole regime this inf-sup norm is uniformly equivalent to the K-functional norm at pore scale σ_ε≍ε, with constants independent of ε, m, and h. Show the perforated Stokes problem is thereby equivalent to a Brinkman problem with homogenized permeability, whose Riesz map is a pressure-mass inverse plus a scaled stiffness inverse. Combine this with operator preconditioning to build a block preconditioner from standard AMG solves.

Result: Two-dimensional Taylor–Hood experiments confirm the predicted robustness in mesh refinement, pillar density, geometric scale, close-packing regime, and time step—the preconditioner's iteration count is essentially independent of both mesh size and pillar density.

Conclusion: The proposed analysis removes the pillar-density-dependent bottleneck in Stokes preconditioning for periodic porous geometries by establishing a rigorous norm-equivalence result that yields a robust, scalable block preconditioner realizable with off-the-shelf AMG.

Abstract: Many microfluidic and porous-media computations reduce to the same core task: solving a Stokes saddle-point system on a domain perforated by a dense periodic array of pillars, as in deterministic lateral displacement (DLD) particle sorters. After rescaling the device to unit size, the geometry is controlled by a single dimensionless parameter $m$---the number of pillars across the device, equal to the inverse period. In realistic devices $m$ reaches the hundreds or thousands, and as it grows the Stokes inf-sup constant decays like $m^{-1}$, the pressure Schur complement becomes severely ill-conditioned, and standard block solvers slow down in proportion to the pillar density.
  We remove this bottleneck by identifying the pressure norm that the divergence operator induces on such geometries. For periodic pillar arrays in the proportional-hole regime, we prove that this inf-sup norm is uniformly equivalent to the $L^2+σ_εH^1$ $K$-functional norm at the pore scale $σ_ε\asympε$, with constants independent of the period, the pillar count $m$, and the mesh size $h$. The equivalence identifies the perforated Stokes problem with a Brinkman problem at a homogenized permeability, and its Riesz map reduces to a pressure-mass inverse plus a scaled stiffness inverse. Combined with operator preconditioning, this yields a block preconditioner built from standard algebraic-multigrid solves whose iteration count is essentially independent of both mesh size and pillar density. Two-dimensional Taylor--Hood experiments confirm the predicted robustness in mesh refinement, pillar density, geometric scale, close packing, and time step.

</details>


### [30] [Limiter-based fully-discrete entropy stable explicit DG schemes for ideal MHD equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07005)
*Yuchang Liu,Yan Jiang,Zheng Sun*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper proposes high-order fully-discrete entropy stable explicit discontinuous Galerkin (DG) solvers for compressible ideal magnetohydrodynamics (MHD) equations, introducing a generalized-path-decomposition framework that establishes weak cell entropy inequality and achieves genuine fully-discrete cell entropy inequality with an entropy stable limiter.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors aim to address the challenge of developing entropy stable DG schemes for ideal MHD equations, particularly those that are fully-discrete and explicit, while properly handling the non-conservative source terms and maintaining entropy stability.

Method: The key method is a novel generalized-path-decomposition framework for MHD equations in Godunov's symmetric form, where the interior volume integral of the non-conservative source term is interpreted as a path integral along a generalized path constructed by the solution polynomial. This is combined with a carefully designed entropy stable limiter to ensure the genuine fully-discrete cell entropy inequality, and the scheme is compatible with locally divergence-free space.

Result: The proposed scheme satisfies the genuine fully-discrete cell entropy inequality, enables a Lax-Wendroff-type theorem showing the solution limit satisfies the entropy condition, demonstrates natural compatibility with locally divergence-free space, and shows low numerical dissipation and strong robustness in extensive numerical experiments.

Conclusion: The generalized-path-decomposition framework provides a unified overarching structure that accommodates other existing DG solvers based on symmetric form, and the resulting fully-discrete entropy stable DG scheme is both theoretically sound and practically robust for MHD simulations.

Abstract: We propose a class of high-order fully-discrete entropy stable (ES) explicit discontinuous Galerkin (DG) solvers for the compressible ideal magnetohydrodynamics (MHD) equations. Our main theoretical contribution is the introduction of a novel generalized-path-decomposition framework for MHD equations in Godunov's symmetric form. By innovatively interpreting the interior volume integral of the non-conservative source term as a path integral along a generalized path constructed by the solution polynomial, we establish the weak cell entropy inequality for the fully-discrete DG schemes. This overarching framework also accommodates other existing DG solvers based on the symmetric form. Combined with a carefully designed ES limiter, the proposed scheme satisfies the genuine fully-discrete cell entropy inequality. With this property, a Lax--Wendroff-type theorem can be obtained to show that the solution limit satisfies the entropy condition. Finally, the scheme is naturally compatible with the locally divergence-free space. Extensive numerical experiments demonstrate the scheme's low numerical dissipation and strong robustness.

</details>


### [31] [A covariance-based reduced-order framework for solving acoustic scattering problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07083)
*Shiwei Sun,Hai Zhang,Jinrui Zhang*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A physics-aware reduced-order method for wave-scattering problems that leverages the low-rank structure of contrast source density via KL expansion, accelerated by a graph neural network surrogate for covariance reconstruction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard model order reduction methods treat scattering as generic parameterized systems and overlook underlying physical structure, limiting their practical effectiveness in wave-scattering computations.

Method: The framework (1) models the incident wave as a random variable with a prior measure, forming the contrast source as a spatial random field; (2) constructs the reduced-order basis via Karhoven-Loève (KL) expansion from the covariance function; (3) introduces a non-intrusive, physics-informed graph neural network (GNN) surrogate to learn the geometry-to-correlation-kernel mapping, enabling efficient covariance reconstruction without expensive high-fidelity assembly; (4) employs an offline-online computational paradigm for large-scale configurations.

Result: Extensive numerical experiments demonstrate robust computational acceleration over full-order models while preserving accuracy across multiple scattering scenarios.

Conclusion: The proposed physics-aware ROM with GNN surrogate achieves scalable and accurate reduced-order modeling for large-scale wave-scattering problems by exploiting both physical structure and efficient learning-based surrogates.

Abstract: This paper presents a physics-aware reduced-order method (ROM) for the efficient computation of wave-scattering problems. Standard model order reduction techniques, typically treating scattering as generic parameterized systems, frequently overlook the underlying physical structure, limiting their effectiveness in practice. To address this limitation, we propose an algorithmic framework that utilizes the intrinsic low-rank structure of the induced contrast source density. By modeling the incident wave as a random variable governed by a specified prior probability measure, we formulate the contrast source as a spatial random field whose covariance function captures essential spatial correlation and physical interactions. The reduced-order basis is then constructed via the Karhunen-Loève (KL) expansion, effectively extracting the dominant features from the scattering process to resolve multiple scattering scenarios. A central algorithmic contribution is the efficient reconstruction of the covariance matrix for arbitrary scatterer geometries and specified incident wave priors. To circumvent the prohibitive computational cost of assembling high-fidelity covariance matrices, we introduce a non-intrusive, physics-informed graph neural network (GNN) surrogate to learn the complex mapping from scatterer geometry to the source correlation kernel, enabling a highly efficient offline-online computational paradigm suitable for large-scale scattering configurations. Extensive numerical experiments demonstrate that the proposed framework achieves robust computational acceleration over full-order models without sacrificing accuracy.

</details>


### [32] [Structure-Preserving Data-Driven Identification of Port-Hamiltonian Differential-Algebraic Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07187)
*Tom Zwerschke,Matthias Ehrhardt,Michael Günther,Claudia Totzeck*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper presents a data-driven method to identify linear index-1 differential-algebraic port-Hamiltonian systems (pH-DAEs) from input-output measurements, using a regularized least-squares formulation reduced to unconstrained optimization with adjoint-based gradients.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Identifying port-Hamiltonian (pH) systems from data is already challenging, but pH-DAEs introduce additional difficulties due to algebraic constraints and index conditions that must be preserved during identification.

Method: The authors establish a structure-preserving formulation of pH-DAEs and derive an implicit midpoint discretization preserving algebraic constraints and dissipation inequality. They formulate identification as a regularized least-squares problem constrained by pH-DAE dynamics, then exploit the index-1 structure to reduce it to unconstrained optimization over system parameters. An adjoint-based formulation is derived for efficient gradient evaluation, enabling gradient-based optimization.

Result: Numerical experiments show the approach successfully identifies surrogate pH-DAE systems that accurately reproduce input-output behavior of reference systems. The method shows potential for reduced-order surrogate modeling, and cross-validation with independent inputs confirms predictive capability.

Conclusion: The proposed structure-preserving identification framework effectively learns pH-DAE surrogate models from data, with demonstrated accuracy, reduced-order modeling potential, and generalization ability, supported by theoretical guarantees on minimizer existence under suitable assumptions.

Abstract: We present a data-driven approach to identifying linear index-1 differential-algebraic pH systems (pH-DAEs) based on input-output measurements. In comparison to the identification of port-Hamiltonian (pH) systems, the algebraic constraint and the index condition pose additional challenges. First, we establish a structure-preserving formulation of the considered pH-DAE class and derive an implicit midpoint discretization that preserves the algebraic constraints and discrete dissipation inequality. We formulate the identification problem as a regularized least-squares minimization problem subject to the pH-DAE dynamics. Exploiting the index-1 structure, we reduce the constrained problem to an unconstrained optimization problem over the system parameters while preserving the port-Hamiltonian structure. Next, we derive an adjoint-based formulation to efficiently evaluate the gradient of the resulting reduced cost functional. This enables us to use gradient-based optimization methods for parameter estimation. Under suitable assumptions on the admissible parameter set, the existence of a minimizer is established. Numerical experiments demonstrate that the proposed approach can identify surrogate pH-DAE systems that accurately reproduce the input-output behavior of reference systems. Further investigations show the approach's potential for identifying reduced-order surrogate models. Cross-validation with independent input signals confirms the predictive capability of the identified models.

</details>


### [33] [On Prior-to-Posterior Stability in the Wasserstein Metric for Bayesian Inverse Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07197)
*Lianghao Cao*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper studies the stability of the prior-to-posterior map in Bayesian inverse problems, where perturbations are measured in the Wasserstein metric W_p. The authors identify conditions on likelihood regularity and admissible prior classes that ensure uniform, Hölder, and Lipschitz stability of the posterior with respect to prior perturbations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Priors in Bayesian inverse problems are often approximated through discretization, hyperparameter estimation, or generative modeling. Understanding how prior approximation errors propagate to the posterior and subsequent predictions is crucial for reliable inference, yet rigorous stability analysis of the prior-to-posterior map in Wasserstein metrics has been lacking.

Method: The paper develops stability estimates for the prior-to-posterior map measured in W_p metrics. It identifies conditions including: (1) bounded uniformly continuous likelihoods with uniformly integrable p-th moments and positive evidence lower bounds for uniform stability; (2) global Hölder regularity of the likelihood with uniform higher moment bounds for Hölder estimates via coupling arguments; (3) interpolation arguments with uniform Poincaré bounds and globally Lipschitz negative log-likelihoods for Lipschitz stability. For Gaussian priors on separable Hilbert spaces with bounded Lipschitz forward models and additive Gaussian noise, posterior W_2 bounds are derived even for mutually singular prior perturbations.

Result: The paper establishes sharp stability exponents for Hölder estimates, shows that Lipschitz likelihoods do not generally yield Lipschitz stability for p>1 even with bounded-support priors, and provides Lipschitz stability under Poincaré bounds. Numerical experiments on a two-layer Darcy inverse problem confirm the predicted Hölder and Lipschitz rates and demonstrate control of errors in posterior mean and standard deviation of a Lipschitz quantity of interest.

Conclusion: The paper provides a comprehensive stability framework for the prior-to-posterior map under Wasserstein perturbations, offering sharp conditions and rates that can guide error analysis in practical Bayesian inverse problems where priors are approximated via discretization or generative models.

Abstract: Priors in Bayesian inverse problems are often approximated through discretization, hyperparameter estimation, or generative modeling. Understanding how prior approximation errors propagate to the posterior and subsequent predictions is therefore important. In this work, we study the stability of the prior-to-posterior map where both prior and posterior perturbations are measured in the same Wasserstein metric $W_p$, $p\geq1$. We identify conditions on likelihood regularity and admissible prior classes that ensure uniform, Hölder, and Lipschitz stability. For bounded likelihoods that are uniformly continuous on bounded sets, uniform stability holds over prior classes with uniformly integrable $p$-th moments and a common positive evidence lower bound. With global Hölder regularity of the likelihood and uniform bounds on higher prior moments, a coupling argument leads to a Hölder estimate with a sharp exponent. For $p>1$, a Lipschitz likelihood need not give Lipschitz stability, even for priors with bounded support. We establish Lipschitz stability through an interpolation argument under uniform Poincaré bounds and a globally Lipschitz negative log-likelihood with uniformly bounded essential oscillation under the priors. For Gaussian priors on separable Hilbert spaces and bounded Lipschitz forward models with additive Gaussian noise, these estimates give posterior $W_2$ bounds even for mutually singular prior perturbations. Numerical experiments for a two-layer Darcy inverse problem illustrate the predicted Hölder and Lipschitz rates and the resulting control of errors in the posterior mean and standard deviation of a Lipschitz quantity of interest.

</details>


### [34] [Analysis of the Ill-Conditioning of the Discrete Inverse Laplace transform in Monte Carlo Simulations of Quantum Many-Body Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07208)
*Phil-Alexander Hofmann,Thomas Chuna,Tobias Dornheim,Michael Hecht*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper provides explicit mathematical bounds on the condition numbers of discretized analytic continuation problems, showing that detailed-balance symmetry and low-dimensional representations substantially mitigate ill-conditioning despite the fundamental ill-posedness of the continuous inverse Laplace transform.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Analytic continuation from imaginary-time quantum Monte Carlo data to real-frequency spectra is a severely ill-posed inverse problem that is essential in quantum many-body calculations, yet its mathematical underpinnings—particularly how the intrinsic ill-posedness propagates to finite-dimensional discretizations—were not well understood.

Method: The authors express the discrete analytic continuation problem as a diagonally scaled monomial Vandermonde matrix with exponentially distributed nodes, and show that imposing detailed-balance symmetry transforms it into a diagonally scaled Chebyshev-Vandermonde system. They then derive explicit lower and upper bounds on the condition numbers in terms of physical and discretization parameters.

Result: The bounds reveal super-exponential growth of the condition number with reconstruction dimension that cannot be removed by increasing imaginary-time samples alone. Detailed-balance substantially improves conditioning especially in the pre-asymptotic regime, though the asymptotic super-exponential dependence remains. A low-dimensional regime is identified where the super-exponential contribution is suppressed, enabling stable reconstruction.

Conclusion: The results provide a mathematical explanation for the effectiveness of low-dimensional spectral representations and detailed-balance in analytic continuation, showing they mitigate ill-conditioning, delay catastrophic growth, and enlarge the range of numerically accessible reconstructions—without removing the fundamental ill-posedness.

Abstract: Analytic continuation of imaginary-time quantum Monte Carlo data to real-frequency spectra requires the inversion of a severely ill-posed two-sided Laplace transform and arises naturally in quantum many-body calculations of dynamic properties. In this work, we distinguish the intrinsic ill-posedness of the continuous inverse two-sided Laplace problem from the conditioning of its finite-dimensional discretization. For equidistant sampling and reconstruction grids, we express the discrete problem via a diagonally scaled monomial Vandermonde matrix with exponentially distributed nodes. Imposing the physical detailed-balance symmetry transforms the discretization into a diagonally scaled Chebyshev-Vandermonde system. Exploiting these structures, we derive explicit lower and upper bounds on the condition numbers in terms of the physical and discretization parameters. For the unconstrained discretization, our bounds reveal super-exponential growth of the condition number with the reconstruction dimension, which cannot be removed by increasing the number of imaginary-time samples alone. Detailed-balance substantially improves the conditioning, especially in the practically relevant pre-asymptotic regime, although the asymptotic super-exponential dependence remains. In both cases, our bounds identify a low-dimensional regime in which the super-exponential contribution is suppressed and stable reconstruction remains feasible. These results provide a mathematical explanation for the effectiveness of low-dimensional spectral representations and detailed-balance in analytic continuation. While they do not remove the fundamental ill-posedness, they substantially mitigate the ill-conditioning of its finite-dimensional discretization, delay the onset of its catastrophic super-exponential growth and thereby enlarge the range of numerically accessible reconstructions.

</details>


### [35] [A new formulation of Discontinuous Galerkin method for interface capturing approaches](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07327)
*W. Herri,K. Benmoussa,R. Bouydou,D. Yakoubi*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper presents a new formulation of the Lesaint-Raviart discontinuous Galerkin method for solving the transport equation in free-surface flow simulations, coupled with Navier-Stokes equations and the Continuum Surface Force model for surface tension, validated on benchmarks like rising bubble and Rayleigh-Taylor instability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Free-surface flow simulations using interface capturing methods require accurate resolution of a transport equation, which is challenging especially for problems without inflow boundaries where standard methods struggle.

Method: The transport equation is solved using a new formulation of the Lesaint-Raviart discontinuous Galerkin method suitable for problems without inflow boundaries. The flow is governed by incompressible Navier-Stokes equations with surface tension, discretized with P2-P1 Taylor-Hood finite elements in space, and surface tension is modeled via the Continuum Surface Force approach.

Result: The proposed approach was implemented and validated on several benchmarks including the single rising bubble and the Rayleigh-Taylor instability, demonstrating its effectiveness.

Conclusion: The new Lesaint-Raviart DG formulation provides a robust interface capturing method for free-surface problems without inflow boundaries, verified on standard benchmark problems.

Abstract: In computational fluid dynamics, the numerical simulation of free-surface flows using interface capturing approaches (level set, pseudo-concentration) requires accurate resolution of a transport equation. In the present work, this equation is solved using a new formulation of the Lesaint--Raviart discontinuous Galerkin method, which is particularly suitable for problems without inflow boundaries. The motion is described by the incompressible Navier--Stokes equations with surface tension, discretized using the standard $P2-P1$ Taylor--Hood finite element method in space. Surface tension effects are represented using the so-called Continuum Surface Force model. Finally, the proposed approach is implemented and tested on several benchmarks, including the single rising bubble and the Rayleigh--Taylor instability.

</details>


### [36] [Frequency Domain Biot--Allard Equations for Isotropic and Anisotropic Poroelastic Media: Two-field formulations and iterative splitting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07388)
*Morten Jakobsen,Jakob Seierstad Stokke,Kundan Kumar,Florin Adrian Radu*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper presents a frequency-domain formulation of Biot-Allard dynamic poroelasticity equations for anisotropic, heterogeneous media with memory effects, using two equivalent two-field representations and stabilized iterative splitting with a conforming Galerkin FEM discretization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a rigorous and efficient framework for modeling wave phenomena in complex porous media, addressing challenges from convolutional memory terms and the lack of adjoint/skew-adjoint structure in coupling operators at finite frequencies, while enabling parallel computation and multi-frequency inversion.

Method: Two equivalent two-field representations: displacement-pressure and velocity-pressure-rate formulations. The velocity-pressure-rate representation restores skew-adjoint structure in the quasi-static limit. Analysis proves continuity of coupling operators and coercivity of diagonal blocks for L-stabilized splitting. Frequency-domain setting eliminates convolutional memory terms via complex-valued parameters, reducing problems to elliptic BVPs. A conforming Galerkin FEM preserves block structure.

Result: Numerical experiments confirm robustness and capture frequency-dependent attenuation. A large-scale pseudo-spectral wave simulation demonstrates discretization independence of the framework.

Conclusion: The work provides a rigorous and efficient framework well-suited for parallel computation and multi-frequency inversion for wave modeling in porous media.

Abstract: We present a frequency-domain formulation of Biot's dynamic poroelastic equations with frequency-dependent dissipation (Biot--Allard) for anisotropic, heterogeneous media with memory effects. Two equivalent two-field representations--a displacement-pressure and a velocity-pressure-rate formulation--enable stabilized iterative splitting. While coupling operators generally lack an adjoint or skew-adjoint relationship at finite frequencies, the velocity--pressure-rate representation restores a skew-adjoint structure in the quasi-static limit. We prove continuity of the coupling operators and coercivity of the diagonal blocks, essential for convergence of the L-stabilized splitting scheme. The frequency-domain setting eliminates convolutional memory terms, incorporates attenuation and dispersion via complex-valued parameters, and reduces the time-dependent problem to a family of elliptic boundary-value problems suited for parallel computation and multi-frequency inversion. A conforming Galerkin finite element discretization preserves block structure, and numerical experiments confirm robustness and capture frequency-dependent attenuation. To illustrate discretization independence, we include a large-scale wave simulation using a pseudo-spectral method. This work provides a rigorous and efficient framework for modeling wave phenomena in complex porous media.

</details>


### [37] [A Systematic Analysis of Automatic Differentiation versus Discretization-based Constraints for Physics-Informed PDE Solvers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07437)
*Xing Guo,Hongwei Tang,Zewei Meng,Yidong Zhang,Shaoqiu Xiao,Feng Liu*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper systematically compares automatic differentiation (AD)-based and discretization-based physics-informed neural networks (PINNs) across problems ranging from linear Poisson to high-Mach hypersonic flows, revealing that discretization-based constraints outperform AD as nonlinearity increases, and that GNNs become more advantageous than MLPs under complex nonlinearity and boundary conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While PINNs using automatic differentiation have become popular for solving PDEs, AD has inherent limitations with higher-order derivatives and discontinuous solutions. Although discretization-based constraints have emerged as an alternative, the comparative applicability of these two paradigms has remained unexplored, motivating systematic investigation.

Method: The authors conduct systematic experiments across a spectrum of problems from simple linear Poisson to high-Mach hypersonic flows with strong discontinuities. They decompose approximation, optimization, and truncation errors, comparing automatic differentiation-based and discretization-based approaches, while evaluating two network architectures: multi-layer perceptron (MLP) and graph neural network (GNN).

Result: The results show a consistent trend: as nonlinearity strengthens, discretization-based constraints gain increasingly pronounced accuracy advantages, with smaller optimization errors compensating for truncation errors. Additionally, more complex nonlinearity and boundary conditions favor GNN over MLP architectures.

Conclusion: The findings establish a robust practical guideline for configuring neural PDE solvers in demanding engineering applications, clarifying the fundamental trade-offs and error-governing mechanisms of both paradigms.

Abstract: Physics-informed neural networks (PINNs) represent a growing frontier in using artificial intelligence to solve partial differential equations (PDEs). Automatic differentiation (AD) plays a central role in this paradigm, which is mesh-free and replaces traditional iterative solvers with gradient-based optimization in continuous space. However, the inherent limitations of AD, particularly in handling higher-order derivatives and discontinuous solutions, pose significant challenges for complex problems. This has motivated a growing number of researchers to explore discretization-based constraints as an alternative path. Yet, the respective applicability of these two paradigms remains largely unexplored. In this work, we conduct systematic experiments across a wide spectrum of problems, from simple linear Poisson to high-Mach hypersonic flows with strong discontinuities. Through a rigorous decomposition of approximation, optimization, and truncation errors, we systematically elucidate the fundamental trade-offs and error-governing mechanisms of both paradigms, as well as two representative network architectures: multi-layer perceptron (MLP) and graph neural network (GNN). Our results reveal a consistent trend: as nonlinearity strengthens, the accuracy advantage of discretization-based constraints becomes increasingly pronounced, with smaller optimization errors compensating for the truncation errors. Moreover, the more complex the nonlinearity and boundary conditions, the greater the advantage of GNN over MLP. These insights offer a robust practical guideline for configuring neural PDE solvers in demanding engineering applications. Our source data and code are available at https://github.com/guoxing0809/neuropde_analysis.

</details>


### [38] [Analysis of a Surface Crouzeix-Raviart Element for the Stokes Problem](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07527)
*Manisha Chowdhury,Carolin Mehlmann*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper studies a surface Stokes system on a 2D manifold embedded in 3D space, using a nonconforming Crouzeix-Raviart finite element for velocity (with piecewise constant pressure). A stabilization via edge-jump penalty is introduced to restore discrete Korn inequality and prevent spurious oscillations. The paper establishes the inf-sup compatibility condition and derives optimal a-priori error estimates in energy and L2 norms, confirmed by numerical experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the challenge of approximating vector-valued flow problems on curved surfaces, where using Crouzeix-Raviart finite elements with the symmetric strain-rate tensor causes spurious oscillations due to failure of the discrete Korn inequality on the nonconforming space.

Method: The method employs a nonconforming Crouzeix-Raviart finite element on a polyhedral approximation of the surface for velocity, piecewise constants for pressure, and stabilizes the momentum equation by adding an edge-jump penalty term to restore coercivity (via discrete Korn inequality).

Result: The stabilized finite element pair satisfies the discrete inf-sup compatibility condition, and optimal a-priori error estimates are derived in both the energy norm and the L2-norm, corroborated by numerical experiments.

Conclusion: The stabilized Crouzeix-Raviart/piecewise-constant pair is a valid and effective discretization for surface Stokes problems, overcoming the Korn inequality failure and yielding optimal convergence rates.

Abstract: Recently, increasing attention has been paid to finite element discretizations of vector-valued flow problems posed on curved surfaces. In this work, we study a surface Stokes system defined on a two-dimensional manifold embedded in three-dimensional space. The surface velocity field is approximated using a nonconforming Crouzeix-Raviart finite element on a polyhedral approximation of the surface, while the pressure is discretized by piecewise constant functions. The governing equations involve the symmetric strain-rate tensor, whose approximation with Crouzeix-Raviart finite elements leads to spurious oscillations in the velocity field because the discrete Korn inequality fails on the nonconforming space. We therefore stabilize the momentum equation by adding an edge-jump penalty term, which restores the coervity of the bilinear term as the discrete Korn inequality holds for this stabilized version. Additionally, we establish that this finite element pair of velocity and pressure spaces satisfies the discrete inf-sup compatibility condition. Furthermore, we derive optimal a-priori error estimates in both the energy norm and the $L^2$-norm. These theoretical results are corroborated by numerical experiments.

</details>


### [39] [A numerical method for two-dimensional Boussinesq-Full Dispersion systems modelling internal wave propagation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07659)
*Angel Durán*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper presents numerical approximation methods for two-dimensional asymptotic models of internal wave propagation in a two-layer fluid system, including well-posedness analysis, a spectral Fourier-Galerkin semidiscrete approximation with convergence analysis, and fully discrete time integration using the implicit midpoint rule, validated through numerical experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop and analyze reliable numerical schemes for approximating solutions of PDE models describing internal wave propagation in stratified two-layer fluid systems, where accurate simulation of interfacial dynamics is important.

Method: The approach combines: (1) well-posedness analysis of the periodic initial-value problem, (2) a semidiscrete spectral Fourier-Galerkin spatial discretization with convergence analysis, and (3) temporal integration via the implicit midpoint rule to form a fully discrete scheme.

Result: The paper establishes convergence of the semidiscrete Fourier-Galerkin approximation, describes properties of the fully discrete implicit midpoint scheme, and demonstrates its performance through numerical experiments.

Conclusion: The proposed spectral semidiscrete approximation coupled with implicit midpoint time-stepping provides an effective and convergent numerical framework for simulating two-dimensional internal wave propagation in two-layer systems.

Abstract: The paper is concerned with the numerical approximation of some two-dimensional asymptotic models for the propagation of internal waves in a two-layer system in the form of pde's for the interfacial deviation and the velocity. After the study of well-posedness of the periodic initial-value problem, the convergence of a semidiscrete approximation based on a spectral Fourier-Galerkin method is analyzed. The resulting semidiscrete system is then integrated numerically in time with the implicit midpoint rule. Some properties of the fully discrete scheme are described and its performance is illustrated with some numerical experiments.

</details>


### [40] [Mapping for Approximation: A Unified View of Rescaled, Variably Scaled and Rational Kernel Methods](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07743)
*Stefano De Marchi*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper advocates a complementary approach to classical approximation methods: instead of changing sampling sets, data points, or bases, keep sampled values fixed and modify the representation through suitable mappings. It unifies several existing constructions (rescaled RBF interpolation, variably scaled kernels, mapped bases, fake nodes, and rational kernel expansions) into a common framework, summarizing their approximation and stability properties, with reproducible numerical experiments demonstrating constant reproduction, error reduction, and suppression of the Runge phenomenon.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical approximation methods are typically improved by changing the sampling set, increasing data points, or selecting different basis functions. The authors propose a complementary viewpoint that unifies existing but disconnected techniques—modifying the representation through mappings while keeping sampled values fixed—to provide a coherent framework for adapting approximation spaces to data geometry and regularity.

Method: The paper develops a common notation and unified framework for several mapping-based mechanisms: rescaled RBF interpolation (normalization enforcing exact constant reproduction), variably scaled kernels (lifting data to higher-dimensional manifolds via scale functions), mapped bases and fake nodes (mapping the approximation space without resampling), and rational kernel expansions (nonlinear mappings of the trial space). It also describes how discontinuous and rational variants fit the same framework, and supports findings with reproducible numerical experiments.

Result: Numerical experiments illustrate three key outcomes: exact constant reproduction, error reduction through data-mimicking scale functions, and suppression of the Runge phenomenon via mapped polynomial bases. The results confirm that mappings provide a flexible and effective way to adapt approximation spaces to data geometry and regularity without modifying original observations.

Conclusion: The paper supports a general principle: mappings offer a flexible complement to classical approximation improvement strategies, unifying previously disparate techniques into one framework that can adapt approximation spaces to data characteristics while keeping the original sampled values unchanged.

Abstract: Classical approximation methods are usually improved by changing the sampling set, increasing the number of data points, or selecting a different basis. We advocate a complementary viewpoint: keep the sampled values fixed and modify the representation through a suitable mapping. This viewpoint unifies several constructions that were originally introduced for different purposes. Rescaled radial basis function interpolation maps the interpolation operator through a normalization that enforces exact reproduction of constants. Variably scaled kernels map the geometry by lifting the data to a higherdimensional manifold determined by a scale function. Mapped bases and fake nodes map the approximation space without resampling the data, while rational kernel expansions can be interpreted as nonlinear mappings of the trial space. We develop a common notation for these mechanisms, summarize their approximation and stability properties, and explain how discontinuous and rational variants fit the same framework. Reproducible numerical experiments illustrate constant reproduction, error reduction through data-mimicking scale functions, and the suppression of the Runge phenomenon by mapped polynomial bases. The results support a general principle: mappings provide a flexible way to adapt approximation spaces to data geometry and regularity without modifying the original observations

</details>


### [41] [LoCCA: Localized Chebyshev Cross Approximation for Kernel Matrix Factorization via Nodal Perturbation Stability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07882)
*Sumit Singh,Shrirup Dutta,Sivaram Ambikasaran*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: LoCCA is a data-driven framework combining smooth polynomial interpolation with flexible matrix factorizations by mapping Chebyshev grid nodes onto nearest physical neighbors in unstructured point sets, supported by new perturbation theory proving stability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard grid-based matrix factorization methods fail on irregular geometries, and there is a need for approaches that maintain mathematical stability and accuracy while working on unstructured point sets.

Method: LoCCA dynamically maps ideal Chebyshev grid nodes to their nearest physical neighbors within unstructured point sets, bridging polynomial interpolation with flexible matrix factorizations. It is supported by a new perturbation theory showing that displacing Chebyshev nodes onto physical data points preserves stability and accuracy.

Result: LoCCA provides strict, reliable error control on irregular geometries where standard grid-based methods fail, achieves substantial speedups, and delivers near-optimal matrix compression.

Conclusion: LoCCA offers a robust data-driven alternative for matrix compression and interpolation on irregular geometries, combining theoretical guarantees with practical speed and accuracy advantages.

Abstract: We propose Local Chebyshev Cross-Approximation (LoCCA), a data-driven framework that bridges smooth polynomial interpolation with flexible matrix factorizations. LoCCA dynamically maps ideal grid nodes to their nearest physical neighbors within unstructured point sets. We support this framework with a new perturbation theory, proving that displacing Chebyshev nodes onto physical data points retains mathematical stability and accuracy without explosive error growth. Numerically, LoCCA provides strict, reliable error control even on irregular geometries where standard grid-based methods fail, achieving substantial speedups and near-optimal matrix compression.

</details>


### [42] [Threshold dynamics for subdiffusive grain growth](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07938)
*Marvin Fritz*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A new fractional threshold-dynamics algorithm combining L1 discretization of the Caputo time derivative with Helmholtz-resolvent thresholding is proposed for subdiffusive interface motion and multiphase grain-growth simulations, showing faster coarsening for smaller fractional orders.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To extend threshold-dynamics methods (such as Merriman-Bence-Osher) to subdiffusive interface motion with temporal memory, enabling more accurate modeling of anomalous diffusion phenomena in grain growth.

Method: Combines L1 discretization of the Caputo time derivative with Helmholtz-resolvent thresholding. At each time step, the L1 convolution produces a convex history average of previously thresholded states, which is propagated by a Helmholtz diffusion solve and projected back to pure phases by thresholding. For α=1 it reduces to a Helmholtz-resolvent analogue of MBO, while for 0<α<1 it introduces subdiffusive temporal memory.

Result: Establishes variational characterization, maximum and comparison principles, and stability for the continuous Helmholtz diffusion step. Derives a memory-tail estimate and finite-dimensional pinning criterion under an explicit resolvent-contraction hypothesis. Numerical experiments on periodic grids show faster coarsening for smaller fractional orders, and examine the influence of L1 history and smoothing length.

Conclusion: The proposed fractional threshold-dynamics algorithm effectively models subdiffusive grain growth with controllable temporal memory, validated through theoretical guarantees and numerical experiments.

Abstract: We propose a new fractional threshold-dynamics algorithm for subdiffusive interface motion and apply it to multiphase grain-growth simulations. The method combines the L1 discretization of the Caputo time derivative with a Helmholtz-resolvent thresholding step. At each time level, the L1 convolution produces a convex history average of the previously thresholded states; this history-dependent field is then propagated by a Helmholtz diffusion solve and projected back to pure phases by thresholding. For $α=1$, the method reduces to a Helmholtz-resolvent analogue of the Merriman-Bence-Osher scheme, whereas for $0<α<1$ it introduces a subdiffusive temporal memory. We establish a variational characterization, maximum and comparison principles, and stability for the continuous Helmholtz diffusion step. We also derive a memory-tail estimate and a finite-dimensional pinning criterion under an explicit resolvent-contraction hypothesis. The equal-tension multiphase scheme is implemented on periodic grids. The numerical experiments investigate the joint influence of the L1 history and the smoothing length. The fixed-step runs show faster coarsening for smaller fractional orders in the tested regime, while a comparison at fixed smoothing length examines the influence of the L1 history on successive updates.

</details>


### [43] [A third-medium approach for electro-thermo-mechanical contact considering Joule heating](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08031)
*Maximilian Vorwerk*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper proposes a unified electro-thermo-mechanical contact formulation based on the third-medium concept for simulating connectors, where mechanical contact, electric current flow, and heat conduction are modeled in a single finite element framework without explicit contact-surface tracking, with strong multiphysics coupling through Joule heating and temperature-dependent conductivity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Connectors frequently combine mechanical contact with electric current transfer and heat generation, and existing approaches struggle to capture these interacting processes in a unified manner without complex interface tracking.

Method: A third-medium-based contact formulation is developed within a unified finite element framework. Deformation-dependent switching functions control electrical and thermal transport onset during interface closure. The displacement, electric potential, and temperature fields are solved monolithically with Joule heating coupling, feedback via temperature-dependent conductivity, and an independently interpolated deformation-gradient-like field for robust third-medium regularization under severe compression.

Result: Numerical examples demonstrate successful capture of contact-induced current transfer, subsequent Joule heating, transient temperature evolution, and localized current paths during progressive closure of rough interfaces.

Conclusion: The proposed formulation effectively models coupled electro-thermo-mechanical contact phenomena in connectors, offering a robust unified approach that avoids explicit contact-surface tracking while capturing the multiphysics interactions.

Abstract: Connectors are ubiquitous in technical systems and frequently combine mechanical contact with electric current transfer and heat generation. To capture these interacting processes, an electro-thermo-mechanical contact formulation based on the third-medium concept is proposed. Mechanical contact, electric current flow, and heat conduction are represented within a unified finite element framework without explicit contact-surface tracking. Deformation-dependent switching functions govern the onset of electrical and thermal transport during interface closure. Electrical conduction is coupled to the transient thermal problem through Joule heating, while the temperature dependence of the electrical conductivity provides the corresponding feedback on the current flow. The coupled displacement, electric potential, and temperature fields are solved monolithically and combined with an independently interpolated deformation-gradient-like field for robust third-medium regularization under severe compression. Numerical examples demonstrate contact-induced current transfer, subsequent Joule heating and transient temperature evolution, as well as localized current paths during progressive closure of rough interfaces.

</details>


### [44] [GridapGeosciences.jl: A Julia finite element package for partial differential equations on general manifolds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08036)
*Tamara A. Tambyah,Alberto F. Martín,David Lee,Santiago Badia*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: Presents GridapGeosciences.jl, a parallel distributed-memory Julia package for solving PDEs on manifolds, built on the Gridap finite element library. It features cubed sphere manifold generation via mesh refinement and demonstrates intrinsic variational formulations across three applications: scalar transport with stabilization, thermal shallow water equations, and linearized Boussinesq equations on 3D manifolds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a flexible, abstract, and high-level numerical framework for approximating PDEs on general manifolds, particularly for geoscience applications, where both intrinsic and extrinsic formulations are needed.

Method: A parallel distributed-memory Julia package built on Gridap that uses mesh refinement to construct discrete manifold domains abstractly. The cubed sphere manifold serves as a showcase. Intrinsic variational formulations of stabilization terms (shown equivalent to extrinsic counterparts) are developed for transport and thermal shallow water equations, and a linearized Boussinesq formulation captures 3D manifold dynamics.

Result: Demonstrates functionality across three applications: intrinsic stabilization terms equivalent to known extrinsic formulations in scalar transport, an intrinsic thermal shallow water formulation with upwinded fluxes, and a linearized Boussinesq model capturing both surface and vertical dynamics on a 3D manifold.

Conclusion: GridapGeosciences.jl provides a scalable, abstract framework for manifold-based PDE simulation in geoscience, validated through multiple applications involving both 2D and 3D manifold geometries.

Abstract: We present GridapGeosciences.jl, a new parallel distributed-memory Julia package for the numerical approximation of partial differential equations on general manifolds. Our abstract approach to generating discrete manifold domains relies on mesh refinement in general, and is implemented within the Gridap finite element library, whose high-level interface enables seamless definition of both intrinsic and extrinsic variational formulations. GridapGeosciences.jl features the cubed sphere manifold, which is used in this study to showcase the functionality of GridapGeosciences.jl in different applications. The first application involves scalar transport where we develop intrinsic formulations of common stabilisation terms, which are shown to be equivalent to well-known extrinsic stabilisation formalisations. Such stabilisation terms are used to develop an intrinsic formulation of the two-dimensional thermal shallow water equations that includes upwinded numerical fluxes. The final application is a linearisation of the Boussinesq equations where we demonstrate the capacity of GridapGeosciences.jl to capture both surface and vertical dynamics on a three-dimensional manifold.

</details>


### [45] [Finite-Precision Symmetric Krylov Methods: Exact Rounding Examples, Block Paige Identities,and a Variable-Block Lanczos Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08066)
*Mohit Sinha*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper analyzes the floating-point behavior of short-recurrence Krylov methods (equivalent in exact arithmetic but divergent in finite precision), provides convergence bounds under perturbation/transfer hypotheses, proves precision bounds for backward error in n updates for n×n matrices, and extends the analysis to block Lanczos with Householder orthogonalization and singular-value truncation including exact block Lanczos relations for nearby symmetric problems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Short-recurrence Krylov methods that are mathematically equivalent in exact arithmetic often produce divergent results in floating-point arithmetic. The paper aims to understand and quantify these divergences, providing theoretical guarantees (convergence theorems, precision bounds, backward error analysis) for methods like steepest descent, Hestenes-Stiefel CG, and block Lanczos under finite-precision conditions.

Method: The paper uses several analytical approaches: (1) constructing an exactly representable two-cycle for steepest descent with recursively updated residuals to illustrate floating-point divergence; (2) proving a complementary convergence theorem giving sufficient conditions for geometric decrease of the stored residual; (3) demonstrating different outcomes between CG and direct Lanczos-Galerkin approaches for a second positive definite family; (4) proving sufficient precision bounds for prescribed backward error within n updates under dense left-to-right evaluation order with computable stopping tests; (5) using polynomial estimates and numerical experiments to examine precision vs. iteration trade-offs; (6) analyzing block Lanczos via Householder orthogonalization and singular-value truncation with componentwise/normwise error bounds, a block form of Paige's identity, and Gram-matrix arguments.

Result: The paper shows: CG finds the exact solution after four updates in one example while a two-step projected system becomes inconsistent; a common spectral enclosure gives comparable convergence bounds under stated hypotheses; a sufficient precision bound for backward error within n updates is proven; block Lanczos computed coefficients satisfy a controlled local recurrence and an exact block Lanczos relation for a nearby symmetric problem in a larger space; a block form of Paige's identity bounds Ritz vector overlap; and an inter-block recurrence with a Gram-matrix argument counts additional nearby Ritz values.

Conclusion: The paper provides a comprehensive floating-point analysis framework for short-recurrence Krylov methods, establishing both divergence counterexamples and convergence guarantees, extending results to block Lanczos methods, and linking the analysis to prior frameworks by Paige and Greenbaum for inexact matrix-vector products and preconditioning.

Abstract: Short-recurrence Krylov methods that are equivalent in exact arithmetic often diverge in floating-point arithmetic. To illustrate this, we provide an exactly representable two-cycle for steepest descent with a recursively updated residual. A complementary convergence theorem gives a sufficient condition under which the stored residual decreases geometrically. We then show that a second positive definite family yields different outcomes for a Hestenes--Stiefel Conjugate Gradient (CG) implementation and a direct Lanczos--Galerkin approach. CG finds the exact solution after four updates, whereas the two-step projected system becomes inconsistent. Under stated perturbation and transfer hypotheses, a common spectral enclosure gives comparable convergence bounds. For a dense, left-to-right evaluation order, we prove a sufficient precision bound for a prescribed backward error within $n$ updates for an $n\times n$ matrix, together with a computable stopping test and specified exponent-range assumptions. We use polynomial estimates and numerical experiments to examine the trade-off between working precision and the number of CG iterations needed to achieve a prescribed backward error. We also compare results on inexact matrix-vector products and preconditioning with the frameworks of Paige and Greenbaum.
  For block Lanczos, we analyze Householder orthogonalization and singular-value truncation as the block size changes. Under componentwise and normwise error bounds, the computed coefficients satisfy a controlled local recurrence and an exact block Lanczos relation for a nearby symmetric problem in a larger space. A block form of Paige's identity bounds the overlap with a Ritz vector along its residual coordinate direction. An inter-block recurrence describes the evolution of overlap, and a Gram-matrix argument gives a count of additional nearby Ritz values.

</details>


### [46] [Third-order Halley-type iterative method with positive and bounded correction function](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08110)
*Hirai Mukasa*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper proposes a new one-point iterative method for finding simple roots of nonlinear equations by modifying Halley's method. The correction function is positive, bounded, and matches Halley's local expansion near simple roots, avoiding singularity and sign reversal issues while maintaining at least third-order convergence and global convergence guarantees.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Halley's method suffers from singularity and sign reversal of its correction function, which can lead to convergence failures. The authors aim to develop a robust modification that retains Halley's favorable convergence properties (at least third-order) while overcoming these numerical instabilities.

Method: The proposed method modifies Halley's correction function so that it becomes positive and bounded on the real line, while still matching the local expansion of Halley's correction function near simple roots. Sufficient conditions for global convergence are also established theoretically.

Result: Numerical experiments show the proposed method achieves high convergence success rates and favorable iteration counts over a wide range of initial guesses, including robust behavior near Halley's correction function singularity. An application to the van der Waals equation demonstrates practical effectiveness.

Conclusion: The modified Halley-type method provides a robust alternative to classical Halley's method, eliminating singularity and sign reversal issues while preserving high-order convergence and extending global convergence guarantees.

Abstract: In this paper, we propose a new one-point iterative method for finding simple roots of nonlinear equations by modifying Halley's method. Its correction function is positive and bounded on $\mathbb{R}$ and matches the local expansion of Halley's correction function near a simple root. The method avoids the singularity and sign reversal of Halley's correction function while retaining at least third-order local convergence. We also establish sufficient conditions for global convergence. Numerical experiments show that the proposed method achieves high convergence success rates and favorable iteration counts over a wide range of initial guesses. Further experiments near the singularity of Halley's correction function demonstrate robust convergence behavior of the proposed method. An application to the van der Waals equation illustrates the effectiveness of the proposed method.

</details>


### [47] [Finite difference methods for three kinds of reaction-diffusion equations with free boundaries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08212)
*Caiyun Huang,Weizhi Liao,Weiping Bu*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper develops finite difference numerical schemes for solving three types of reaction-diffusion equations with free boundaries, using a front-fixing transformation and M-matrix-based discretization, with only mild time-step restrictions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop reliable numerical methods for reaction-diffusion equations with free boundaries, and to establish numerical theory (positivity, monotonicity, stability) without imposing overly tight constraints on the time step-size as required in prior work.

Method: The authors (1) apply a front-fixing method to convert free boundary problems into fixed boundary problems; (2) discretize the transformed problems using the finite difference method, yielding coefficient matrices that are M-matrices; (3) establish numerical theory for positivity preservation, monotonicity preservation, and stability of the schemes.

Result: Numerical schemes are developed that require only a mild restriction on the time step-size (unlike existing works with tight restrictions), and numerical examples confirm that the schemes perform well and validate the theoretical results.

Conclusion: The proposed front-fixing finite difference schemes provide stable, positivity- and monotonicity-preserving numerical solutions for reaction-diffusion equations with free boundaries under mild time-step conditions.

Abstract: This work considers to numerically solve three kinds of reaction-diffusion equations with free boundaries. First, the popular front-fixing method is used to transform the considered free boundary problems into fixed boundary problems. Then, by employing the finite difference method, numerical schemes with $\mathrm{M}$-matrices as their coefficient matrices are developed for these transformed fixed-boundary problems. Next, for the developed numerical schemes, we establish numerical theory involving positivity preservation, monotonicity preservation, and stability. It is noteworthy that, unlike some existing works that impose tight restrictions on the time step-size to discuss the numerical theory, the proposed numerical schemes require only a mild restriction. Finally, numerical examples are provided to test the developed numerical schemes and to confirm the theoretical results.

</details>


### [48] [A discontinuous finite element method for the hydroelastic analysis of submerged structures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08313)
*Zachary J. Wegert,Oriol Colomés,Michael H. Meylan,Ngamta Thamwattana,Vivien J. Challis*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper presents a finite element method for wave-structure interaction problems involving submerged elastic plates in both 2D and 3D. It combines a symmetric discontinuous Galerkin scheme for the fluid, Dirichlet-to-Neumann-based non-local boundary conditions for radiation, and a continuous/discontinuous Galerkin method for the 4th-order plate operator, with proven well-posedness and validation against a hypersingular boundary integral method.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a robust and flexible numerical framework for hydroelastic modelling of submerged elastic plates, which previously lacked methods capturing potential discontinuities across plates and handling radiation conditions without continuously differentiable finite elements.

Method: The method uses (1) a symmetric discontinuous Galerkin scheme for the fluid to capture potential discontinuities across the plate, (2) Dirichlet-to-Neumann map-inspired analytic boundary conditions in semi-infinite domains for Sommerfeld radiation, and (3) a continuous/discontinuous Galerkin method for the plate's 4th-order operator. Well-posedness is proven via boundedness of the sesquilinear form and Gårding inequality. Implementation is in Julia using the Gridap package.

Result: The method is proven well-posed (unique and stable solution) and validated against a hypersingular boundary integral method with excellent agreement. Demonstrations include 2D slanted plates and 3D geometries (centred/offset rectangular plates, annulus-shaped plates).

Conclusion: The finite element method is general, open-source, and readily applicable to wave energy conversion of piezoelectric bimorphs, extendable to scattering from fixed/rigid structures and variable bottom topography, and not limited to hydroelastic problems.

Abstract: This paper develops a finite element method for wave-structure interaction problems arising in the hydroelastic modelling of submerged elastic plates. The approach is formulated for both two-dimensional settings and three-dimensional channels. For the fluid part of the problem, we use a symmetric discontinuous Galerkin scheme that captures the discontinuity in the potential across the plate. To impose the Sommerfeld radiation conditions, we take inspiration from the Dirichlet-to-Neumann map approach and use the analytic solution in the semi-infinite domains to construct appropriate non-local boundary conditions. For the plate, we use a continuous/discontinuous Galerkin method to resolve the 4$^{\rm th}$ order operator without using continuously differentiable finite elements. We prove well-posedness of the method, showing that the resulting sesquilinear form is bounded and satisfies the Gårding inequality, leading to a unique and stable solution. We implement our formulation as an open-source tool using the Gridap package in Julia. We validate our method against a hypersingular boundary integral method, finding excellent agreement. The flexibility of the methodology is demonstrated through simulations of a two-dimensional slanted plate and three-dimensional plate geometries including centred rectangular plates, offset rectangular plates, and annulus-shaped plates. The finite element method presented here can be readily applied to model wave energy conversion of piezoelectric bimorphs. Furthermore, the method could easily be extended to consider wave scattering from fixed or rigid structures or from variable bottom topography, and is not limited to hydroelastic applications.

</details>


### [49] [Compact implicit high-resolution numerical scheme for multicomponent transport problems with Langmuir sorption](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08320)
*Dagmar Zakova,Peter Frolkovic*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper presents a compact implicit high-resolution finite volume scheme for 1D multicomponent transport with nonlinear Langmuir sorption, combining second-order compact implicit discretization with WENO reconstruction and a time limiter using local characteristic Courant numbers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop an implicit numerical method for multicomponent transport problems with nonlinear sorption that can handle steep gradients and discontinuities while allowing significantly larger Courant numbers than explicit methods, improving computational efficiency.

Method: A second-order compact implicit finite volume scheme is combined with WENO reconstruction for spatial accuracy and a time limiter to handle steep gradients and discontinuities. The limiter uses local characteristic Courant numbers based on eigenvalues of the inverse retardation matrix.

Result: Numerical experiments demonstrate second-order convergence for smooth solutions, and the implicit approach allows significantly larger Courant numbers compared to explicit first and second-order methods. The method was successfully applied to a three-component displacement chromatography problem with sharp concentration fronts.
