TL;DR: This paper uses a computer-assisted proof to show that singularities in the three-dimensional cubic complex Ginzburg-Landau equation can lead to non-uniqueness of solutions after blowup time, driven by an unstable eigenvalue in the linearization around the forward self-similar profile.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The work is motivated by conjectured behavior for the Navier-Stokes equation, since the complex Ginzburg-Landau equation shares key properties with it, such as energy inequality and scaling symmetry. Understanding non-uniqueness in this simpler setting may shed light on analogous questions for Navier-Stokes.

Method: The authors start with backward self-similar solutions and identify an unstable eigenvalue of the linearization around the forward self-similar profile, which is generated by the singularity. The existence of this eigenvalue (and hence non-uniqueness) is established via a computer-assisted proof for specific parameter values.

Result: The paper establishes the existence of the unstable eigenvalue through computer-assisted proof for specific parameter values, demonstrating that singularities developing from smooth initial data can induce non-uniqueness of solutions after the time of blowup.

Conclusion: Singularity formation in the three-dimensional cubic complex Ginzburg-Landau equation can lead to non-unique solutions past blowup time, with potential implications for understanding similar phenomena in the Navier-Stokes equation.

Abstract: We show that singularities of the three-dimensional cubic complex Ginzburg-Landau equation developing from smooth initial data can induce non-uniqueness of solutions after the time of blowup. The solutions we study start as backward self-similar solutions, and the source of the non-uniqueness is an unstable eigenvalue of the linearization around the forward self-similar profile generated by the singularity. We establish the existence of this eigenvalue, and hence the non-uniqueness, by a computer-assisted proof for specific values of the parameters in the equation. The work is in part motivated by similarities to conjectured behavior for the Navier-Stokes equation, with which the complex Ginzburg-Landau equation shares several important properties, such as an energy inequality and the scaling symmetry.

</details>


### [159] [Well-posedness and exponential stability for abstract evolution equations with delay in the nonlinear source: frictional and viscoelastic cases](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08986)
*Houria Chellaoua,Yamna Boukhatem,Cristina Pignotti*

Main category: math.AP

TL;DR: This paper establishes global well-posedness and exponential stability for a class of semilinear neutral abstract evolution equations with constant time delay, considering both frictional damping (CC*u_t) and viscoelastic damping with memory. For sufficiently small initial data, the authors prove existence, uniqueness, and exponential decay of solutions using semigroup theory, energy estimates, Duhamel's formula, and Gronwall-type inequalities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neutral evolution equations with time delay arise in diverse applied fields (structural mechanics, seismic isolation, control theory), yet a unified framework addressing well-posedness and stability for semilinear cases with constant delay was lacking. Establishing such a framework is important for both theoretical understanding and practical applications.

Method: The analysis combines semigroup theory, energy estimates, Duhamel's formula, and Gronwall-type arguments. The authors treat two damping mechanisms—frictional damping of the form CC*u_t and viscoelastic damping with memory—and impose suitable assumptions on the nonlinearities to derive global well-posedness and exponential decay results for small initial data. Concrete examples illustrate the abstract results.

Result: Global well-posedness (existence and uniqueness) and exponential decay of solutions are proven for sufficiently small initial data under both frictional and viscoelastic damping scenarios. The results provide a unified framework applicable to a wide range of neutral systems.

Conclusion: The paper delivers a comprehensive stability and well-posedness analysis for semilinear neutral abstract evolution equations with constant delay, offering a unified theoretical foundation with broad applicability and demonstrated via concrete examples.

Abstract: In this paper, we establish the well-posedness and exponential stability of a class of semilinear neutral abstract evolution equations with constant time delay. Two damping mechanisms are considered: frictional damping of the form $CC^*u_t$ and viscoelastic damping with memory. Under suitable assumptions, we prove global well-posedness and exponential decay of solutions for sufficiently small initial data. This result is of considerable importance, as it provides a unified framework for the analysis of a wide class of neutral systems arising in structural mechanics, seismic isolation, and control theory. The abstract results are illustrated by some concrete examples. The analysis relies on a combination of semigroup theory, energy estimates, Duhamel's formula, and Gronwall-type arguments.

</details>


### [160] [Controllability for 2D water waves: effects of bottom topography and constant vorticity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09014)
*E. Haus,S. Pasquali*

Main category: math.AP

TL;DR: This paper proves controllability results for two-dimensional water waves with gravity and surface tension, covering both irrotational waves in finite-depth domains with general (non-flat) bottom topographies and rotational waves with constant vorticity over flat bottoms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Extending controllability results for water waves beyond the well-studied flat bottom case is challenging because eigenvalues of the Dirichlet-Neumann operator are not explicit for general bottom topographies, nor necessarily close to flat-bottom eigenvalues for low frequencies. This complicates the Ingham-type estimates needed for observability, and the paper aims to address this gap.

Method: The authors prove controllability for irrotational waves over general bottom topographies in an open and dense set of H^{s+1/2}(T) (with s sufficiently large and bottoms not touching the free surface). They develop a more involved argument to establish Ingham-type estimates for observability in this setting. They also handle the case of waves with constant vorticity over a flat bottom.

Result: Controllability is established for irrotational water waves over an open and dense set of admissible non-flat bottom topographies, as well as for constant-vorticity waves over flat bottoms.

Conclusion: The paper extends controllability results for water waves to more general settings (non-flat bottoms and rotational flows), overcoming significant technical obstacles related to eigenvalue properties and observability estimates. The restriction to open and dense bottom topographies is a necessary consequence of these difficulties.

Abstract: In this paper we consider two-dimensional water waves, under the action of gravity and surface tension. We prove a controllability result for irrotational waves in a fluid domain with finite depth and general bottom topography. The result holds for an open and dense set of bottom topographies in $H^{s+1/2}(\mathbb{T})$ (where $s$ is sufficiently large) that do not touch the free surface. We point out that the bottom topographies that we allow for are not necessarily small perturbations of the flat bottom case: this leads to many technical difficulties, since the eigenvalues of the Dirichlet-Neumann operator at a still free surface with general bottom topography are not explicit, nor are they necessarily close (for low frequencies) to the eigenvalues of the corresponding operator for the flat bottom case. In turn, this leads to a more involved argument to prove Ingham-type estimates, which are needed to prove observability, and motivates the restriction mentioned above on the admissible bottom topographies.
  We also prove a controllability result for waves with constant vorticity in a fluid domain with flat bottom topography.

</details>


### [161] [Speed and stability of segregated waves in a pressure-based model of heterogeneous cell populations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09043)
*Carles Falcó,Rebecca M. Crossley,Martina Conte,Tommaso Lorenzi*

Main category: math.AP

TL;DR: This paper analyzes a pressure-based cross-diffusion model of heterogeneous cell populations (proliferative and non-proliferative cells with different mobilities). The authors derive an almost explicit variational bound on the speed of segregated traveling waves by reformulating the problem as a free-boundary problem for a generalized porous-Fisher equation, show that 1D segregated waves are stable only when non-proliferative cells are more mobile, and investigate circular wave instability via asymptotic shape-perturbation analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The model admits 1D traveling wave solutions with strictly segregated components (non-proliferative cells at the leading edge, proliferative cells at the rear), but the wave speed, parameter dependence, and stability of these waves remain poorly understood.

Method: The problem is reformulated as a free-boundary problem for a generalized porous-Fisher equation to derive variational bounds on wave speed. Stability of 1D waves is analyzed, and asymptotic shape-perturbation analysis is used to study the stability of incompressible segregated circular waves.

Result: The estimates apply to general pressure laws and growth kinetics, agree closely with numerical simulations, and become sharp in the incompressible limit (yielding a fully explicit wave speed characterization). Segregated 1D waves are stable only when non-proliferative cells are more mobile. In contrast, circular wave stability is NOT determined solely by relative mobility coefficients, and instabilities may arise regardless of which cell type is more mobile, explaining fingering instability mechanisms.

Conclusion: The findings provide explicit expressions for pressure, interface velocity, and angular mode growth rates, making destabilization mechanisms evident. Notably, circular waves can be unstable irrespective of which cell type has the larger mobility—contrasting sharply with the 1D case—which may lead to fingering instabilities.

Abstract: We consider a minimal pressure-based model of heterogeneous cell populations consisting of proliferative and non-proliferative cells with different mobilities. The model is formulated as a system of reaction--cross--diffusion equations describing the spatio-temporal dynamics of the cell densities. The model is known to admit one-dimensional travelling wave solutions with strictly segregated components: non-proliferative cells occupy a finite region at the leading edge, while proliferative cells remain at the rear. However, the speed, parameter dependence, and stability of these waves remain poorly understood. In this work, we derive an almost explicit variational bound on the wave speed by reformulating the problem as a free-boundary problem for a generalised porous--Fisher equation. The estimates we obtain apply to general pressure laws and growth kinetics, agree closely with the results of numerical simulations, and become sharp in the incompressible limit, where we formally recover a fully explicit characterisation of the wave speed. We then analyse the stability of the waves to show that segregated waves are stable only when non-proliferative cells are more mobile than proliferative cells. Finally, motivated by numerical observations of finger-like protrusions, we investigate the stability of incompressible segregated circular waves through asymptotic shape-perturbation analysis. This yields explicit expressions for the pressure, interface velocity, and growth rates of angular modes, thereby making evident the destabilisation mechanisms that may lead to the emergence of fingering instability. Interestingly, we find that, in contrast with the one-dimensional case, the stability of such circular waves is not determined solely by the relative value of the mobility coefficients, and thus instabilities may arise irrespective of which cell type has the larger mobility.

</details>


### [162] [Pathwise Global-in-Time Existence for the generalised KPZ Equation in the Full Subcritical Regime](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09096)
*Jonas Sauer,Rhys Steele*

Main category: math.AP

TL;DR: This paper provides a pathwise proof of global-in-time well-posedness for the generalised KPZ equation in the full subcritical regime, adapting a strategy recently applied to the generalised Parabolic Anderson Model. The key addition is a treatment of the initial layer for gKPZ with only L∞ initial data (rather than C^θ data), achieved through an expansion around a deterministic profile and an integrating factor to handle critical scaling at time zero.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Extend the recent successful strategy for the generalised Parabolic Anderson Model to the generalised KPZ equation, achieving global-in-time well-posedness in the full subcritical regime. This requires overcoming the challenge that the strategy relies on control of the supremum norm for solution continuation, which demands handling the initial layer with merely L∞ initial data instead of the more standard C^θ initial data.

Method: The proof adapts the strategy from the generalised Parabolic Anderson Model, crucially adding a treatment of the initial layer for gKPZ with L∞ initial data. This is done by expanding the solution around a deterministic profile and introducing an integrating factor to remove terms with critical scaling at time 0. The techniques are first demonstrated on the standard (1+1)-dimensional KPZ equation before tackling the more computationally involved generalised KPZ case.

Result: A pathwise proof of global-in-time well-posedness for the generalised KPZ equation in the full subcritical regime is established, handling the initial layer effectively with merely L∞ initial data rather than requiring smoother C^θ initial conditions.

Conclusion: The paper successfully extends the global well-posedness framework to generalised KPZ by resolving the initial layer issue for rough (L∞) initial data through a deterministic profile expansion combined with an integrating factor technique, presented pedagogically first for standard KPZ.

Abstract: We provide a pathwise proof of global-in-time well-posedness for the generalised KPZ equation in the full subcritical regime by an adaptation of the strategy recently applied to the generalised Parabolic Anderson Model in [ES26]. Since this strategy relies crucially on the assumption that control of the supremum norm is sufficient to continue the solution, the main additional ingredient required is a treatment of the initial layer for gKPZ with merely $L^\infty$ initial data, rather than the more usual setting of $C^θ$ initial data with $θ> 0$. Our approach is based on an expansion around a deterministic profile followed by the introduction of an integrating factor in order to remove the terms which have critical scaling at time $0$. For pedagogical purposes, we first demonstrate the proof techniques in the case of the standard $(1+1)$-dimensional KPZ equation before turning to the more computationally involved case of generalised KPZ.

</details>


### [163] [The Trudinger inequality is true for $M^{1,s}$ spaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09101)
*Ryan Alvarado,Ahmed Dughayshim,Piotr Hajłasz*

Main category: math.AP

TL;DR: The paper proves the sharp Trudinger inequality with optimal exponent s/(s-1) for M^{1,s} Sobolev spaces on metric measure spaces, relying only on a measure growth condition. It also yields sharp asymptotic Sobolev constant bounds and extends Trudinger results to non-connected doubling spaces for p&lt;s, while showing via counterexample that this fails at the critical exponent p=s.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Improve previously known exponential integrability results (with power one) for Trudinger-type inequalities in M^{1,s} Sobolev spaces on metric measure spaces, and determine the sharp/optimal ranges of assumptions (connectivity, doubling, critical exponents) under which such inequalities hold.

Method: The authors establish the Trudinger inequality achieving the sharp exponent s/(s-1) under only a measure lower growth bound μ(B(x,r)) ≥ br^s. They also derive sharp asymptotic bounds for Sobolev constants as p↑s, extend results to non-connected doubling spaces for p&lt;s using a (1,p)-Poincaré inequality, and provide an Ahlfors regular counterexample to demonstrate sharpness at p=s.

Result: Sharp Trudinger inequality with exponent s/(s-1) is proven under the sole measure growth condition, improving prior power-one exponential integrability. Sharp asymptotics for Sobolev constants as p↑s are obtained. On doubling spaces, the connectedness assumption is removed for p&lt;s, but an Ahlfors regular counterexample shows the extension fails at the critical exponent p=s.

Conclusion: The optimal Trudinger exponent is achievable under minimal measure growth assumptions, the results are essentially sharp with respect to assumptions on connectivity and critical exponents, as demonstrated by the counterexample at p=s.

Abstract: We prove the Trudinger inequality with exponent $\frac{s}{s-1}$ for $M^{1,s}$ Sobolev spaces on metric measure spaces under the sole measure growth assumption $μ(B(x,r))\ge br^s$. This improves the previously known exponential integrability with power one. Our argument also yields sharp asymptotic bounds for the Sobolev constants as $p\uparrow s$. On doubling spaces, we further remove the connectedness assumption from the Trudinger inequality associated with a $(1,p)$-Poincaré inequality when $p<s$. An Ahlfors regular counterexample shows that this extension fails at the critical exponent $p=s$.

</details>


### [164] [Differentiability of Lipschitz mappings into metric spaces: area and co-area formulas](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09121)
*Behnam Esmayli,Paweł Goldstein,Piotr Hajłasz*

Main category: math.AP

TL;DR: This paper provides a self-contained treatment of metric differentiability for Lipschitz maps from Euclidean sets into arbitrary metric spaces, along with area and co-area formulas. Its key novelty is a new metric implicit function theorem used to give a direct, geometric proof of the co-area formula for Lipschitz maps into metric spaces.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To offer a self-contained and unified exposition of metric differentiability and the associated area/co-area formulas for Lipschitz mappings from Euclidean domains into arbitrary metric spaces, while addressing the challenge of proving the co-area formula without the standard tools available in Euclidean settings.

Method: The paper develops metric differentiability theory for Lipschitz maps from Euclidean sets into arbitrary metric spaces, and introduces a novel metric implicit function theorem. This theorem is then leveraged to provide a direct and geometric proof of the co-area formula for such mappings.

Result: A new metric implicit function theorem is established, which enables a direct and geometric proof of the co-area formula for Lipschitz mappings into metric spaces, complementing the area formula within a self-contained framework.

Conclusion: The paper presents a complete, self-contained exposition of metric differentiability, area, and co-area formulas for Lipschitz maps from Euclidean sets into arbitrary metric spaces, with the co-area formula proven via a novel geometric approach based on the new metric implicit function theorem.

Abstract: We give a self-contained exposition of metric differentiability for Lipschitz mappings from sets in Euclidean spaces into arbitrary metric spaces, together with the corresponding area and co-area formulas. The novelty is a new metric implicit function theorem for mappings into metric spaces, which is then used to give a direct and geometric proof of the co-area formula for Lipschitz mappings into metric spaces.

</details>

### [165] [Physical-Field Reconstruction from Sparse Observations: When Are Diffusion Models Preferable to Deterministic Regression?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06135)
*Hao Zhou,Rui Zhang,Qi Wang,Hao Sun*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: This paper compares deterministic U-Net, conditional diffusion, and prior-guided diffusion for reconstructing physical fields from sparse observations, finding that accuracy is field- and regime-dependent; ensemble means aid phase alignment while individual samples preserve variability; and conditional diffusion offers cheaper, reliable uncertainty while prior-guided diffusion is more robust to distribution shifts but computationally expensive.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sparse field reconstruction is an ill-posed inverse problem, and there is no clear consensus on when a single point estimate suffices versus when a distribution of plausible reconstructions is more appropriate. A fair, controlled comparison of deterministic and generative approaches is needed.

Method: A matched experimental comparison of three methods—deterministic U-Net, conditional diffusion, and prior-guided diffusion—applied to sparse reconstruction tasks across the 2D Poisson equation, 2D Navier-Stokes flow, and 1D Kuramoto-Sivashinsky dynamics.

Result: Three observations: (1) no systematic accuracy advantage for diffusion under higher complexity or sparser observations; (2) ensemble means improve phase-aligned accuracy while individual samples preserve variability and high-wavenumber power; (3) conditional diffusion provides more reliable uncertainty at lower cost, whereas prior-guided diffusion is more robust to mask-distribution shift but incurs higher inference cost and requires guidance tuning.

Conclusion: The study clarifies when generative reconstruction is beneficial and offers practical guidance on uncertainty estimation, fine-scale sample fidelity, robustness, and computational efficiency for sparse field reconstruction.

Abstract: Reconstructing physical fields from sparse observations is central to system identification, forecasting, and control, yet sparse measurements generally underdetermine the full field. This makes reconstruction an ill-posed inverse problem rather than simple interpolation. Although many deterministic and generative methods have been developed, there is still no clear consensus on when a single point estimate is sufficient and when a distribution of plausible reconstructions is more useful. We conduct a fair comparison of a deterministic U-Net, conditional diffusion, and prior-guided diffusion under matched experimental settings, including 2D Poisson equation, 2D Navier-Stokes flow, and 1D Kuramoto-Sivashinsky dynamics. Through this comparison, we make three observations. First, accuracy is field- and regime-dependent, with no systematic advantage for diffusion under higher complexity or sparser observations. Second, ensemble means improve phase-aligned accuracy, whereas individual samples better preserve variability and can retain high-wavenumber power in selected regimes. Third, conditional diffusion provides more reliable uncertainty estimates at lower cost, while prior-guided diffusion is more robust to mask-distribution shifts but requires substantially higher inference cost and guidance tuning. These results clarify when generative reconstruction is useful and provide guidance for improving uncertainty estimation, fine-scale sample fidelity, robustness, and computational efficiency in sparse field reconstruction.

</details>


### [166] [A Sparsity-Promoting Electromagnetic Inversion Method Regularized by $\ell_1 / \ell_2$-Norm of the Model Gradient](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06524)
*Lingqi Gao,Hakan Bagci*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: A nonlinear electromagnetic (EM) inversion method using ℓ1/ℓ2-norm regularization on the model gradient is proposed. The scale-invariant ratio of ℓ1 to ℓ2 norms provides better sparsity characterization than conventional ℓ1-norm. The nonconvex, nonsmooth problem is solved via ADMM with five sub-steps, incorporating Gauss-Newton for linearization. Numerical tests on synthetic and experimental data show superior permittivity reconstruction—preserving sharp edges, suppressing artifacts—at no extra computational cost, with robustness to noise and reduced sensitivity to regularization weight.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional ℓ1-norm regularization has limitations in accurately characterizing sparsity in EM inversion, motivating the use of the scale-invariant ℓ1/ℓ2-norm ratio for better sparsity representation while addressing the challenges of nonlinearity, nonconvexity, and nonsmoothness.

Method: The inversion problem is reformulated using ℓ1/ℓ2-norm regularization of the model gradient and solved via the alternating direction method of multipliers (ADMM). The optimization algorithm comprises five sub-steps, with the Gauss-Newton method used in the first sub-step to linearize the nonlinear inversion.

Result: Numerical experiments on synthetic and experimental datasets demonstrate that the proposed method outperforms Tikhonov and total variation regularization, yielding superior permittivity reconstructions—particularly in preserving sharp edges and suppressing artifacts—without additional computational cost. The method also shows strong robustness to measurement noise and reduced sensitivity to regularization weight selection.

Conclusion: The ℓ1/ℓ2-norm regularized nonlinear EM inversion method is an efficient and robust alternative to standard regularization approaches, achieving better reconstruction quality with no computational overhead and improved stability under noise and parameter tuning variations.

Abstract: A nonlinear EM inversion method regularized by $\ell_1 / \ell_2$-norm of the model gradient is proposed. The $\ell_1 / \ell_2$-norm, defined as the ratio of $\ell_1$-norm to $\ell_2$-norm, exhibits a scale-invariant property that enables it to more accurately characterize sparsity in the solution compared with the conventional $\ell_1$-norm. To address the nonconvexity and nonsmoothness introduced by the quotient structure of $\ell_1 / \ell_2$-norm, the inversion problem is reformulated and solved using the alternating direction method of multipliers, resulting in an efficient optimization algorithm comprising five sub-steps. The Gauss--Newton method is incorporated into the first sub-step to linearize the nonlinear inversion. The proposed method is validated by a series of numerical examples using both synthetic and experimental datasets, and is compared against standard regularization methods such as Tikhonov and total variation. The results demonstrate that the proposed method yields superior reconstructions of permittivity profiles, particularly in preserving sharp edges and suppressing artifacts, without incurring additional computational cost. Furthermore, the method exhibits strong robustness to measurement noise and reduced sensitivity to the choice of the regularization weight.

</details>


### [167] [A HIP-Compatible Accelerator Backend for Fourier-Bessel Particle-in-Cell Simulations on CPU/DCU Heterogeneous Clusters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06680)
*Jingliang Fan,Ruiqing He,Yang Wan,Jiandong Shang,Hengliang Guo,Qiang Chen*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: This paper ports FBPIC (Fourier-Bessel particle-in-cell), a relativistic plasma simulation code originally using Numba CUDA, to a HIP-compatible backend enabling efficient execution on DCU accelerators while preserving the Python interface. The new backend achieves 1.32-1.54x speedups over original FBPIC on NVIDIA V100 and enables efficient DCU execution. Multi-DCU tests show 1.88x strong-scaling speedup on four accelerators and 2.72x aggregate throughput increase at ~68% weak-scaling efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The original FBPIC code relied on Numba CUDA, limiting its deployment on HIP-based accelerator platforms such as DCU (Deep Computing Unit). The authors aim to extend FBPIC's portability to heterogeneous accelerator environments.

Method: The authors develop an accelerator backend compatible with HIP that allows FBPIC to run on DCU platforms while maintaining the original Python user interface and simulation workflow. They port the code from CUDA to HIP and optimize for DCU hardware, along with communication analysis for multi-accelerator scaling.

Result: The proposed backend achieves 1.32-1.54x speedups over original FBPIC on NVIDIA V100 for LWFA workloads, enables efficient execution on DCU platforms, and demonstrates 1.88x strong-scaling speedup on four DCU accelerators with 2.72x aggregate throughput improvement at ~68% weak-scaling efficiency. Communication analysis identifies inter-node communication and synchronization as the main scalability bottlenecks.

Conclusion: The work provides a practical reference for porting and optimizing Python-based scientific computing applications on heterogeneous accelerator platforms beyond FBPIC, and highlights key lessons learned from DCU porting.

Abstract: FBPIC (Fourier-Bessel particle-in-cell) is a high-performance simulation code for relativistic plasma and accelerator physics. Its original accelerator backend relies on Numba CUDA, which limits its direct deployment on accelerators using the HIP (Heterogeneous-Compute Interface for Portability) programming environment, such as DCU (Deep Computing Unit) accelerators. In this work, we develop an accelerator backend compatible with HIP that enables FBPIC to run efficiently on DCU platforms while preserving its Python user interface and high level simulation workflow. For the evaluated LWFA (laser-wakefield acceleration) workloads, the proposed backend achieves 1.32-1.54x speedups over the original FBPIC implementation on an NVIDIA V100 GPU and enables efficient execution on the DCU platform. We also summarize the key lessons learned from porting FBPIC to the DCU platform. Multi-DCU experiments achieve a 1.88x strong-scaling speedup on four accelerators and a 2.72x increase in aggregate throughput at approximately 68\% weak-scaling efficiency, with communication analysis identifying inter-node communication and synchronization as the main scalability limitations. Beyond FBPIC, the proposed approach provides a practical reference for porting and optimizing other scientific computing applications developed with Python on heterogeneous accelerator platforms.

</details>


### [168] [Beyond Residuals: Energy based solutions of partial differential equations using scientific machine learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08239)
*Timon Rabczuk,Yizheng Wang*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: This paper systematically revisits the Deep Energy Method (DEM), a physics-informed neural network approach that solves PDEs by minimizing energy/incremental potential functionals rather than residual losses. It positions DEM within the broader context of variational modeling, clarifies its relationship to PINNs and VPINNs, and demonstrates through numerical examples that DEM offers advantages in stability, robustness, and interpretability for variational problems—particularly those involving incremental formulations, nonlinearity, history-dependence, and dissipative systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors aim to systematically place DEM in the broader context of physics-informed learning and variational modeling, clarifying when and why energy minimization provides intrinsic advantages over residual-based approaches (PINNs/VPINNs) in terms of stability, robustness, and interpretability.

Method: The paper revisits DEM, highlighting its direct computation of solutions as minimizers of energy or incremental potential functionals—eliminating residual weighting and high-order derivatives. It emphasizes incremental variational formulations to extend DEM to nonlinear, history-dependent, and time-dependent problems (e.g., phase-field fracture, dissipative systems), and illustrates how the variational structure supports optimization and inverse problems with energy as a physics-based constraint.

Result: Through numerical examples, the paper demonstrates that DEM serves as a principled and effective alternative to residual-based methods for variational problems, while also identifying its strengths and limitations relative to existing physics-informed approaches.

Conclusion: DEM provides a physically consistent and stable framework for a wide class of variational PDE problems, particularly those with incremental and dissipative structure, offering distinct advantages over residual-based PINN variants while remaining complementary to them in broader physics-informed learning contexts.

Abstract: Energy-based approaches provide a natural and physically consistent framework for a large class of partial differential equations arising in solid and fluid mechanics, where the governing equations follow from variational principles. In contrast to residual-based physics-informed neural networks (PINNs) and their weak-form variants, which enforce the strong or weak form of the equations through loss minimization, the Deep Energy Method (DEM) directly computes the solution as the minimizer of an energy or incremental potential functional. This eliminates the need for residual weighting, avoids high-order derivatives, and enables the direct enforcement of physical constraints through the variational structure.In this work, we systematically revisit the Deep Energy Method, placing it in the broader context of physics-informed learning and variational modeling. We clarify the relationship between DEM, PINNs, and VPINNs, and identify the class of problems for which energy minimization provides intrinsic advantages in terms of stability, robustness and interpretability. Particular emphasis is placed on incremental variational formulations, which allow DEM to be applied to nonlinear, history-dependent and time-dependent problems, including phase-field fracture and dissipative systems. The variational structure underlying DEM further provides a natural foundation for optimization and inverse problems, where the energy functional acts as a physics-based constraint rather than a residual penalty. Through a series of numerical examples, we demonstrate that DEM offers a principled and effective alternative to residual-based methods for variational problems, highlighting its strengths and limitations relative to existing physics-informed approaches.

</details>


### [169] [A finite-strain logarithmic viscoelastic model for Antarctic ice shelves based on an additive split](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08451)
*Maxime Nutte,Sebastian Skatulla,Carlo Sansour*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: This paper develops a finite-strain Maxwell viscoelastic model for glacier ice using logarithmic (Hencky) strain space, with a Glen-type power-law dashpot, and applies it to idealized ice-shelf front geometries to study how viscoelasticity and morphology control near-front stresses governing calving.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ice shelf mass loss is primarily driven by calving, which is controlled by the near-front stress field spanning both elastic flexure and viscous creep timescales. A formulation capable of capturing this finite-strain viscoelastic behavior is needed to understand stress distributions near ice-shelf fronts.

Method: A finite-strain Maxwell model is formulated in logarithmic (Hencky) strain space, where strain rates split additively into elastic (isotropic Hencky) and viscous (Glen-type power law) parts. The model is integrated with midpoint evaluation and backward-Euler correction of the trial dual, implemented in a finite-element framework, and applied to an idealized ice tongue with depth-dependent density/moduli, temperature-dependent fluidity, and cliff geometries including frontal foot and basal undercutting.

Result: After validating against a viscoelastic column benchmark, the model produces stress fields for idealized ice-shelf fronts showing that viscoelasticity and front morphology (frontal foot, basal undercutting) control tension near the terminus.

Conclusion: The proposed finite-strain Maxwell formulation in logarithmic strain space provides a thermodynamically consistent framework for studying near-front ice-shelf stresses, revealing how viscoelastic relaxation and geometric features modulate the tensile stress state that controls calving.

Abstract: Ice shelves lose mass primarily by calving, a process controlled by the near-front stress field on timescales that span elastic flexure and viscous creep. We formulate a finite-strain Maxwell model for glacier ice in logarithmic strain space. The Hencky strain of a fixed reference configuration is split additively at the level of rates into elastic and viscous parts; the spring is isotropic Hencky elasticity and the dashpot is a Glen-type power law written on the logarithmic strain rate and its work-conjugate stress. At infinitesimal strain the dashpot coincides with Glen's flow law; the elastic strains in the ice-shelf configurations of this paper remain in that regime. The model is integrated with a midpoint evaluation and a backward-Euler correction of the trial dual, and implemented in a finite-element setting. After a viscoelastic column benchmark, the formulation is applied to an idealised ice tongue, including depth-dependent density and moduli, temperature-dependent fluidity, and cliff geometries with a frontal foot or basal undercutting. The resulting stress fields show how viscoelasticity and front morphology control tension near the terminus.

</details>


### [170] [Structure-Informed Bayesian Inference of Anomalous Transport and Hidden Molecular Trapping in Amorphous Media](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08780)
*Andrey Ananev,Maria Potapova,Nikolay Kondratyuk,Timur Vostroknutov,Aleksey Khlyupin*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: The paper presents a structure-informed Bayesian regularization method for extracting localized trapping states from single-particle tracking trajectories in fluctuating amorphous media, leveraging discrete Morse theory to build physical priors from the host matrix's topological skeleton.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Extracting localized trapping states from single-particle tracking trajectories in fluctuating media is fundamentally challenging because thermal structural breathing continuously reconfigures pore boundaries, causing conventional geometric algorithms to systematically merge distinct localized states during cyclic molecular returns.

Method: The method shifts from local geometric recurrence to structure-informed Bayesian regularization. It uses discrete Morse theory to extract the time-invariant topological skeleton of the fluctuating host matrix, constructing gas-specific physical priors that account for molecular dimensions. Trajectory steps are then partitioned via a two-stage probabilistic refinement that adapts dynamically to the transport landscape.

Result: When benchmarked against synthetic particles exploring the actual interconnected matrix graph, the approach eliminates systematic biases with macroscopic trapping parameter deviations at only a few percent under optimal linear O(N) computational scaling. Applied to hydrogen and methane transport in type-I kerogen, it successfully decodes hidden microscopic mechanisms of confined diffusion.

Conclusion: The method offers an accessible, open-source strategy adaptable to a broad spectrum of tracking phenomena, from ion transport in battery polymers to protein trafficking in cellular environments, with immediate broad impact potential.

Abstract: Molecular diffusion in fluctuating amorphous and macromolecular media governs key transport processes across soft-matter physics, energy storage, and biological membranes. Extracting localized trapping states from single-particle tracking trajectories remains a fundamental challenge; because thermal structural breathing continuously reconfigures pore boundaries, conventional geometric algorithms suffer from severe systematic biases, erroneously merging distinct localized states during cyclic molecular returns. Here, we address this deadlock by shifting the paradigm from local geometric recurrence to a structure-informed Bayesian regularization. Leveraging discrete Morse theory, we extract the time-invariant topological skeleton of the fluctuating host matrix to construct robust, gas-specific physical priors that account for individual molecular dimensions. Trajectory steps are sequentially partitioned via a two-stage probabilistic refinement that dynamically adapts to the transport landscape. Benchmarked against a rigorous environment where synthetic particles explore the actual interconnected matrix graph, our approach eliminates systemic biases, restricting macroscopic trapping parameter deviations to just a few percent under optimal linear $O(N)$ computational scaling. Applied to hydrogen and methane transport within a type-I kerogen matrix, serving as a prototype for highly tortuous, flexible macromolecular networks, the method successfully decodes the hidden microscopic mechanisms of confined diffusion. To ensure immediate broad impact, the documented open-source code and data are made publicly available, offering an accessible strategy readily adaptable to a broad spectrum of tracking phenomena, from ion transport in battery polymers to protein trafficking within cellular environments.

</details>


### [171] [The Path Integral Monte Carlo Sign Problem Is Not Always NP-Hard: Harmonic Fermions Can Be Solved in Quadratic Time](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09071)
*Aarif Chaudhary,Jonas Valenzuela,Siu A. Chin*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: The paper derives an exact closed-form finite-bead partition function for fermionic Path Integral Monte Carlo (PIMC) in harmonic traps using a λ-ring reformulation of the recursion relation. This enables O(n²) evaluation for up to 10⁴+ fermions, completely bypassing the numerical sign problem and countering the claim that all fermionic PIMC sign problems are NP-hard.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fermionic PIMC simulations suffer from the sign problem, where the fermionic antisymmetry causes the partition function to oscillate between positive and negative terms, leading to severe numerical instability. There is a prevailing belief that all truly fermionic PIMC sign problems are NP-hard. This work aims to find a case (harmonically trapped fermions) where the sign problem can be completely circumvented analytically.

Method: The authors reformulate the recursion relation for finite-bead PIMC partition functions (obtained from contracted determinant form of the propagator) in the λ-ring language. This yields a closed-form finite-bead partition function in two dimensions expressed in terms of permutation statistics. A specialized algorithm evaluates this in O(n²) time with numerical stability.

Result: The closed-form partition function enables exact and numerically stable reproduction of fermion PIMC energies for systems of n=10⁴ or more fermions, effectively bypassing the sign problem entirely for this class of problems.

Conclusion: This work provides a concrete framework where the numerical instability of the fermionic sign problem is completely circumvented, serving as a counterexample to the claim that all truly fermionic PIMC sign problems are NP-hard.

Abstract: In the Path Integral Monte Carlo (PIMC) simulation of fermions in a harmonic trap, with and without pairwise harmonic interactions, the partition functions for any discrete number of imaginary time slices (or beads) and for any choice of the short-time propagator can be analytically obtained from the contracted determinant form of the propagator. This work shows that the resulting recursion relation can be reformulated in the $λ$-ring language, yielding a closed-form finite-bead partition function in two dimensions in terms of permutation statistics. This closed-form partition function can be evaluated by a special algorithm in $O(n^2)$ time, providing an exact and numerically stable scheme for reproducing the energies of the original (undoable) fermion PIMC simulation for $n=10^4$ or more fermions. This result provides a concrete framework in which the numerical instability of the sign problem is completely bypassed, and serves as a counterexample to the prevailing view that all truly fermionic PIMC sign problems are NP-hard.

</details>
