# physics.plasm-ph [[Back]](#toc)

### [172] [A Minimal Self-Consistent Model for the Nonlinear Dynamics of Asymmetric Capacitively Coupled Radio-Frequency Plasmas](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05687)
*Thomas Mussenbrock*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: The paper develops a minimal lumped-element model for nonlinear radio-frequency dynamics in geometrically asymmetric capacitively coupled plasmas, coupling fast electrical dynamics (sheath charge, blocking-capacitor voltage, discharge current) to stationary particle/energy balances to explain nonlinear current oscillations and plasma series resonance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To capture the nonlinear RF dynamics and plasma series resonance (PSR) in capacitively coupled plasmas within a minimal self-consistent framework, rather than relying on complex full physics models.

Method: A self-consistent lumped-element model with three dynamical variables describing the fast electrical response is coupled to stationary particle and energy balance equations. Linearization about the periodic state gives the high-frequency response via time-dependent differential sheath elastance, and a moment-based effective elastance estimates the PSR frequency.

Result: The model produces nonlinear current oscillations, a broad higher-harmonic response from plasma series resonance, and correlated changes in harmonic spectrum, power absorption, and plasma density with RF voltage variation.

Conclusion: The model successfully bridges nonlinear RF circuit dynamics and plasma sustainment in a compact self-consistent description, providing a minimal but complete framework for asymmetric CCP systems.

Abstract: We develop a minimal self-consistent lumped-element model for the nonlinear radio-frequency dynamics of geometrically asymmetric capacitively coupled plasmas. The fast electrical response is described by three dynamical variables: the powered-sheath charge, the blocking-capacitor voltage, and the discharge current. The periodic RF solution is coupled to stationary particle and energy balances, which determine the electron temperature and plasma density from the operating conditions and the absorbed power. For an argon discharge, the model produces nonlinear current oscillations and a broad higher-harmonic response associated with the plasma series resonance. Variation of the RF voltage leads to correlated changes in the harmonic spectrum, absorbed power, and plasma density. Linearization about the periodic state relates the high-frequency response to the time-dependent differential sheath elastance. A moment-based effective elastance provides a compact estimate of the corresponding characteristic PSR frequency. The model thereby connects nonlinear RF dynamics and plasma sustainment within a minimal self-consistent description.

</details>


### [173] [Novel approach to general curvilinear coordinates for plasma fluid applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05712)
*Federico D. Halpern Tess N. Bernard,Oleksandr Koshkarov,Ronald E. Waltz*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: This paper presents a reformulation of plasma fluid equations that preserves geometry and conservation properties (mass, angular momentum, energy) in numerical simulations by concealing geometric sources. The approach requires only minimal discrete-space conditions—anti-symmetry of the first derivative and orthogonality of scalar/cross products—and is validated on resistive MHD using liquid metal flows and the Orszag-Tang vortex.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Plasma fluid equations in general geometry contain nonlinear geometric sources from fictitious forces that create significant numerical simulation challenges. The authors aim to reformulate the hierarchy to rigorously preserve geometry and conservation properties critical to numerical simulations.

Method: The authors reformulate the plasma fluid hierarchy to hide geometric sources while rigorously preserving geometry and conservation laws. In discrete form, conservation of mass, angular momentum, and energy emerges naturally by analogy with continuum equations, requiring only anti-symmetry of the first derivative operator and orthogonality of scalar and cross products. The formulation decouples magnetic geometry, coordinate systems, and numerical discretization.

Result: The novel representation is validated on the resistive magnetohydrodynamic (MHD) system—chosen as a testbed because it involves a complete set of curvilinear operations. Correctness is verified using steady-state liquid metal flows and the classic Orszag-Tang vortex test.

Conclusion: The reformulated plasma fluid models enable maximum numerical flexibility while preserving physics fidelity by naturally conserving mass, angular momentum, and energy in discrete form. The approach successfully handles full curvilinear operations as demonstrated through resistive MHD validation cases.

Abstract: In general geometry, plasma fluid equations include nonlinear geometric sources associated with fictitious forces, which pose significant challenges to computer simulations. We reformulate the plasma fluid hierarchy to rigorously preserve geometry and conservation properties critical to numerical simulations, while concealing the geometric sources. In their discrete form, the reformulated models conserve mass, angular momentum, and energy naturally, by simple analogy with the continuum equations. These conservation properties have minimal requirements in discrete space, namely, the anti-symmetry of the first derivative and the orthogonality of the scalar and cross products. By decoupling magnetic geometry, coordinate systems, and numerical discretization, this enables maximum flexibility while preserving physics fidelity. As a testbed, we apply the novel representation to the resistive magnetohydrodynamic system, which involves a complete set of curvilinear operations. We verify the correctness of the approach using steady state liquid metal flows and the classic Orszag-Tang vortex.

</details>


### [174] [Experimental realization of a dusty plasma rocking ratchet with current reversal](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06321)
*Shun-xin Zhang,Shuo Wang,Ting-yu Yao,Yong-liang Zhang,Bao-quan Ai,Ya-feng He*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: This paper experimentally studies transport dynamics of a dust particle in an underdamped dusty plasma rocking ratchet, driven by two periodic laser beams. The particle exhibits positive, zero, or negative net currents with current reversals at critical driving thresholds. A simplified model based on competition between driving force and ratchet confinement explains the behavior, revealing two requirements for directional transport: the driving force must exceed the depinning threshold, and the driving semicycle must be longer than the uphill escape time. The dynamic phase diagram quantitatively reproduces experimental results.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand nonequilibrium transport phenomena of underdamped particles in rocking ratchets, and to establish dusty plasma as a versatile experimental platform for such investigations.

Method: A single dust particle is confined in an asymmetric ratchet potential and driven periodically by two oppositely directed laser beams. Transport dynamics are experimentally measured under varying driving amplitudes and frequencies. A simplified analytical model is developed based on the competition between the driving force and ratchet confinement, incorporating depinning threshold and uphill escape time criteria to construct a dynamic phase diagram.

Result: The particle shows positive, zero, or negative net currents depending on driving conditions, with current reversals observed when driving parameters cross critical thresholds. The dynamic phase diagram from the model quantitatively reproduces the experimentally observed transport regimes and current reversals.

Conclusion: The work demonstrates that dusty plasma systems serve as a versatile platform for studying nonequilibrium transport of underdamped particles in rocking ratchets, with the model successfully capturing the underlying physics governing directional transport and current reversals.

Abstract: A single dust particle confined in an asymmetric ratchet potential is periodically driven by two oppositely directed laser beams, forming an underdamped dusty plasma rocking ratchet. We experimentally investigate the transport dynamics of the particle under varying driving amplitudes and frequencies. Depending on the driving conditions, the particle exhibits positive, zero, or negative net currents, and current reversal is observed when the driving parameters cross critical thresholds. To interpret these transport behaviors, we develop a simplified model based on the competition between the driving force and the ratchet confinement. The model reveals that directional transport is governed by two requirements: the driving force must exceed the depinning threshold, and the duration of a driving semicycle must be longer than the uphill escape time from a ratchet well. The resulting dynamic phase diagram quantitatively reproduces the experimentally observed transport regimes and current reversals. These results demonstrate dusty plasma as a versatile platform for investigating nonequilibrium transport phenomena of underdamped particles in rocking ratchets.

</details>


### [175] [Separation of bi-dispersed microspheres in dusty plasma ratchet experiments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06325)
*Ting-yu Yao,Ji-xu Gao,Miao Tian,Shun-xin Zhang,Fu-cheng Liu,Bao-quan Ai,Ya-feng He*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: This paper experimentally demonstrates effective separation of bi-dispersed microspheres (dust particles) in an underdamped, strongly-coupled regime using a designed dusty plasma ratchet, where particles exhibit directional transport at different speeds or opposite directions depending on discharge conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To achieve effective separation of bi-dispersed dust particles in a dusty plasma ratchet operating in the underdamped and strongly-coupled regime, and to understand the underlying transport mechanisms.

Method: Experimental realization of a dusty plasma ratchet for particle separation, combined with fluid simulations of a capacitively coupled Argon discharge to model the plasma environment and ratchet potentials experienced by dust particles of different sizes.

Result: Bi-dispersed dust particles undergo directional transport at varying speeds or even in opposite directions depending on discharge conditions, enabling successful separation. Simulations show the particles are suspended at different balance heights in the plasma sheath and experience distinct ratchet potentials, leading to varied flow velocities.

Conclusion: The discovery of height-dependent transport of dust particles provides fundamental insights into transport of underdamped strongly-coupled particles in dusty plasma ratchets.

Abstract: It is demonstrated experimentally that the effective separation of bi-dispersed microspheres (dust particles) in the underdamped and strongly-coupled regime is realized using a designed dusty plasma ratchet. Experimental findings reveal that these dust particles can undergo directional transport at varying speeds, even moving in opposite directions depending on the discharge conditions, enabling successful particle separation. Numerical simulations of the plasma environment surrounding the dust particles are performed using fluid simulations of the capacitively coupled discharge of Argon. The simulation results indicate that the bi-dispersed dust particles are suspended at different balance heights within the plasma sheath and experience distinct ratchet potentials that govern their directional transport, resulting in varied flow velocities. The discovery of height-dependent transport of dust particles here provides insights of transport fundamental of underdamped strongly-coupled particles in dusty plasma ratchets.

</details>


### [176] [Magnetic island structures in relativistic laser-driven plasma channels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06562)
*Dongchi Cai,Zheng Gong,Guanqi Qiu,Deji Liu,Yinren Shou,Xueqing Yan*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: This paper develops a theoretical model describing self-generated magnetic islands in relativistic laser-driven channels within near-critical-density plasmas, deriving scaling laws for island geometry (transverse width H and longitudinal period L) that are validated by particle-in-cell simulations across broad parameter ranges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Magnetic island structures in relativistic laser-plasma channels have been observed qualitatively but lack a predictive theoretical framework. Understanding their formation and geometry is essential for applications in electron transport, particle acceleration, radiation generation, and fusion ignition schemes.

Method: The authors construct an analytical model based on the nonlinear superposition of quasi-static magnetic fields from longitudinal channel current (j_x) and transverse laser-front driven current (j_y). They derive critical conditions linking laser depletion, symmetric channel formation, and magnetic-island formation to identify the parameter window where islands exist. Within this window, they relate island transverse width H to the balance of ponderomotive and charge-separation forces (via effective electron density n_eff), and longitudinal period L to the mismatch between laser group and phase velocities. Validation is performed via large-scale particle-in-cell (PIC) simulations over varied laser intensities and plasma densities.

Result: The model successfully identifies the laser-plasma parameter window for magnetic-island existence and provides validated scaling laws for both transverse width H and longitudinal period L against PIC simulation results over a broad parameter range.

Conclusion: The model transforms magnetic-island geometry from a qualitative observation into a quantitatively predictable quantity, establishing a foundation for engineering electron transport, particle acceleration, high-energy radiation, and novel fusion ignition approaches in relativistic laser-plasma interactions.

Abstract: We develop a theoretical model for self-generated magnetic islands in relativistic laser-driven channels in near-critical-density plasmas. The islands arise from the nonlinear superposition of the quasi-static magnetic fields generated by the longitudinal channel current $j_x$ and the laser-front driven transverse current $j_y$. By deriving the critical conditions among laser depletion, transversely symmetric channel formation, and magnetic-island formation, we identify the laser-plasma parameter window in which the magnetic island structures can exist. Within this window, the balance between the laser ponderomotive force and the charge-separation force, expressed through an effec tive electron density $n_\mathrm{eff}$, determines the transverse island width $H$, whereas the mismatch between the laser group and phase velocities determines the longitudinal period $L$. Large-scale particle-in-cell simulations over a broad range of laser intensities and plasma densities validate the resulting scaling laws. The model turns the island geometry from a qualitative feature of the channel field into a predictable quantity, providing a basis for tailoring electron transport, particle acceleration, high-energy radiation, and novel fusion ignition schemes in relativistic laser-plasma interactions.

</details>


### [177] [Particle pinch in global tokamak edge simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06744)
*Ben Zhu*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: Using the flux-driven global turbulence model GDB, this paper shows that a strong inward particle pinch can self-consistently build a centrally peaked density profile in the tokamak edge from a flat initial state. Two mechanisms operate: early-stage drift-wave turbulence driving inward flux via electron thermal diffusion when density and temperature gradients oppose each other, and a late-stage persistent inward equilibrium E×B flux carried by poloidally asymmetric density and potential, opened by non-ideal effects (parallel resistivity, electron inertia, electromagnetic induction) in the electron force balance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The inward particle flux (particle pinch) is routinely observed in magnetically confined fusion experiments, but its underlying mechanism is not fully understood—particularly in the tokamak edge where density pedestal formation remains an open question.

Method: The authors employ the flux-driven global turbulence model GDB, simulating a tokamak edge starting from a flat density profile fueled only near the last closed flux surface. They analyze the inward particle flux through two distinct phases and channels, examining the roles of drift-wave turbulence, electron thermal diffusion, equilibrium E×B fluxes, poloidally asymmetric density/potential structures, and non-ideal effects in the electron force balance.

Result: The simulation develops a strong inward particle flux building a centrally peaked density profile over ~O(10) ms. Two mechanisms carry the pinch: (1) early drift-wave turbulence drives inward flux through electron thermal diffusion under opposing density and temperature gradients (ηα<0); (2) in the late stage, a persistent inward equilibrium E×B flux, carried by poloidally asymmetric density and electrostatic potential, drives central peaking. This flux flows through a non-ideal channel (parallel resistivity, electron inertia, electromagnetic induction) that shifts the potential poloidally against the density by δs≈−0.08π.

Conclusion: A flux-driven global edge simulation can self-consistently produce a centrally peaked density profile without ad hoc assumptions, and these findings bear on longstanding edge questions such as density pedestal formation.

Abstract: The inward particle flux, or particle pinch, is routinely observed in magnetically confined fusion experiments, yet its mechanism is not fully understood. We study the particle pinch in the tokamak edge with the flux-driven global turbulence model GDB. Starting from a flat density profile fueled only near the last closed flux surface, the simulation develops a strong inward particle flux that builds a centrally peaked density profile over $O(10)$ ms. The pinch coexists with the usual outward turbulent heat transport; two distinct mechanisms carry it. In the early stage, when density and temperature gradients oppose each other ($η_α=L_n/L_{T_α}<0$), drift-wave turbulence drives the inward flux through electron thermal diffusion. In the late stage, once the density profile has flattened, a persistent inward equilibrium $E\times B$ flux, carried by the poloidally asymmetric density and electrostatic potential, drives the central peaking. While the Pfirsch--Schlüter neoclassical transport sets the amplitude of the up-down asymmetric density, classical theory predicts no net radial flux from this asymmetry at leading order. The observed flux flows in a separate non-ideal channel, opened by parallel resistivity, electron inertia, and electromagnetic induction in the equilibrium electron force balance. This channel shifts the equilibrium potential poloidally against the density by $δ_s\simeq-0.08π$ and supplies the late-phase density build-up. These results show that a flux-driven global edge simulation can self-consistently produce a centrally peaked density profile without ad hoc assumptions, and bear on longstanding edge questions such as density pedestal formation.

</details>


### [178] [Electromagnetic filament coalescence as magnetic island merging with diamagnetic effects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07104)
*Souvik Mondal,N Bisai,Abhijit Sen,Indranil Bandyopadhyay*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: This paper uses a 3D electromagnetic fluid model to study the nonlinear coalescence of two current-carrying ELM filaments. It finds magnetic island-like reconnection in the flat-density limit, and shows that blob-like density perturbations reduce and delay reconnection while enhancing the post-compression sloshing of the filaments, identifying density perturbation as a control parameter for edge plasma dynamics in tokamaks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the coupled electromagnetic and pressure-driven dynamics governing the nonlinear evolution of ELM filaments in the tokamak edge, specifically how density perturbations affect filament coalescence and reconnection.

Method: Three-dimensional electromagnetic fluid model simulation of two current-carrying ELM filaments undergoing nonlinear coalescence, with parametric study of blob-like density perturbation amplitudes. Analysis uses the induction equation to distinguish resistive versus advective dynamics.

Result: Flat-density coalescence shows X-point formation, current-sheet development, and Sweet-Parker-like resistive scaling. Weak density perturbations leave the peak reconnection rate nearly unchanged, but larger amplitudes decrease and delay reconnection. Dynamics transition from resistive to density-dependent advective behavior, and finite density perturbations enhance post-compression sloshing, whose amplitude scales with density-gradient pressure force.

Conclusion: Density perturbation acts as an additional control parameter for both reconnection and filament sloshing in ELM filament coalescence, highlighting the important coupling between electromagnetic reconnection and pressure-driven dynamics in the tokamak edge.

Abstract: We investigate the nonlinear coalescence of two current-carrying ELM filaments using a three-dimensional electromagnetic fluid model. In the flat-density limit, the coalescence exhibits magnetic island-like reconnection, characterized by X-point formation, current-sheet development, and Sweet-Parker-like resistive scaling. Introducing a blob-like density perturbation modifies the reconnection dynamics: while the peak reconnection rate remains nearly unchanged for weak perturbations, it decreases and is increasingly delayed for larger density amplitudes. Analysis of the induction equation reveals a transition from resistive to increasingly density-dependent advective dynamics. Finite density perturbations also enhance the post-compression rebound, or sloshing, of the filaments. The sloshing amplitude increases with the density-gradient pressure force, establishing density perturbation as an additional control parameter for both reconnection and filament sloshing. These results highlight the coupled electromagnetic and pressure-driven dynamics governing the nonlinear evolution of ELM filaments in the tokamak edge.

</details>


### [179] [Dual-decomposition multi-GPU particle-in-cell method for cylindrical plasmas: Batched Fourier-mode multigrid and balanced particle slabs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07172)
*Yinjian Zhao,Xi Chen,Yingjie Chen*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: This paper presents a dual-decomposition multi-GPU method for 3D electrostatic PIC simulations, where azimuthal Fourier modes are owned during the field solve while spatial slabs own particles, achieving efficient parallelization on GPUs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 3D electrostatic PIC simulations combine irregular particle operations with a globally coupled Poisson solve, and their preferred parallel decompositions conflict, requiring an approach that balances both workloads efficiently across multiple GPUs.

Method: The method uses a dual-decomposition approach: complete azimuthal Fourier modes are owned during the field solve, while spatial slabs own particles. It employs exact full-spectrum diagonalization and batched matrix-free geometric multigrid to keep collective operations outside the V-cycle, after which every GPU reconstructs the full field. Peer migration, cell reordering, warp-aggregated deposition, and capacity-constrained dynamic cuts restore particle locality and balance.

Result: CPU/GPU Poisson solutions agree to relative L2 errors below 6.9e-16; all 65 physical modes on eight V100 GPUs are solved in 9.350 ms with relative errors below 8.2e-15. For a 512*128*400 problem with 707,788,800 particles per species, the PIC loop reaches 0.149108 s per step and strong-scales from five to eight GPUs with 91.81% efficiency. A cross-resolution study shows approximately threefold aggregate throughput relative to a single RTX 5090.

Conclusion: The dual-decomposition multi-GPU approach effectively resolves the decomposition conflict between particle operations and field solves, achieving high accuracy and strong scalability on multiple GPUs for large-scale 3D electrostatic PIC simulations.

Abstract: Three-dimensional electrostatic particle-in-cell (PIC) simulations combine irregular particle operations with a globally coupled Poisson solve, whose preferred parallel decompositions conflict. We introduce a dual-decomposition multi-GPU method: complete azimuthal Fourier modes are owned during the field solve, whereas spatial slabs own particles. Exact full-spectrum diagonalization and batched matrix-free geometric multigrid keep collectives outside the V-cycle, after which every GPU reconstructs the full field. Peer migration, cell reordering, warp-aggregated deposition, and capacity-constrained dynamic cuts restore particle locality and balance. CPU/GPU Poisson solutions agree to relative L2 errors below 6.9e-16; all 65 physical modes on eight V100 GPUs are solved in 9.350 ms with relative errors below 8.2e-15. For an identical 512*128*400 problem containing 707,788,800 particles per species, the complete PIC loop reaches 0.149108 s per step and strong-scales from five to eight GPUs with 91.81% efficiency. A separate cross-resolution production study shows approximately threefold aggregate particle-update and Poisson-cell throughput relative to a single RTX 5090 calculation; this measures refined-problem capability rather than a hardware speedup or formal convergence order.

</details>


### [180] [Self-organized positron reorienting and pinching mechanism for the experimental detection of the linear Breit-Wheeler process](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07584)
*Yutong He,Alexey Arefiev,Mario Manuel,Hui Chen,Christopher Ridgers*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: This paper proposes a simple experimental setup to observe the linear Breit-Wheeler process using real photons, which enhances positron signals by 2-3 orders of magnitude (reaching ~10^6 MeV^-1 str^-1) compared to previous schemes, achieved by a self-organized positron reorienting and pinching mechanism from in-situ plasma fields.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The linear Breit-Wheeler process is a fundamental QED prediction yet to be experimentally observed with real photons. Previous laser-plasma-based schemes suffer from high background noise (low signal-to-noise ratio), hindering the first-ever detection.

Method: The authors present a simple experimental setup using intense laser-plasma interactions, exploiting a newly discovered self-organized positron reorienting and pinching mechanism enabled by in-situ strong plasma fields. This mechanism redirects positrons opposite to laser propagation where background is quieter.

Result: The proposed scheme enhances expected positron signals by 2-3 orders of magnitude compared to previously proposed schemes, reaching 10^6 MeV^-1 str^-1, while achieving the signal in a direction (opposite to laser propagation) with significantly lower background.

Conclusion: The proposed experimental design could enable the first-ever experimental detection of the linear Breit-Wheeler process using real photons by dramatically improving both signal strength and signal-to-noise ratio.

Abstract: The linear Breit-Wheeler (LBW) process ($γ+γ\rightarrow e^{-}+e^{+}$) is a fundamental prediction of quantum electrodynamics, but yet to be observed under laboratory conditions using real photons. In recent years, a few experimental schemes utilizing high-intense ($\sim10^{22}$W/cm$^2$) laser-plasma interactions to observe the LBW process have been proposed. However, a high level of signal-to-noise-ratio are expected in these schemes, hindering the first-ever experimental detection of the LBW process by real photons. In this paper, we present a simple experimental setup which could enhance the expected positron signals by 2-3 orders of magnitude compared to previously proposed schemes, reaching the level of $10^{6}$MeV$^{-1}$str$^{-1}$. Moreover, such high positron signal is achieve in the direction opposite to the laser propagation, where a significantly quieter background is expected compared to the previously focused direction of laser propagation. The key to achieve this result is a newly discovered self-organized positron reorienting and pinching mechanism, enabled by the in-situ strong plasma fields from the laser-plasma interaction.

</details>


### [181] [Thresholdless IBW Emission and Alpha-to-Thermal-Ion Energy Channeling via Pole Resonance of Fusion-Product Ions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07732)
*Hong Qin,Nathaniel J. Fisch*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: This paper demonstrates that a thermal background plasma can enable a new instability mechanism where the cyclotron pole of minority energetic ions (e.g., fusion-product alphas) destabilizes a stable ion Bernstein wave eigenmode, enabling thresholdless root-pole resonance and providing a linear mechanism for alpha-particle energy channeling in fusion plasmas.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fusion reactions produce energetic ions that can drive Dory-Guest-Harris (DGH) instabilities, but these require finite energetic-ion density and can be stabilized by thermal background plasma. The authors investigate whether the thermal background can instead enable a distinct instability mechanism for transferring fusion-product ion energy.

Method: The authors analyze the dispersion relation where the cyclotron pole of the minority energetic-ion susceptibility destabilizes a stable ion Bernstein wave (IBW) eigenmode. They derive conditions for exact root-pole resonance and perform species-resolved power balance calculations to trace energy flow between energetic ions and thermal plasma.

Result: When the energetic-ion harmonic is distinct from thermal-ion harmonics, exact root-pole resonance is thresholdless in the ideal collisionless limit. Species-resolved power balance shows energetic ions supply free energy while thermal plasma receives it. In the LAPD proton-alpha example, 99.96% of alpha-particle power loss enters the coherent proton response.

Conclusion: This self-excited, ion-directed energy transfer provides a possible linear building block for alpha-particle energy channeling in proton-Boron11 fusion, where aneutronic fusion reactions release nearly all energy as kinetic energy of fusion-product ions.

Abstract: Fusion reactions can release a substantial fraction, and in aneutronic reactions nearly all, of their energy as the kinetic energy of fusion-product ions. In magnetized plasmas, these energetic ions can form ring distributions in velocity space. Such distributions can drive Dory-Guest-Harris (DGH) electrostatic instabilities, but these self-instabilities require a finite energetic-ion density and can be stabilized by a thermal background. We show that the same background can instead enable a distinct instability mechanism: a cyclotron pole of the minority energetic-ion susceptibility destabilizes a stable ion Bernstein wave (IBW) eigenmode. When the energetic-ion harmonic is distinct from the thermal-ion harmonics, exact root-pole resonance is thresholdless in the ideal collisionless limit. A species-resolved power balance shows that the energetic ions supply the free energy while the thermal plasma receives it. In the LAPD proton--alpha example, $99.96\%$ of the alpha-particle power loss enters the coherent proton response. This self-excited, ion-directed transfer provides a possible linear building block for alpha-particle energy channeling in proton-Boron11 fusion.

</details>


### [182] [Hamiltonian Particle Dynamics in Fusion Plasmas: Orbital Tomography and Spectrum Analysis for Energy and Momentum Transport under Resonant Non-Axisymmetric Perturbations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07759)
*Yiannis Antonenas*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: This thesis develops analytical and numerical methods based on Action-Angle variables and drift-center approximations to analyze resonant mode-particle interactions in toroidal fusion plasmas, identifying resonance locations, transport barriers, and Arnold diffusion in guiding-center phase space, with applications to both idealized and realistic equilibria.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mode-particle resonant interactions can strongly influence particle, momentum, and energy transport in toroidal fusion plasmas, affecting confinement. There is a need for computationally efficient tools to predict where these resonances occur and how they impact transport, especially under non-axisymmetric perturbations.

Method: The work uses (1) Action-Angle variables for efficient resonance identification in guiding-center phase space, characterising resonance chains and transport barriers; (2) drift center (DC) approximation with analytical frequency and kinetic q-factor expressions for large-aspect-ratio equilibria; (3) extension of orbital-frequency analysis to realistic reconstructed equilibria via a semi-analytical geometrical approach; (4) extension to time-dependent perturbations to study Arnold diffusion in LAR equilibria.

Result: Analytical expressions are validated against numerical simulations, demonstrating an efficient predictive tool for transport barriers and energetic-particle behavior under non-axisymmetric perturbations. The method extends successfully to realistic equilibria and time-dependent cases, revealing Arnold diffusion and the role of the Arnold web in particle transport.

Conclusion: The work advances understanding of mode-particle resonant interactions and provides computationally efficient predictive tools for transport and confinement in realistic fusion configurations.

Abstract: This thesis investigates the impact of resonant mode-particle interactions on transport and confinement in toroidal fusion plasmas. Using analytical and numerical approaches, we study how intrinsic and externally applied magnetic perturbations affect plasma particles with different kinetic characteristics, focusing on resonant interactions with guiding-center motion.
  We develop a novel, computationally efficient method based on Action-Angle variables to identify resonance locations and characterize resonances in guiding-center phase space, including the number of islands in resonance chains and the formation of transport barriers. Using the drift center (DC) approximation, we derive analytical expressions for orbital frequencies and the kinetic $q$ factor for large-aspect-ratio (LAR) equilibria. These results provide the conditions for mode-particle resonances, which can strongly influence particle, momentum, and energy transport and, consequently, plasma confinement. The analytical results are validated against numerical simulations, demonstrating an efficient tool for predicting transport barriers and energetic-particle behavior under non-axisymmetric perturbations.
  We further extend the orbital-frequency analysis from LAR equilibria to numerically reconstructed, realistic equilibria using a computationally efficient, semi-analytical geometrical method applicable to arbitrary unperturbed equilibria.
  Finally, for the LAR equilibrium, we extend the analysis to time-dependent perturbations and demonstrate the emergence of Arnold diffusion in guiding-center phase space, highlighting the role of the Arnold web in particle transport. Overall, this work advances the understanding of mode-particle resonant interactions and provides computational tools for predicting transport and confinement in realistic fusion configurations.

</details>


### [183] [Runaway electron control by self-excited waves](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08051)
*Kun Huang,Boris Breizman*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: This paper develops a reduced quasi-steady-state model for runaway-electron avalanches in tokamaks, where whistler-wave instabilities driven by the non-Maxwellian runaway distribution regulate runaway current. Three regimes emerge: subcritical Ohmic, avalanche, and instability-regulated, with marginal stability between whistler-wave drive and collisional damping governing the dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Runaway-electron avalanches in tokamak plasmas pose a threat to device integrity, but can be limited by kinetic instabilities driven by the non-Maxwellian runaway distribution. A framework is needed to understand the quasi-steady states arising from these interactions when total plasma current and bulk electron temperature are prescribed.

Method: The authors formulate a reduced model where the inductive electric field is determined self-consistently from the partitioning between Ohmic bulk current and runaway-electron current. Assuming the wave growth time is short relative to current-decay time, they invoke a marginal-stability regime where whistler-wave drive by runaway electrons balances collisional damping.

Result: The model yields three distinct regimes: (1) a subcritical Ohmic regime without avalanche, (2) an avalanche regime where runaway growth relaxes the inductive field to the avalanche threshold, and (3) an instability-regulated regime where self-excited whistler waves enhance momentum-space diffusion and limit runaway current. In the instability-regulated regime, the whistler wave spectrum forms a narrow ridge and low-energy runaway electrons dominate the runaway current.

Conclusion: Kinetic instabilities, specifically whistler waves, can naturally regulate runaway-electron avalanches in tokamaks, leading to a distinct instability-regulated steady state characterized by a narrow whistler-wave spectral ridge and dominant low-energy runaway current carriers.

Abstract: Runaway-electron avalanches in tokamak plasmas can be limited by kinetic instabilities driven by the non-Maxwellian runaway distribution. We formulate a reduced model for the quasi-steady state in which the total plasma current and bulk electron temperature are prescribed, while the inductive electric field is determined self-consistently from the partition between Ohmic bulk current and runaway-electron current. Because the wave growth time is short compared with the current-decay time, we consider a marginal-stability regime, in which whistler-wave drive by the runaway electrons balances collisional damping.
  The resulting states separate into three regimes: a subcritical Ohmic regime without an avalanche, an avalanche regime in which runaway growth relaxes the inductive field to the avalanche threshold, and an instability-regulated regime in which self-excited whistler waves enhance momentum-space diffusion and limit the runaway current.
  In the instability-regulated regime, the whistler wave spectrum forms a narrow ridge, and low-energy runaway electrons carry most of the runaway current.

</details>


### [184] [Generalized Similarity Theory for Plasmas](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08413)
*Yangyang Fu*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: The paper establishes a generalized theory of plasma similarity derived from scaling the Boltzmann equation coupled with Maxwell's equations, validated via particle-in-cell and fluid simulations across diverse plasma regimes including collisionless, relativistic, electromagnetic-wave-driven, and discharge plasmas, revealing intrinsic scale-invariances rooted in the governing equations' symmetries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a comprehensive and generalized theoretical framework for plasma similarity that extends beyond existing limited treatments, enabling prediction and understanding of scale-invariant behaviors across diverse plasma operational regimes.

Method: The generalized plasma similarity theory is derived by scaling the Boltzmann equation together with the full set of Maxwell's equations, and its predictions are verified through first-principles particle-in-cell and fluid simulations across multiple plasma regimes.

Result: The predicted similarity scalings were successfully demonstrated across collisionless plasmas with two-stream instabilities, electron diodes from classical to relativistic regimes, electromagnetic-wave-driven plasmas, and discharge plasmas from low to high ionization.

Conclusion: The generalized theory uncovers the intrinsic scale-invariant nature of plasmas under specified conditions, which originates from the symmetry and scaling transformations of the underlying governing equations.

Abstract: A generalized theory of plasma similarity is established based on the scaling of the Boltzmann equation coupled with the full set of Maxwell's equations. The predicted similarity scalings are demonstrated through first-principles particle-in-cell and fluid simulations across diverse operational regimes, including collisionless plasmas with two-stream instabilities, pure electron diodes spanning the classical to relativistic regimes, electromagnetic-wave-driven plasmas, and discharge plasmas ranging from low to high ionization regimes. The generalized theory reveals the intrinsic scale-invariant nature of plasmas under specified conditions, rooted in the symmetry and scaling transformations of the governing equations.

</details>


### [185] [Demonstration of a 10-metre-long discharge plasma source for plasma wakefield acceleration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08551)
*C. Amoedo,N. Lopes,N. E. Torrado,F. Silva,P. Muggli,L. Verra,M. Turner,G. Zevi Della Porta,M. Bergamaschi,A. Clairembaud,J. Mezger,F. Pannell,N. Z. van Gils,E. Gschwendtner,A. Sublet,the AWAKE Collaboration*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: The paper reports the first experimental characterization and beam-based validation of a 10-metre pulsed-DC discharge plasma source (DPS) as an alternative to the laser-ionised rubidium vapour plasma source (VPS) for the AWAKE experiment at CERN. The DPS delivered plasma densities between 10^14 and 10^15 cm^-3 in He, Ar, and Xe, with proton bunch self-modulation observed in all gases and density reproducibility within 0.63%, establishing the DPS as a valid alternative for beam-driven wakefield experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Realising AWAKE's potential for accelerating electron bunches to hundreds of GeV requires a plasma source extending to tens of metres with high longitudinal uniformity and reproducibility, motivating the need for an alternative to the laser-ionised rubidium vapour plasma source.

Method: A 10-metre pulsed-DC discharge plasma source was experimentally characterised using three noble gases (He, Ar, Xe) at electron densities between 10^14 and 10^15 cm^-3. Proton bunch self-modulation was observed with all three gases, and the modulation frequency served as a beam-based diagnostic for plasma density, cross-validated against laboratory interferometry measurements.

Result: The DPS reliably delivered plasma in all three noble gases, and proton bunch self-modulation was observed in each case. The beam-based density measurements agreed well with interferometry. Event-to-event modulation frequency reproducibility was demonstrated to within 0.63%, comparable to the VPS.

Conclusion: The DPS is established as a valid alternative plasma source for beam-driven wakefield experiments and supports further development towards longer plasma lengths. The discharge concept offers a route to long, reproducible plasma sources with straightforward density control for other beam-plasma experiments.

Abstract: The AWAKE experiment at CERN uses high-energy proton bunches to drive plasma wakefields capable of accelerating electron bunches up to hundreds of GeV in a single plasma. Realising this potential requires a plasma source that can extend to tens of metres while maintaining high longitudinal uniformity and reproducibility. Towards this goal, we report on the first experimental characterisation and beam-based validation of a 10-metre-long pulsed-DC discharge plasma source (DPS) used as an alternative to the laser-ionised rubidium vapour plasma source (VPS). The DPS reliably delivered plasma densities in three noble gases (He, Ar, Xe) at electron densities between 10$^{14}$ and 10$^{15}$ cm$^{-3}$. Proton bunch self-modulation was observed with all three gases, and the resulting modulation frequency was used as a beam-based diagnostic of the plasma density, showing good agreement with laboratory interferometry measurements. The event-to-event modulation frequency was demonstrated to be reproducible to within 0.63%, comparable to that of the VPS. These results establish the DPS as a valid alternative plasma source for beam-driven wakefield experiments, and support its further development towards longer plasma lengths. The present design is tailored to AWAKE requirements, but the underlying discharge concept provides a route to long, reproducible plasma sources with straightforward density control through discharge-beam timing for other beam-plasma experiments.

</details>


### [186] [On the modeling of oblique firehose instabilities in regularized Kappa plasmas using ALPS](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09036)
*D. L. Schröder,M. Lazar,H. Fichtner,K. G. Klein,D. Verscharen*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: This paper uses the Regularized kappa-Distribution (RKD) for the first time to study oblique firehose instabilities driven by temperature anisotropies in electrons and protons in space plasmas, employing the Arbitrary Linear Plasma Solver (ALPS). The results show suprathermal populations enhance firehose growth rates and RKDs significantly modify instability characteristics at oblique angles beyond what Maxwellian and Standard kappa-Distributions predict.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Space plasma particle velocity distributions deviate from Maxwellian equilibrium due to anisotropies and suprathermal populations. While the Standard kappa-Distribution (SKD) has been used to model these non-equilibrium states, it suffers from unphysical implications such as divergent velocity moments. The Regularized kappa-Distribution (RKD) was introduced to address these issues, and applying it to study firehose instabilities provides more physically realistic insights into plasma self-regulation processes.

Method: The study applies anisotropic RKDs to investigate oblique firehose instabilities induced by temperature anisotropies of electrons and protons. Since a general dispersion tensor for RKD plasmas is not yet available, the authors employ the Arbitrary Linear Plasma Solver (ALPS). The solver is first validated against established results from Maxwellian and SKD plasmas before being applied to RKD cases.

Result: Validation of ALPS against Maxwellian and SKD results confirms its capability. For RKDs, lower kappa values generally enhance firehose growth rates, confirming the stimulating effect of suprathermal populations. For the oblique electron firehose instability, RKDs substantially modify the competition between periodic and aperiodic branches only at intermediate angles, and can sustain significantly increased growth rates of especially aperiodic modes at highly oblique propagation, extending beyond both Maxwellian unstable regimes and those accessible with SKDs.

Conclusion: RKD-based analysis provides more physically consistent modeling of firehose instabilities in space plasmas, overcoming SKD limitations. The findings highlight the significant role suprathermal populations play in plasma self-regulation mechanisms such as in the expanding solar wind, demonstrating that RKDs can reveal instability behavior beyond what traditional Maxwellian and SKD frameworks can capture, particularly for highly oblique propagation.

Abstract: In-situ measurements in space plasmas indicate that the velocity distributions of charged particles are not in thermal equilibrium, deviating from a standard Maxwellian mainly due to anisotropies and suprathermal populations which enhance high-energy tails. Although the Standard \k{appa}-Distribution (SKD) is well established in modeling these non-equilibrium distributions, its application is often viewed controversially due to certain unphysical implications, in particular divergent velocity moments. To address these issues, the Regularized \k{appa}-Distribution (RKD) was introduced. Such advanced, in general anisotropic RKDs are invoked here for the first time to investigate oblique firehose instabilities, including those induced by the temperature anisotropy of electrons and protons, the dominant species in space plasmas. In weakly collisional plasmas, both of these instabilities are expected to play significant roles in the self-regulation of the macroscopic properties of space plasmas (e.g. the expanding solar wind) reported by observations. Is not yet possible to resort to a general dispersion tensor related to RKD plasmas (whose derivation is still a challenge), instead the Arbitrary Linear Plasma Solver (ALPS) is exploited here. The unstable solutions obtained for the already established Maxwellian & SKDs successfully validate the capability of ALPS. For RKDs, the instabilities confirm the stimulating effect of suprathermal populations, with lower \k{appa} values generally enhancing the firehose growth rates. For the oblique electron firehose instability, RKDs substantially modify the competition between periodic & aperiodic branches only at intermediate angles & can sustain significantly increased growth rates of especially aperiodic modes at highly oblique propagation beyond both the Maxwellian unstable regime & the one that is accessible with SKDs.

</details>

### [187] [Emergent Equilibrium Structure Along a Critical Cluster Recursion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07332)
*Shuo Wei,Abbas Ali Saberi,Youjin Deng*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: This paper shows that criticality alone does not fix the microscopic conditional structure of a probability measure. A bicolored cluster recursion held at criticality at every generation, without imposed equilibrium spin measures or fixed couplings, nonetheless develops Ising/FK-compatible structure in 2D and 3D, with the q=2 case uniquely factorizing among cluster colorings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Critical universality constrains macroscopic behavior but not the detailed microscopic conditional structure of probability measures. The authors ask whether equilibrium-compatible conditional structure can emerge purely from a constraint of maintaining criticality, without imposing equilibrium measures or fixed couplings.

Method: They study a history-dependent bicolored cluster recursion that is forced to remain critical at every generation (no equilibrium spin measure or fixed coupling imposed). The analysis is carried out in both two and three dimensions, examining conditional laws, effective couplings, and cluster/interface observables, along with an exact cluster-coloring factorization analysis.

Result: In both 2D and 3D, the generated sequence develops a common Ising/FK compatibility structure: second-shell dependence in one-site conditional laws is strongly suppressed, nearest-neighbor effective couplings converge toward the critical Ising value, and cluster/interface observables organize around the corresponding FK geometry. An exact coloring factorization identifies q=2 as the special point where residual connectivity weight vanishes from the two-color spin marginal.

Conclusion: Equilibrium-compatible conditional structure can emerge along a trajectory that remains critical throughout, suggesting that criticality itself may canalize microscopic structure toward Ising/FK universality even without equilibrium constraints.

Abstract: Critical universality does not determine the microscopic conditional structure of a probability measure. We study a bicolored cluster recursion constrained to remain critical at every generation, with no equilibrium spin measure or fixed coupling imposed. In both two and three dimensions, the resulting history-dependent sequence develops a common Ising/Fortuin--Kasteleyn (FK) compatibility structure: the second-shell dependence of a one-site conditional law is strongly suppressed, nearest-neighbor effective couplings move progressively toward one another near the critical Ising value, and cluster and interface observables organize around the corresponding FK geometry. An exact cluster-coloring factorization singles out $q=2$ as the point where the residual connectivity weight disappears from the two-color spin marginal. Thus equilibrium-compatible conditional structure can emerge along a trajectory that remains critical throughout.

</details>

### [188] [Optimization Algorithm-Assisted Nanostructure Engineering for Performance Enhancement of Thin-Film Solar Cells](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06191)
*Abyaz Karim,Sajid Ahmed Chowdhury,A. F. M. Afnan Uzzaman Sheikh,Asif Al Suny,Mustafa Habib Chowdhury*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: This computational study uses FDTD optical simulations combined with optimization algorithms to find optimal geometric parameters for core-shell nanostructures with triangular cross-sections embedded in thin-film solar cells. The optimized nanostructures produced short-circuit current densities of 28.90–35.39 mA/cm², a significant improvement over the 13.69 mA/cm² of reference cells without nanostructures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite extensive research on light-trapping nanostructures in thin-film solar cells, the relationship between their geometric parameters and cell performance is not yet fully understood. Finding the optimal parameter set is essential for maximizing device performance.

Method: Finite-difference time-domain (FDTD) method was used for optical simulations of thin-film solar cells with embedded core-shell nanostructures of triangular cross-section. Three optimization algorithms were applied to search for geometric parameters maximizing short-circuit current density (J_SC), and results were analyzed through sensitivity analysis to examine the effects of small parameter perturbations.

Result: The optimized nanostructure parameters yielded J_SC values between 28.90 and 35.39 mA/cm², compared to 13.69 mA/cm² for a reference TFSC without nanostructures—representing more than a two-fold improvement in photocurrent generation.

Conclusion: Optimized periodic grating structures with core-shell triangular cross-sections can provide substantial performance improvements in thin-film solar cells, demonstrating the value of combining FDTD simulations with optimization algorithms for light-trapping structure design.

Abstract: A computation-based study is presented that utilizes the finite-difference time-domain (FDTD) method to perform optical simulations. These simulations were used to observe the effects of core-shell nanostructures with triangular cross-sections embedded inside the light absorbing layer of thin-film solar cells (TFSCs), for improving the optoelectronic performance. Despite extensive research, the relationship between the geometric parameters of light trapping nanostructures and TFSC performance is not yet fully elucidated. To assist in finding the optimal set of parameters, optimization algorithms are used to find a set of parameters that provide maximal short-circuit current density ($J_{SC}$). Three separate algorithms were applied, and the results were scrutinized in-depth with a sensitivity analysis, observing the effects of small changes in the optimal parameters. The optimal parameters of the nanostructure produced $J_{SC}$ values ranging from 28.90 to 35.39 mA/cm$^2$ compared to 13.69 mA/cm$^2$ for a reference TFSC with no nanostructure present. From the results of this study, it can be inferred that optimized periodic grating structures can provide substantial improvement in performance of TFSCs.

</details>


<div id='physics.soc-ph'></div>

# physics.soc-ph [[Back]](#toc)

### [189] [Phase spaces of phase spaces: A method of constructing generalizable and topologically informed bases from time series demonstrated by reconstruction of the geometry of the macroeconomy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07019)
*Maximilian Topel*

Main category: physics.soc-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces a framework that extracts interpretable, low-dimensional, dynamically informed bases from arbitrary timeseries using Takens' Delay Embedding Theorem, dimensionality reduction, and optimal transport. This creates a "phase space of phase spaces" (PSoPS) where interrelated dynamical series coexist via barycenters and transport plans. Applied to 281,536 macroeconomic series, it yields a single connected object of effective dimension ~7.3, into which canonical economic laws (Phillips, Okun, Solow) embed as ~2D looped sub-attractors. The framework recovers directed monetary transmission pathways from geometry and demonstrates irreducibility of nonlinear structure via surrogate testing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Both traditional small-law approaches (too few variables to capture system variation) and large-data universal models (dimensionality explosion around fundamentally low-dimensional dynamics) fail through the same flaw: an incorrect basis. There is a need for a framework that bridges these extremes by extracting a mathematically sound, interpretable low-dimensional basis directly from timeseries data.

Method: The framework combines three mathematical tools: (1) Takens' Delay Embedding Theorem to reconstruct phase spaces from timeseries, (2) dimensionality reduction to manage complexity, and (3) optimal transport to construct barycenters across many phase spaces. This creates a PSoPS—a phase space of phase spaces—where barycenters form a basis describing relational geometry of each timeseries, and transport plans between individual phase spaces and barycenters encode the topology of relationships.

Result: The macroeconomic PSoPS built from 281,536 series is a single connected object with effective dimension 7.3 ± 0.2. Canonical economic laws (Phillips, Okun, Solow) embed as roughly 2-dimensional looped sub-attractors. The coupling between concept barycenters is directed, with the price of money and productivity as net sources and credit and output as sinks—recovering monetary transmission purely from geometry. IAAFT phase-randomized surrogates destroy 36-43% of dimension across the three laws and 38% on the full corpus, confirming the basis is irreducibly nonlinear.

Conclusion: The paper closes by proposing extensions to fields whose dynamics share attractor geometries and to systems that are intrinsically low dimensional yet difficult to navigate in raw coordinates. The framework appears generalizable beyond economics to any domain with underlying dynamical structure.

Abstract: Laws from physics to social science rest on a small, enumerable number of factors explaining variation in the systems they describe. While the orthodoxy of economics, physics and chemistry relies on a small number of analytically interpretable laws, large data models pursuing universality throw in all available data. Both fail in their extremes through the same flaw, an incorrect basis: too few orthogonal variables to describe system variation, or an explosion of dimensionality around fundamentally low dimensional but unknown dynamics. We propose and validate a framework that extracts an interpretable, mathematically sound, low dimensional and dynamically informed basis directly from arbitrary timeseries. Combining Takens' Delay Embedding Theorem, dimensionality reduction and optimal transport, we produce a phase space of phase spaces (PSoPS) on which all interrelated dynamical series exist: barycenters formed from many phase spaces, together with the families of transport plans between individual phase spaces and the barycenter, yield a basis describing the relational geometry of each timeseries and the topology of economic relationships. The macroeconomic PSoPS built from 281,536 series is a single connected object of effective dimension 7.3 +/- 0.2, into which the canonical laws (Phillips, Okun, Solow) embed as roughly 2-dimensional looped sub-attractors. The coupling between concept barycenters is directed, with the price of money and productivity as net sources and credit and output as sinks, recovering monetary transmission from geometry alone. IAAFT phase-randomized surrogates destroy 36-43% of the dimension across the three laws and 38% on the full corpus, so the basis is irreducibly nonlinear. We close with proposed extensions to fields whose dynamics share attractor geometries and to systems that are intrinsically low dimensional yet hard to navigate in raw coordinates.

</details>

# astro-ph.HE [[Back]](#toc)

### [190] [Two-Phase Structure of Synchrotron-Cooling-Unstable Relativistic Plasma](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07793)
*Agnieszka Wierzchucka,Pablo J. Bilbao,Robert J. Ewart,Dmitri A. Uzdensky,Alexander A. Schekochihin*

Main category: astro-ph.HE

TL;DR: The paper shows that relativistic, synchrotron-cooling, collisionless high-β pair plasmas filament into a two-phase medium through the interplay of the synchrotron cooling instability (SCI) and synchrotron firehose instability (SFHI), and proposes a fluid model to describe and predict this behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how relativistic pair plasmas with synchrotron cooling and high plasma β self-organize into multi-phase structures, a problem relevant to astrophysical environments such as pulsar wind nebulae and relativistic jets.

Method: Combines analytic theory, radiative particle-in-cell (PIC) simulations, and fluid simulations to study the instability dynamics and two-phase filamentation.

Result: Identifies two distinct plasma phases: one with high β infested with small-scale firehose fluctuations that pin pressure anisotropy to marginal stability, and another with much lower β where firehose modes are suppressed allowing large pressure anisotropies.

Conclusion: A fluid model is proposed that captures linear and nonlinear evolution of SCI and SFHI and can predict the emergence time of the two-phase structure.

Abstract: Using analytic theory, radiative particle-in-cell (PIC) simulations, and fluid simulations, we show that relativistic, synchrotron-cooling, collisionless, high-$β$ pair plasmas filament into a two-phase medium. This process occurs through the interplay of the synchrotron cooling instability (SCI) with the synchrotron firehose instability (SFHI). One phase has high plasma~$β$ and is infested with small-scale firehose fluctuations, which scatter particles and pin the pressure anisotropy to the firehose-marginal level. The other phase has much lower~$β$, causing the suppression of firehose modes and thus allowing large pressure anisotropies. We propose a fluid model for this two-phase plasma, which we use to study the linear and nonlinear evolution of the SCI and SFHI, and to predict the emergence time of the two-phase structure.

</details>


<div id='math.CA'></div>

# math.CA [[Back]](#toc)

### [191] [Terminating Zero-Balanced Hypergeometric Series Using Divided Differences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06168)
*Fatma Zürnacı Yetiş*

Main category: math.CA

TL;DR: The paper uses divided differences to derive and prove terminating hypergeometric series identities, particularly zero-balanced cases. It provides alternative proofs for ${}_3F_2(1)$ and ${}_4F_3(1)$ summation formulas, extends the argument to general $_{r+1}F_r(1)$ cases, and combines Lagrange representation with the Leibniz rule to obtain a finite convolution transformation for $_{r+2}F_{r+1}(1)$ series.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a known close relationship between divided differences and hypergeometric series, and recent work shows divided differences can effectively derive terminating hypergeometric identities. The authors aim to apply this approach to terminating zero-balanced hypergeometric series to provide alternative proofs and obtain new transformations.

Method: The approach uses explicit product evaluations arising from divided differences to prove zero-balanced ${}_3F_2(1)$ and ${}_4F_3(1)$ summation formulas. The argument is then generalized to the terminating $_{r+1}F_r(1)$ case. Additionally, the authors combine the Lagrange representation with the Leibniz rule for divided differences to derive a finite convolution transformation for terminating $_{r+2}F_{r+1}(1)$ series.

Result: The divided-difference approach reproduces known zero-balanced summation formulas (for ${}_3F_2(1)$ and ${}_4F_3(1)$) and extends to the general terminating case. It also yields a new finite convolution transformation for terminating hypergeometric $_{r+2}F_{r+1}(1)$ series, along with its zero-balanced specialization.

Conclusion: Divided differences provide a unified and effective framework for both proving known zero-balanced hypergeometric summation identities and deriving new structural transformations (finite convolution) for terminating hypergeometric series.

Abstract: There is a close relationship between divided differences and hypergeometric series, and recent studies have shown that divided differences can be used effectively to derive terminating hypergeometric identities. In this paper, we apply this approach to terminating zero-balanced hypergeometric series. Using explicit product evaluations arising from divided differences, we give alternative proofs of zero-balanced ${}_3F_2(1)$ and ${}_4F_3(1)$ summation formulas and then extend the argument to the general terminating ${}_{r+1}F_r(1)$ case. We also combine the Lagrange representation with the Leibniz rule for divided differences to derive a finite convolution transformation for a terminating ${}_{r+2}F_{r+1}(1)$ series. Thus, the divided-difference approach not only reproduces the known zero-balanced summation formulas but also yields a finite convolution transformation for terminating hypergeometric series, together with its zero-balanced specialization.

</details>


### [192] [Polynomial Bohnenblust--Hille Bounds for product of cyclic groups](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07758)
*Joseph Slote,Alexander Volberg*

Main category: math.CA

TL;DR: The paper proves that dimension-free Bohnenblust-Hille constants for Fourier interactions of order d on the group (Z/KZ)^N grow polynomially in d, with exponent on the order of 4γ_K+5, where γ_2=1/2 and γ_K=K log(K-1)/(4(K-2)) for K≥3.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To establish that Bohnenblust-Hille constants restricted to interactions of bounded order exhibit polynomial (rather than exponential) growth in the interaction order, providing a self-contained proof for multi-dimensional cyclic groups.

Method: The proof works on the finite abelian group C_K^N ≅ (Z/KZ)^N. For each Fourier character χ_α with s(α) active coordinates, it bounds the ℓ^{p_d}-norm of Fourier coefficients (restricted to interactions up to order d) against the sup-norm, where p_d = 2d/(d+1). The key exponents γ_K encode the group-dependent growth constants.

Result: The authors obtain the polynomial bound (Σ_{s(α)≤d} |f̂(α)|^{p_d})^{1/p_d} ≤ C_K d^{4γ_K+5} ||f||_∞, with explicit exponent depending on K. The same bound applies to the total-degree analogue.

Conclusion: The dimension-free Bohnenblust-Hille constants with restricted interaction order grow at most polynomially in d, confirming a proposed sharp (or near-sharp) growth rate for this family of constants.

Abstract: Fix an integer $K\ge2$, and let $C_K^N\cong(\Z/K\Z)^N$ be the product of cyclic groups of order $K$. For a Fourier character $χ_α$, let $s(α)$ be the number of active coordinates. We give a self-contained proposed proof that the dimension-free Bohnenblust--Hille constants governed by interaction order grow polynomially: if $p_d=2d/(d+1)$ and \[
  γ_2=\frac12,
  \qquad
  γ_K=\frac{K\log(K-1)}{4(K-2)}\quad(K\ge3), \] then \[
  \left(\sum_{s(α)\le d}
  \abs{\wh f(α)}^{p_d}\right)^{1/p_d}
  \le C_K d^{4γ_K+5}\norm f_\infty. \] The same estimate follows for the canonical total-degree class.

</details>

### [193] [The 2M Multiplication Algorithm for Complex Matrices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05419)
*Peter Caday*

Main category: cs.DC

TL;DR: This paper reduces the cost of complex matrix multiplication from 3 real GEMMs (3M algorithm) to just 2 real GEMMs for integer matrices, and combines this with the Ozaki-II scheme for floating-point matrices to achieve complex GEMM in roughly twice the time of a real GEMM.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Complex matrix multiplication is computationally expensive, requiring multiple real GEMMs. The standard approach uses 4 real GEMMs, and the 3M algorithm reduces this to 3. The authors aim to further reduce this cost, especially given that complex GEMM is a fundamental operation in many scientific and engineering applications.

Method: The paper reduces the 3M algorithm to a 2M algorithm for matrices with integer real and imaginary parts, requiring only 2 real GEMMs of the same size plus quadratic-time pre- and post-processing. For floating-point matrices, the 2M approach is combined with the Ozaki-II scheme to produce a high-performance, practical algorithm. The method also yields new algorithms for symmetric rank-k updates (SYRK/HERK) that internally use full rectangular GEMMs.

Result: The proposed approach enables complex GEMM to be computed using only 2 real GEMMs for integer matrices, and achieves practical high-performance floating-point complex GEMM at roughly twice the time of a real GEMM of the same size. New SYRK/HERK algorithms are derived as corollaries.

Conclusion: Complex matrix multiplication can be significantly accelerated by reducing the number of real GEMMs required from 3 to 2, with the floating-point variant made practical via combination with the Ozaki-II scheme, achieving near-optimal performance relative to real GEMM.

Abstract: Complex matrix multiplication is typically computed using 4 real matrix multiplications (GEMMs) of the same size. The well-known 3M multiplication algorithm reduces this cost to 3 real GEMMs, together with quadratic time pre- and post-processing steps.
  In this paper, we reduce 3M to 2M for matrices with integer real and imaginary parts, performing complex GEMM with only 2 real GEMMs of the same size, along with quadratic time pre- and post-processing. For floating-point matrices, 2M multiplication combines naturally with the Ozaki-II scheme, yielding a practical, high-performance algorithm for computing a complex floating-point GEMM in roughly twice the time of a real GEMM of the same size. As corollaries, we derive new algorithms for symmetric rank-$k$ updates (SYRK/HERK) that internally use full rectangular GEMMs.

</details>

### [194] [Capacitance of Undoped Thin-Film Diodes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08047)
*Oskar J. Sandberg,Mathias Nyman,Stefan Zeiske,Staffan Dahlstrom,Johannes Benduhn,Ardalan Armin*

Main category: physics.app-ph

TL;DR: The paper presents an analytical framework for the capacitance of thin-film diodes based on undoped semiconductors, accounting for injected charge carriers and electrode charge effects. It also introduces a method to extract built-in voltage, validated through drift-diffusion simulations and experiments on organic solar cells.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The capacitance of thin-film diodes based on undoped semiconductors is dominated by injected charge carriers rather than doping-induced carriers, yet no analytical framework existed to describe this behavior.

Method: The authors derive an analytical description of the capacitance that fully accounts for injected charge carrier and electrode charge effects. They validate the theoretical framework numerically via drift-diffusion simulations and experimentally on organic solar cells.

Result: The analytical framework is substantiated by both drift-diffusion simulations and experimental measurements on organic solar cells. Based on the derived capacitance model, a method to extract the built-in voltage is presented.

Conclusion: A complete analytical description of capacitance in undoped semiconductor thin-film diodes is established, enabling built-in voltage extraction—a capability previously lacking for such devices.

Abstract: The capacitance of thin-film diodes based on undoped semiconductors are dominated by injected charge carriers rather than doping-induced carriers. However, an analytical framework of the capacitance in these devices has remained elusive. Here, we derive an analytical description of the capacitance, fully accounting for injected charge carrier and electrode charge effects. Based on these findings, a method to extract the built-in voltage in these devices is presented. The theoretical framework is substantiated numerically by drift-diffusion simulations and experimentally on organic solar cells.

</details>
