# math.RA [[Back]](#toc)

### [208] [A \(3\times 3\) counterexample to Lin and Wimmer's rank-minimization conjecture associated with Roth's similarity theorem](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07113)
*Shuo Shi,Juan Zhang,Yun Zhang*

Main category: math.RA

TL;DR: The paper presents a 3×3 counterexample, valid over any field, to a rank-minimization conjecture by Lin and Wimmer related to Roth's similarity theorem for the Sylvester matrix equation. It also proves that over the complex field, no counterexample exists when one matrix size is less than 3, making the example dimensionally minimal over the complex field.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To resolve a conjecture by Lin and Wimmer about rank minimization related to the Sylvester matrix equation and Roth's similarity theorem, by either proving or finding a counterexample.

Method: The authors construct an explicit 3×3 counterexample valid over every field, and separately prove a result over the complex field showing that no counterexample can exist when one matrix dimension is smaller than 3.

Result: A dimensionally minimal 3×3 counterexample to the Lin-Wimmer rank-minimization conjecture is found, valid over all fields. Over the complex field, it is shown that no counterexample exists for smaller matrix sizes.

Conclusion: The Lin-Wimmer conjecture is false in general, and the counterexample is dimensionally minimal over the complex field, establishing the sharp threshold for when the conjecture can fail.

Abstract: We give a \(3\times 3\) counterexample, valid over every field, to a rank-minimization conjecture of Lin and Wimmer (Bull. Aust. Math. Soc., 84 (3) (2011), 441--443) related to Roth's similarity theorem for the Sylvester matrix equation. We also prove that, over the complex field, no counterexample can occur when one of the two matrix sizes is less than \(3\). Hence the example is dimensionally minimal over the complex field.

</details>


<div id='physics.atm-clus'></div>

# physics.atm-clus [[Back]](#toc)

### [209] [Core-Level Spectroscopy Decodes Bond-Alternation Dynamics of Cyclo[18]Carbon](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06916)
*Minrui Wei,Zeyu Liu,Sheng-Yu Wang,Jun-Rong Zhang,Lu Zhang,Guoyan Ge,Weijie Hua*

Main category: physics.atm-clus

TL;DR: Using first-principles simulations on cyclo[18]carbon (C18), the paper shows that core ionization symmetrizes the BLA double-well potential, and that X-ray spectra (C1s IPs and NEXAFS π* peaks) are highly sensitive to bond-length alternation—shifting by up to 2.4 eV and 4 eV respectively—providing a quantitative spectroscopy–structure dictionary for future ultrafast X-ray experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address whether localized core excitations probed by time-resolved X-ray spectroscopy can report on global collective distortions such as bond-length alternation dynamics.

Method: Mapping two-dimensional potential energy surfaces via first-principles simulations of cyclo[18]carbon, and computing X-ray spectra (C1s ionization potentials and NEXAFS spectra) across the BLA coordinate.

Result: Core ionization symmetrizes the ground-state double-well potential. C1s ionization potentials vary by up to 2.4 eV across the BLA coordinate (1.1–1.4 Å), with 0.9 eV variation between minima from different functionals; NEXAFS π* peaks shift by up to 4 eV.

Conclusion: X-ray spectroscopy can indeed serve as a sensitive reporter of global collective distortions, and the derived spectroscopy–structure relationships enable real-time monitoring of bond-alternation dynamics in future ultrafast X-ray experiments.

Abstract: The advent of X-ray free-electron lasers and high-harmonic generation has made time-resolved X-ray spectroscopy a powerful tool for probing local atomic environments, yet whether localized core excitations can report on global collective distortions remains open. Cyclo[18]carbon (C$_{18}$), with its polyynic ground state (D$_\text{9h}$) and cumulenic transition state (D$_\text{18h}$), provides an ideal model to address this long-standing issue in bond-length alternation (BLA) dynamics. Mapping two-dimensional potential energy surfaces by first-principles simulations, we find that core ionization symmetrizes the ground-state double-well potential along the BLA coordinate. Our calculated X-ray spectra reveal remarkable sensitivity to bond-length variations: C1s ionization potentials vary by up to 2.4~eV across the BLA coordinate (1.1--1.4~Å), with a 0.9~eV variation for minima predicted by different functionals, while NEXAFS $π^*$ peaks shift by up to 4~eV across the same coordinate. These predicted signatures provide a quantitative spectroscopy--structure dictionary for decoding transient structures in future ultrafast X-ray experiments and monitoring bond-alternation dynamics in real time.

</details>


<div id='cond-mat.mtrl-sci'></div>

# cond-mat.mtrl-sci [[Back]](#toc)

### [210] [Momentum-Resolved Electronic Structure for Quasicrystals: Full-Band Spectra and Chern Number](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06043)
*Donglin Yang,Huajie Chen,Dexuan Zhou,Xiaoxu Li*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper develops a systematically convergent reciprocal-space tight-binding framework for quasicrystals, combining Fourier-module scattering-channel Hamiltonians with expanding pseudo-Brillouin zones to compute full-band momentum-resolved electronic structure. Applied to Penrose and Ammann-Beenker models, it reveals quasibands, pseudogap mechanisms, Zeeman-driven topological transitions, and phason-invariant observables.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quasicrystals lack translational symmetry, which prevents the use of Bloch decomposition and Brillouin-zone integration—tools essential for conventional momentum-resolved electronic structure calculations. This makes full-band electronic structure computation difficult or impossible with existing approaches.

Method: The framework combines two systematically refinable components: (1) a Fourier-module scattering-channel Hamiltonian providing local spectral and current-current correlation quantities at each physical momentum, and (2) an expanding hierarchy of pseudo-Brillouin zones with an exact local-to-global relation converting local quantities into bulk thermodynamic observables.

Result: Applied to Penrose and Ammann-Beenker quasicrystals, the framework reveals full-band momentum-resolved quasibands and a multichannel pseudogap formation mechanism beyond low-energy effective models. It resolves Zeeman-driven gap closings/reopenings, quantized Chern plateaus, and phason invariance of bulk spectral and topological observables.

Conclusion: The framework provides a unified reciprocal-space route to full-band spectral and topological properties of quasicrystals, overcoming the lack of translational symmetry that has hindered such computations.

Abstract: Quasicrystals lack the translational symmetry that underlies Bloch decomposition and Brillouin-zone integration, making full-band momentum-resolved electronic structure difficult to formulate and compute. We develop a systematically convergent reciprocal-space tight-binding framework for a broad class of quasicrystals. The method combines two systematically refinable components: a Fourier-module scattering-channel Hamiltonian that yields local spectral and current-current correlation quantities at each physical momentum, and an expanding hierarchy of pseudo-Brillouin zones that converts the resulting local quantities into bulk thermodynamic observables through an exact local-to-global relation. Applied to the Penrose and Ammann-Beenker models, the framework uncovers full-band momentum-resolved quasibands and a multichannel mechanism for pseudogap formation, both beyond the scope of low-energy effective models. It further resolves Zeeman-driven gap closings and reopenings, quantized Chern plateaus, and the phason invariance of bulk spectral and topological observables. This framework provides a unified reciprocal-space route to full-band spectral and topological properties of quasicrystals.

</details>


<div id='astro-ph.SR'></div>

# astro-ph.SR [[Back]](#toc)

### [211] [R-matrix calculations for opacities: V. Temperature-density dependence of photoabsorption cross sections and opacity spectra of oxygen ions O VI and O VII](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07649)
*Divya Chari,Sultana N Nahar,Anil K Pradhan*

Main category: astro-ph.SR

TL;DR: This paper presents R-matrix photoabsorption cross sections for Li-like O VI and He-like O VII oxygen ions, examining how plasma broadening modifies these cross sections as functions of temperature and density relevant to astrophysical and high-energy-density (HED) plasma environments, showing progressive dissolution of autoionizing resonances into the continuum at increasing electron density.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: All atomic systems in plasma environments are subject to environment effects that modify photoabsorption, which governs radiation propagation through bound-bound spectral lines and bound-free photoionization with autoionizing resonances. Understanding these effects is critical for modeling astrophysical plasmas and laboratory HED experiments (e.g., inertial confinement fusion), motivating a need for accurate photoabsorption cross sections that account for plasma broadening across a range of temperature and density conditions.

Method: R-matrix calculations of photoabsorption cross sections are performed for O VI and O VII. The study systematically examines how plasma broadening modifies these cross sections by varying electron density along representative isotherms (T = 1e6 and 2e6 K) with electron densities ranging from 1e18 to 1e23 cm^-3, covering conditions from the solar convection zone to laboratory HED plasmas. Results are compared against previous Opacity Project (OP) calculations.

Result: Autoionizing resonances progressively dissolve into the continuum with increasing electron density at each isotherm, flattening and merging into background cross sections at BCZ conditions at much lower densities than required for bound-bound line features. R-matrix photoionization cross sections cover a much higher energy range with significantly richer autoionizing resonance spectra not included in OP, showing significant quantitative differences in monochromatic opacities.

Conclusion: The work demonstrates general features and methodology for plasma broadening of photoabsorption applicable across a broad range of plasma conditions, useful for modeling HED plasmas in astrophysics and analyzing transmission spectra from laboratory ICF experiments.

Abstract: We present R-matrix photoabsorption cross sections for Li-like oxygen O VI and He-like oxygen O VII and examine how plasma broadening modifies them as a function of temperature and density in astrophysical and laboratory high-energy-density (HED) plasma sources. All atomic systems are subject to plasma environment effects, and the propagation of radiation depends on photoabsorption via bound-bound transitions as spectral lines and autoionizing resonances in bound-free photoionization cross sections. We identify and illustrate low and high temperature-density limits for the onset of plasma broadening in O VI and O VII, demonstrating general features and methodology applicable across a broad range of plasma conditions. Calculations are presented along two representative isotherms corresponding to the solar base of convection zone (BCZ) at $T = 1 \times 10^6$ and $2 \times 10^6$ K, and electron densities $N_e = 10^{18}$-$10^{23}$ cm$^{-3}$. Autoionizing resonances progressively dissolve into the continuum with increasing electron density at each isotherm, flattening and merging into the background cross sections at BCZ conditions, at much lower densities than bound-bound line features require. Illustrative examples are given for energy regions containing Rydberg resonance series, including large photoexcitation-of-core (PEC) resonances. Comparisons with previous Opacity Project results reveal that (i) the R-matrix photoionization cross sections cover a much higher energy range where a significantly richer spectrum of autoionizing resonances are present that are not included in OP, and (ii) the corresponding monochromatic opacities show significant quantitative differences. This work is generally applicable to modeling HED plasmas in astrophysics, and to the analysis of transmission spectra in laboratory experiments on inertial confinement fusion (ICF) devices.

</details>


<div id='math.OC'></div>

# math.OC [[Back]](#toc)

### [212] [Wasserstein Stability and Free Boundaries in Measure-Parameterized Bilevel Obstacle Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08454)
*Kun Huang*

Main category: math.OC

TL;DR: This paper studies obstacle-constrained variational problems where reduced energies depend on a probability law through a lower-level optimizer. It proves Lipschitz continuity of the policy map under strong convexity and type-Lipschitz reduced marginals, characterizes the coincidence set's structure in one-dimensional linear-obstacle subclasses with sharp Wasserstein modulus bounds, establishes finite-sample rates for empirical laws, proves asymptotic normality of empirical thresholds at transversal roots, and illustrates results with a firm response model producing a nonlinear corporate-tax schedule.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to establish rigorous stability and statistical guarantees for obstacle-constrained variational problems whose energies depend on probability distributions through lower-level optimizers. Such problems arise in applications like optimal policy design with endogenous responses (e.g., tax policy) where the reduced energy depends on the population law, requiring understanding of how the upper-level policy and coincidence-set thresholds vary with the underlying distribution.

Method: The authors use a hierarchical structure: (1) lower-level optimizer characterized via uniform strong convexity yielding a single-valued Lipschitz follower response, combined with convexity and a Poincare inequality for unique upper-level policies; (2) type-Lipschitz reduced marginals to derive Lipschitz continuity of the policy map from the 1-Wasserstein metric to the energy space; (3) in one dimension, policy derivative identified as positive part of cumulative forcing, with single-crossing yielding interval coincidence sets and algebraic crossing orders giving W_1^{1/m} moduli for endpoints; (4) dimension-dependent Wasserstein concentration bounds for finite-sample rates; (5) influence function analysis for asymptotic normality of empirical thresholds; and (6) conditional level-set arguments for Hausdorff stability in higher dimensions.

Result: The key results include: Lipschitz continuity of policy maps from 1-Wasserstein space to energy space; an interval structure for coincidence sets under single-crossing; sharp W_1^{1/m} moduli for algebraic crossings (proved sharp via odd-power examples) with Lipschitz recovery for transversal crossings; finite-sample convergence rates for policies and thresholds from empirical laws; asymptotic linearity and CLT for empirical thresholds at transversal roots with explicit influence functions; and local Hausdorff stability for higher-dimensional regular patches given a nondegenerate switching function.

Conclusion: The paper provides a systematic theoretical framework for obstacle-constrained variational problems with law-dependent energies. The application to a corporate-tax model with quadratic firm response yields an endogenous zero-tax region and Wasserstein-stable threshold, demonstrating the framework's practical utility with a fully analytic (non-calibrated) illustration that captures realistic tax policy phenomena.

Abstract: We study obstacle-constrained variational problems whose reduced energies depend on a probability law through a lower-level optimizer. Uniform strong convexity yields a single-valued Lipschitz follower response, while convexity and a Poincare inequality give a unique upper-level policy. A type-Lipschitz reduced marginal then implies Lipschitz continuity of the policy map from the 1-Wasserstein metric to the energy space. In a one-dimensional linear-obstacle subclass, the policy derivative is the positive part of a cumulative forcing. Single crossing makes the coincidence set an interval. An algebraic crossing of order m gives a $W_1^{1/m}$ modulus for its endpoint; odd-power examples show that this exponent is sharp, while a transversal crossing recovers Lipschitz stability. For empirical laws on compact subsets of $\mathbb{R}^k$, dimension-dependent Wasserstein bounds yield finite-sample rates for policies and thresholds. At a transversal population root, the empirical threshold is asymptotically linear, with an explicit influence function and central limit theorem. For higher-dimensional regular patches, a conditional level-set argument gives local Hausdorff stability when a nondegenerate switching function is available. An explicit quadratic firm response produces a nonlinear corporate-tax schedule with an endogenous zero-tax region and a Wasserstein-stable threshold. The tax illustration is analytic and uses no empirical calibration.

</details>


### [213] [On the cost of reaching an attainable state for the boundary-controlled 1-D heat equation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08842)
*Olivier Glass*

Main category: math.OC

TL;DR: This paper studies the minimal control cost required to drive the heat equation on an interval to a given attainable state using Dirichlet boundary controls, and provides an optimal estimate of this cost as the time horizon T approaches zero.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding the minimal cost (control size) needed to reach a target state in the heat equation is a fundamental question in control theory. The authors aim to characterize this cost precisely, particularly in the asymptotic regime of short time horizons where estimates tend to be most challenging and interesting.

Method: The paper considers the one-dimensional heat equation on an interval with null initial condition and Dirichlet boundary controls. It characterizes the cost of reaching an attainable state by estimating the minimal norm of boundary controls needed, expressed in terms of an appropriate norm of the target state. The estimate is shown to be optimal in the limit as T goes to 0.

Result: An estimate of the smallest boundary controls required to reach a given attainable state is provided, expressed via a suitable norm of the target state. This estimate is proven to be optimal in the sense that it cannot be improved as the time horizon T tends to 0.

Conclusion: The paper provides a sharp (optimal) characterization of the control cost for the heat equation with Dirichlet boundary controls in the short-time limit, offering a precise quantitative understanding of controllability cost for this classical parabolic system.

Abstract: We consider the heat equation in an interval of the real line, with null initial condition, and Dirichlet boundary controls. We study the cost of reaching an attainable state at some time $T>0$. Precisely, given a state in the interval that we know is reachable by suitable boundary controls, we estimate the size of the smallest controls that reach it, in terms of an appropriate norm of this state. Our estimate is optimal in some sense as the time horizon goes to $0$.

</details>


<div id='hep-ex'></div>

# hep-ex [[Back]](#toc)

### [214] [Reducing simulation-related emissions in rare-event searches through optimised event biasing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07523)
*P. Knights,L. J. Milligan,K. Nikolopoulos*

Main category: hep-ex

TL;DR: This paper discusses an optimization study of importance-splitting event biasing for shielding simulations in rare-event search experiments, aiming to balance statistical precision with CPU time while reducing researchers' computing-related carbon footprint.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Rare-event search experiments require increasingly low backgrounds and detailed shielding simulations. However, simulations contribute ~10% to a particle physicist's carbon footprint, and as sensitivity requirements grow (backgrounds below 0.01 counts/kg/keV), more computing resources are needed—increasing the carbon footprint. There is a lack of systematic guidance on how to optimally apply event biasing techniques to mitigate this.

Method: The paper presents an optimization study of the importance-splitting biasing technique, focused on finding the optimal trade-off between statistical precision and simulation CPU-time in shielding simulations.

Result: The study demonstrates how optimized event biasing can balance statistical precision with computational cost, thereby reducing the computing-related carbon footprint of rare-event researchers.

Conclusion: Event biasing optimization provides a means to reduce the carbon footprint associated with simulations in rare-event search experiments while maintaining the required statistical precision for accurate background understanding.

Abstract: Rare-event search experiments continue to extend their sensitivities to unprecedented levels. This requires increasingly small backgrounds, and shielding schemes that can suppress external backgrounds by several orders of magnitude. Moreover, detailed simulations are required to attain a good understanding of these experimental backgrounds. Studies have suggested, however, that simulations and computing-related tasks contribute approximately 10\% to the average particle physicist's carbon footprint. With backgrounds frequently below 0.01 counts per kg of target per keV of energy, simulations require more computing resources; growing a rare-event researcher's computing-related carbon footprint. Event biasing is often applied in shielding simulations as a remedy, yet there is a lack of systematic guidance on how to maximally benefit from them. An optimisation study for the importance-splitting biasing technique is discussed, focused on balancing statistical precision with simulation CPU-time, and how this can benefit the average researcher's carbon footprint.

</details>


<div id='cs.CE'></div>

# cs.CE [[Back]](#toc)

### [215] [Annular liquid jets: exact constraint absorption and finite-time loss of transversality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05457)
*Francisco R. Villatoro*

Main category: cs.CE

TL;DR: This paper presents a new formulation for modeling annular liquid jets with surface tension, showing that established index-reduction schemes fail to enforce the free-end constraint and become degenerate in the steady limit. The authors avoid this by absorbing the constraint identically through a change of dependent variable, enabling Chebyshev collocation with geometric convergence. They discover three key results: convergence length decreases strictly with thickness-to-radius ratio (contradicting published data), forced response is resonant in pressure coefficient but not convergence length, and the free end ceases to be transversal under forcing, causing scalar free-boundary reduction to fail at parameter values previously published as periodic.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The established numerical schemes for annular liquid jet models differentiate the algebraic constraint on the state at the free end in time (index reduction), but this approach leaves the constraint unenforced and becomes degenerate in the steady limit. The authors seek to resolve this numerical deficiency and uncover potential inaccuracies in previously published results.

Method: The authors avoid index reduction entirely by absorbing the algebraic constraint identically through a judicious choice of dependent variables, yielding a reduced system with no constraints. This system is discretised using Chebyshev collocation, achieving geometric convergence. Unsteady solutions are verified a posteriori using residuals evaluated off the collocation nodes.

Result: The method recovers published steady data while revealing three findings: (1) convergence length strictly decreases with thickness-to-radius ratio for transversal free ends, contradicting a published table; (2) forced response is resonant in the pressure coefficient but not convergence length; (3) under forcing, the free end loses transversality in finite time, invalidating the scalar free-boundary reduction at parameter values published as periodic responses—with failure times aligning with near-vertical segments in published records, though coarse discretisations fail to detect this.

Conclusion: The proposed constraint-free formulation is superior to index-reduction approaches for annular liquid jet modeling, and it exposes significant errors in published results stemming from improper treatment of the free-end condition and detection failures in coarse discretisations.

Abstract: An annular liquid jet encloses a volume of gas and, when surface tension dominates inertia, closes on the symmetry axis at a finite distance. In the one-dimensional model of this configuration that distance is not a boundary condition but an algebraic constraint on the state at the free end, and the established schemes differentiate it in time and march the resulting equation for the domain length. We show that this index reduction leaves the constraint unenforced and, in the steady limit, degenerate, and we avoid it by absorbing the constraint identically in the choice of dependent variable. The reduced system carries no constraint and is discretised by Chebyshev collocation. Convergence is geometric, published steady data are recovered, and a residual evaluated off the collocation nodes verifies the unsteady solution a posteriori. Three results follow. For jets of finite thickness the convergence length decreases strictly with the thickness-to-radius ratio at the nozzle whenever the free end is transversal, which contradicts a published table and localises the discrepancy in the treatment of the free end. The forced response is resonant in the pressure coefficient but not in the convergence length. And under forcing the free end ceases to be transversal in finite time, so that the reduction of the free boundary to a single scalar fails, at parameter values published as periodic responses. In one of them the computed failure time coincides with a near-vertical segment of the published record, and coarse discretisations do not detect the failure at all.

</details>


### [216] [Assessing Nonlinear Elimination Preconditioning for Trust-Region Phase-Field Fracture](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05458)
*Tianchen Hu*

Main category: cs.CE

TL;DR: The paper presents an on-demand nonlinear-elimination preconditioned trust-region Newton method for phase-field fracture problems. The method uses an energy trust region for globalization, a primal-dual active set for irreversibility constraints, and selectively eliminates a coupled "hard set" of unknowns only when monolithic Newton stalls. This on-demand approach reduces outer nonlinear iterations by 17-25% compared to well-globalized monolithic Newton while bounding overhead, but shows no significant total-work advantage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Phase-field fracture simulations require solving bound-constrained minimization of nonconvex coupled displacement-damage energy. Monolithic Newton stalls when nonlinearity localizes at advancing crack fronts, while staggered (alternate-minimization) schemes converge slowly. Existing methods suffer from either stalling or slow convergence in this challenging regime.

Method: The method combines: (1) an energy Steihaug-Toint trust region for globalization, (2) a primal-dual active set for damage irreversibility, (3) a bound-constrained field-split sweep that eliminates an algebraically-identified "hard set" spanning both fields before steps, and (4) an on-demand trigger — elimination is applied only when coupled Newton stalls, otherwise the method reduces to monolithic Newton with no added cost.

Result: With the energy trust region globalization fixed, monolithic Newton completes all loading histories without cutbacks (where residual-merit Newton fails, alternate minimization stalls, and full-field sweep loses robustness). On-demand elimination cuts outer nonlinear iterations by 19-25% (brittle) and 17% (ductile); always-on elimination reaches 26-28% and ~39% at ductile nucleation. Measured via full-mesh-equivalent assembly-work proxy, the method is competitive with (within ~10% of) monolithic Newton, while always-on sweep adds up to 30% overhead.

Conclusion: Nonlinear elimination serves as an iteration-reduction mechanism whose overhead is bounded by the on-demand gating strategy. However, it demonstrates no total-work advantage over well-globalized monolithic Newton. The key insight is that energy trust-region globalization, rather than elimination, is the primary source of robustness.

Abstract: Each quasi-static load step of phase-field fracture is a bound-constrained minimization of a nonconvex, coupled displacement-damage energy under an irreversibility bound on the damage. Monolithic Newton stalls once the nonlinearity localizes at the advancing crack front, and staggered (alternate-minimization) schemes converge slowly there. We present an on-demand nonlinear-elimination preconditioned trust-region Newton method: an energy Steihaug-Toint trust region, a primal-dual active set for irreversibility, and a bound-constrained field-split sweep that eliminates an algebraically-identified "hard set" spanning both fields before each step. The elimination is applied on demand -- triggered by the coupled Newton's own stalling and otherwise skipped -- so the method reduces to monolithic Newton at no surcharge where the step is already healthy. We find the robustness to come from the energy trust region: with that globalization fixed, monolithic Newton already completes every loading history without cutbacks, where residual-merit Newton death-spirals, alternate minimization stalls, and the full-field sweep loses robustness. Against that well-globalized baseline, the on-demand elimination cuts outer nonlinear iterations by 19-25% (brittle) and 17% (ductile), with always-on elimination reaching 26-28% and about $39\%$ at the ductile nucleation step. Measured machine-independently, as a full-mesh-equivalent assembly-work proxy rather than wall-clock, it is competitive with -- not faster than -- monolithic Newton (within about 10%), whereas an always-on sweep adds up to 30%. Nonlinear elimination is thus an iteration-reduction mechanism whose overhead the on-demand gate bounds, with no demonstrated total-work advantage over well-globalized monolithic Newton.

</details>


### [217] [Compositional Aeroelastic Operators for Morphing Flexible Multibody Aircraft: A Geometric Framework with Structural Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05464)
*Gelin Chen,Chen Song,Chao Yang*

Main category: cs.CE

TL;DR: The paper develops a compositional formulation for morphing flexible multibody aircraft where lifting surfaces are assumed to maintain material attachment to geometrically exact beams. The formulation separates component root pose from section fields, uses exact relative logarithms for strain/potential energy and reference-anchored section coordinates for deformed geometry, and unifies beam balance, moving-surface potential flow, assembly, and power identities in a common twist-wrench convention. Verification confirms expected convergence order, and a cubic static-manifold correction reduces cantilever displacement error from 0.479 to 0.255.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Morphing flexible multibody aircraft require structural strain, aerodynamic geometry, surface velocity, and generalized loading to remain mutually compatible as joints and flexible components change configuration—a challenge that necessitates a compositional formulation linking each lifting surface to a geometrically exact beam.

Method: An assumed material attachment between each lifting surface and a geometrically exact beam is used. Component root pose is separated from the section field so that body strain and elastic potential depend only on the component's own elastic coordinates, while upstream motion enters kinetic terms and external-load pullbacks. The element-level exact relative logarithm d provides strain and potential energy, while a reference-anchored section coordinate σ provides deformed section geometry. Finite-order expansions retain finite reference geometry exactly while truncating only endpoint perturbations. The attachment map generates surface geometry, velocities, and force Jacobians from common section kinematics. Euler-Poincaré beam balance, moving-surface potential-flow relations, graph cotangent assembly, and the semidiscrete power identity are stated in a common twist-wrench convention.

Result: Verification yields the expected N_d+1 convergence order for degree-N_d relative-log expansions. In a geometrically nonlinear cantilever comparison, a cubic static-manifold correction reduces mean full-record displacement error from 0.479 to 0.255 over four completed load cases. Results provide structural and interface-level evidence rather than full aircraft aeroelastic validation.

Conclusion: The compositional approach yields a consistent structural and interface-level framework for morphing multibody aircraft, with verification confirming convergence behavior and improvement in nonlinear static accuracy, though full aircraft-level aeroelastic validation remains future work.

Abstract: Morphing flexible multibody aircraft require structural strain, aerodynamic geometry, surface velocity, and generalized loading to remain compatible as joints and flexible components change configuration. A compositional formulation is developed around an assumed material attachment between each lifting surface and a geometrically exact beam. Separating the component root pose from the section field shows that the body strain and elastic potential of a component depend on its own elastic coordinates, while upstream motion enters kinetic terms and external-load pullbacks. At element level, an exact relative logarithm $d$ supplies strain and potential energy, whereas a reference-anchored section coordinate $σ$ supplies deformed section geometry. Finite-order expansions retain the finite reference geometry exactly and truncate only endpoint perturbations. The attachment map then generates surface points, tangents, normals, velocities, and force Jacobians from common section kinematics. Euler--Poincare beam balance, moving-surface potential-flow relations, graph cotangent assembly, and the associated semidiscrete power identity are stated in a common twist--wrench convention. Collocation, pressure, equivalent-load, and structural-station sites are distinguished to expose their approximation errors. Verification gives the expected $N_d+1$ convergence order for degree-$N_d$ relative-log expansions. In a geometrically nonlinear cantilever comparison, a cubic static-manifold correction reduces mean full-record displacement error from $0.479$ to $0.255$ over four completed load cases. These results provide structural and interface-level evidence rather than validation of a complete aircraft aeroelastic prediction.

</details>


### [218] [Robust topology optimization with non-Gaussian material fields using polygonal finite elements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05466)
*Nilton Cuellar,Anderson Pereira,Ivan F. M. Menezes,Americo Cunha*

Main category: cs.CE

TL;DR: A computational framework for robust topology optimization combining polygonal finite elements, spatially correlated non-Gaussian random material fields (via memoryless transformation of truncated Karhunen-Loève expansion), and non-intrusive polynomial-chaos surrogates. The approach is physically consistent (positive stiffness), computationally efficient (order of magnitude fewer FE analyses than intrusive/Monte Carlo), and demonstrates that non-Gaussian material variability causes systematic load-path redistribution with 6-12% structural volume reallocation and reduced compliance scatter.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a topology optimization framework that accounts for realistic, non-Gaussian spatial material uncertainty in a physically consistent and computationally tractable manner, overcoming limitations of Gaussian assumptions and the high cost of intrusive or sampling-based uncertainty quantification.

Method: (1) Model spatial Young's modulus uncertainty as a homogeneous non-Gaussian random field using a memoryless (translation) transformation of a truncated Karhunen-Loève expansion, preserving the prescribed autocovariance while ensuring positive stiffness. (2) Use polygonal finite-element discretizations for stable density-based topology optimization on unstructured meshes, mitigating checkerboard artifacts and mesh bias. (3) Employ sparse polynomial-chaos expansions as non-intrusive surrogates to efficiently estimate low-order statistical moments required by the robust objective.

Result: On cantilever and curved beam benchmarks, non-Gaussian material variability induces systematic load-path redistribution with 6-12% structural volume reallocation and reduced compliance scatter. The non-intrusive polynomial-chaos surrogate reproduces intrusive reference results within 3% while requiring an order of magnitude fewer full finite-element analyses.

Conclusion: The proposed framework provides a physically consistent and computationally efficient route to robust topology-optimized designs that remain reliable under realistic (non-Gaussian) material uncertainty, offering a practical alternative to intrusive or Monte Carlo approaches.

Abstract: We present a computational framework for robust topology optimization that integrates polygonal finite-element discretizations, spatially correlated non-Gaussian material modeling, and non-intrusive polynomial-chaos surrogates. Spatial uncertainty in Young's modulus is represented as a homogeneous non-Gaussian random field obtained via a memoryless transformation of a truncated Karhunen-Loève expansion, ensuring physical admissibility through positivity of stiffness while preserving the prescribed autocovariance. Polygonal finite elements provide a stable discretization for density-based optimization on unstructured meshes and mitigate checkerboard artefacts and mesh bias, while the sparse polynomial-chaos expansion enables efficient estimation of low-order statistical moments required by the robust objective at a fraction of the cost of intrusive or Monte Carlo approaches. Numerical studies on a cantilever and a curved beam show that introducing non-Gaussian material variability leads to systematic load-path redistribution and a reallocation of 6-12% of the structural volume, together with a reduction in compliance scatter. The non-intrusive surrogate reproduces intrusive reference results within 3% using an order of magnitude fewer full finite-element analyses. These results demonstrate that the proposed framework offers a physically consistent and computationally efficient route to topology-optimized designs that remain reliable under realistic material uncertainty.

</details>


### [219] [Verification, Sensitivity, and Operating Limits of a GPU-Accelerated Planar WCSPH Model for Hydrodynamic Ram](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05494)
*Md Mujahid Alam,Debojit Biswas,Sukanta Chakraborty*

Main category: cs.CE

TL;DR: This paper presents a planar 2D GPU-accelerated WCSPH solver with an Adami-type ghost-particle boundary condition, extended for the first time to a moving, decelerating disk penetrating a confined liquid channel at ballistic velocity. The work validates the boundary treatment against analytical solutions, performs a convergence study, and provides comprehensive characterization of the solver's operating regime for hydrodynamic ram loading.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hydrodynamic ram (HRAM) loading remains challenging due to difficulty in achieving convincing quantitative agreement in reduced-order representations. Prevailing studies typically use a single mesh and boundary treatment validated against one dataset, leaving unresolved whether agreement reflects genuine fidelity or compensating discretization errors.

Method: A planar 2D GPU-accelerated WCSPH solver is developed using an Adami-type ghost-particle boundary condition, extended to a moving, decelerating disk in a confined liquid channel. The boundary condition is validated against a potential-flow added-mass solution and analytical acoustic reflection coefficient. A three-point resolution convergence study, coefficient sweep, conservation diagnostics, and O(N) GPU throughput scaling are performed.

Result: The stiff equation of state and cavitation cutoff delineate the solver's operating regime. A non-monotone near-probe pressure peak was found due to bounded ghost-fluid density inconsistency. Energy diagnostics reveal modest total energy growth, with the fluid absorbing disproportionately more energy than the projectile relinquishes, indicating prescribed non-back-reacting projectile kinematics.

Conclusion: The solver, while confined to planar geometry, delivers verified, convergence-checked performance well suited for rapid parametric design exploration in HRAM applications.

Abstract: Hydrodynamic ram (HRAM) loading remains a persistent challenge in impact mechanics, less due to exotic physics than to the difficulty of achieving convincing quantitative agreement in reduced-order representations. Prevailing studies typically employ a single mesh and boundary treatment validated against one experimental dataset, leaving unresolved whether agreement reflects genuine fidelity or compensating discretization errors. This work presents a planar 2D, GPU-accelerated WCSPH solver employing an Adami-type ghost-particle boundary condition, extended for the first time to a moving, decelerating disk penetrating a confined liquid channel at ballistic velocity. The boundary condition is examined against a purpose-derived potential-flow added-mass solution and the analytical acoustic reflection coefficient at the steel-water interface; this exploration reveals that the stiff equation of state and cavitation cutoff impose identifiable delineation of the solver's operating regime on quantitative agreement. A three-point resolution convergence study reveals a non-monotone near-probe pressure peak, attributed to a bounded ghost-fluid density inconsistency. A comprehensive coefficient sweep, conservation diagnostics, and O(N) GPU throughput scaling enable exhaustive characterization impractical in 3D. Qualitative comparison against literature evidence at 900 and 600m/s clarifies load bearing versus approximate elements. Notably, energy diagnostics reveal a modest total energy growth over the simulated window, with the fluid absorbing disproportionately more energy than the projectile relinquishes - an instructive signature of prescribed, non-back-reacting projectile kinematics that helps map the formulation's operating envelope. The resulting solver, while confined to planar geometry, delivers verified, convergence-checked performance well suited for rapid parametric design exploration.

</details>


### [220] [Buildability Assessment of 3D-Printed Concrete Structures Using Smoothed Mohr-Coulomb Plasticity with Isotropic Hardening](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07679)
*Saif-Ur-Rehman,Annika Robens-Radermacher,R. J. M. Wolfs,Jörg F. Unger*

Main category: cs.CE

TL;DR: This paper presents a computational framework for predicting buildability in 3D concrete printing, combining a smoothed Mohr-Coulomb yield surface with nonlinear isotropic cohesion hardening in an updated Lagrangian setting. The framework accurately reproduces experimental wall collapse, predicts hollow cylinder collapse better than existing models, and reveals that slender vs. intermediate cylinder failures represent limits of a single failure process rather than distinct mechanisms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Extrusion-based 3D concrete printing requires freshly deposited material to support its own growing weight while still hardening. Predicting buildability (maximum printable layers) depends critically on accurate constitutive modeling of fresh concrete. Existing finite element frameworks only pair gradual pre-failure hardening with smooth yield surfaces, restricting the Mohr-Coulomb surface—which best describes fresh concrete—to perfect plasticity, limiting prediction accuracy.

Method: The framework combines a smoothed Mohr-Coulomb yield surface with nonlinear isotropic hardening of cohesion, formulated in an updated Lagrangian setting with an activation field for layer deposition. Failure is detected via a tangent-stiffness eigenvalue criterion that matches the analytical self-weight buckling load of slender walls and is insensitive to geometric imperfection.

Result: The framework reproduces measured collapse of a printed straight wall within one layer accuracy and predicts hollow cylinder collapse closer to experiment than existing frameworks. A parametric study across six diameters and six hardening rates shows buildability peaks at intermediate diameters and increases with hardening rate. Slender cylinders fail with significant strength in reserve while intermediate ones use nearly all of their strength.

Conclusion: The two observed failure modes (slender and intermediate) are correctly interpreted as limits of a single failure process rather than separate failure mechanisms, unifying the understanding of buildability failure in 3D concrete printing.

Abstract: Extrusion-based 3D concrete printing builds structural elements layer upon layer without formwork, so freshly deposited material must carry the growing weight of the structure while it is still gaining strength and stiffness. The largest number of layers that can be printed before failure, the buildability, is therefore central to process design. Its prediction rests above all on how accurately the constitutive model represents fresh concrete. Existing finite element frameworks pair gradual pre-failure hardening only with smooth yield surfaces, which leaves the Mohr-Coulomb surface that best describes fresh concrete restricted to perfect plasticity. This work combines a smoothed Mohr-Coulomb yield surface with nonlinear isotropic hardening of the cohesion, formulated in an updated Lagrangian setting with an activation field for layer deposition. Failure is identified from a tangent-stiffness eigenvalue criterion that matches the analytical self-weight buckling load of a slender wall and is insensitive to geometric imperfection. The framework reproduces the measured collapse of a printed straight wall within one layer and predicts the collapse of a hollow cylinder closer to experiment than existing frameworks. A study over six diameters and six hardening rates shows buildability peaking at intermediate diameters and rising with the hardening rate. Recording how much of its strength the material has used at failure, slender cylinders fail with much still in reserve while intermediate ones use nearly all of it. The two failure modes are therefore identified as the limits of one process rather than separate failure mechanisms.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [221] [PhysSAE: Mechanistic Interpretability with Sparse Autoencoders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07061)
*Nandita N. Patil,Eshwar R. A.,Gajanan V. Honnavar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PhysSAE is a mechanistic interpretability framework that trains sparse autoencoders on PINN penultimate-layer activations to discover and causally validate physically meaningful latent features in physics-informed neural networks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: PINNs embed PDE residuals into neural network training but their internal representations are opaque—it is unknown what physical features hidden layers encode or whether those features have a localized causal role in the network's predictions.

Method: The framework trains overcomplete sparse autoencoders (SAEs) on PINN penultimate-layer activations, then evaluates dictionary atoms via direct causal intervention in the frozen hidden state (h_cf = h − αz_k d_k), bypassing the SAE decoder. Evaluated across six PDE families with multiple PINN and SAE seeds.

Result: SAE atoms align with independently-defined physical observables (max Pearson |r|=0.951, always ≫ permutation null); top-aligned atom ablation yields 1.2–4.2× more spatially concentrated causal footprints than PCA/ICA; top-aligned atoms outperform controls on causal localization (ESF_80 advantage 0.04–0.44); two-atom bilateral representations improve R² by 0.05–0.15 over single atoms.

Conclusion: PINNs develop sparse, physically structured latent representations that can be identified and causally interrogated post-hoc, opening a path toward interpretability-aware scientific machine learning.

Abstract: Physics-Informed Neural Networks (PINNs) embed PDE residuals into neural network training, but their internal representations remain opaque: it is unknown what physical features their hidden layers encode or whether those features have a localized causal role. We present PhysSAE, a mechanistic interpretability framework that trains overcomplete sparse autoencoders (SAEs) on PINN penultimate-layer activations and evaluates dictionary atoms through direct causal intervention in the original frozen hidden state: $h_{\mathrm{cf}} = h - αz_k d_k$, bypassing the SAE decoder entirely. Across six PDE families, with 3 PINN seeds and 3 SAE seeds each---we show that (i) Our discovered SAE atoms align with independently-defined physical observables (max Pearson $|r|=0.951$, always $\gg$ permutation null), (ii) the causal footprint of top-aligned atom ablation is 1.2--4.2$\times$ more spatially concentrated canonical than PCA or ICA interventions, and (iii) top-aligned atoms outperform matched random controls on causal localization for structured physical concepts (ESF$_{80}$ advantage 0.04-0.44). Two-atom bilateral representations improve concept regression R$^2$ by $ΔR^2\!=\!0.05\text{-}0.15$ over single atoms, while random pairs decrease it by up to 0.60. These results demonstrate that PINNs develop sparse, physically structured latent representations that can be identified and causally interrogated post-hoc, opening a path toward interpretability-aware scientific machine learning.

</details>


### [222] [Constitutive State-Space Modeling of Path-Dependent Plasticity: A Resolution-Consistent and Parallelizable Computational Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07294)
*Rui Barreira,Taylan Soydan,Francesco Scipione,Miguel A. Bessa,Dirk Mohr*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces a Constitutive State Space (CSS) model using structured state-space dynamics as an incremental constitutive operator, achieving higher accuracy, faster training, better discretization robustness, and parallel-scan efficiency compared to recurrent neural network-based approaches for path-dependent plasticity modeling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Data-driven constitutive models using nonlinear RNNs suffer from two key limitations: (1) sequential state evolution prevents parallel training, and (2) predictions are sensitive to the discretization of the applied strain path. There is a need for a model that is both trainable in parallel and robust to strain-path resolution.

Method: The CSS model decomposes the strain increment into magnitude and direction. The loading direction drives the latent state-space system, while the increment magnitude enters the zero-order-hold discretization of the continuous-time linear recurrence. This design guarantees stationarity under zero increments, reduces sensitivity to strain-path resolution, and leverages the parallel-scan structure of S5 for efficient training on long histories.

Result: CSS matches or exceeds MSC accuracy across four multiaxial path-dependent material models, achieving up to an order of magnitude lower validation losses for plastically incompressible materials. CSS maintains low errors across large changes in strain-path discretization (unlike MSC) and trains substantially faster with fewer training pairs. Analysis of the learned latent state reveals structure consistent with the physical dimensionality of the constitutive models.

Conclusion: Mechanics-tailored structured state-space dynamics provide an efficient and discretization-robust computational framework for data-driven constitutive modeling of path-dependent plasticity, outperforming RNN-based alternatives.

Abstract: Data-driven constitutive models for path-dependent plasticity are commonly formulated using nonlinear recurrent neural networks, whose sequential state evolution limits parallel training and whose predictions may depend on the discretization of the applied strain path. We introduce a Constitutive State Space (CSS) model that reformulates structured state-space dynamics as an incremental constitutive operator. The strain increment is decomposed into magnitude and direction: the loading direction drives the latent state-space system, while the increment magnitude enters the zero-order-hold discretization of its continuous-time linear recurrence. This mechanics-tailored construction guarantees stationarity under zero increments, strongly reduces sensitivity to strain-path resolution, and retains the parallel-scan structure of S5 for efficient training on long constitutive histories. The CSS and Minimal State Cell (MSC) architectures are compared for four multiaxial path-dependent material models including isotropic J2 plasticity, pressure-sensitive foam plasticity, and combined isotropic-kinematic hardening. CSS matches or exceeds the prediction accuracy of the MSC, including one order of magnitude lower validation losses for the plastically incompressible materials. Importantly, CSS maintains low errors across large changes in strain-path discretization, whereas the MSC error increases substantially when evaluated at coarser resolutions than used for training. CSS trains substantially faster and requires fewer strain-stress pairs to attain comparable or better accuracy. Analysis of the learned state further reveals latent structure consistent with the dimensionality of the underlying physical constitutive models. These results establish mechanics-tailored structured state-space dynamics as a computational framework for efficient and discretization-robust data-driven constitutive modeling.

</details>


### [223] [Latent-MoE: Domain-Aware Mixture-of-Experts for PDEs with Multi-Regime Physics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07814)
*Hanwen Wang,Paris Perdikaris*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper identifies that PINNs fail on PDEs with spatially varying physics because standard coordinate networks have translation-variant NTKs causing long-range coupling. It proposes Latent-MoE, which uses mixture-of-experts blocks with centered compact-support routers to achieve a uniformly banded NTK that localizes learning, while allowing capacity to flow across regions via a shared backbone.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: PINNs struggle on PDEs whose governing physics varies across the domain. The authors trace this to a structural property of standard coordinate networks: their neural tangent kernel is translation-variant, allowing training points of large coordinate magnitude to disproportionately influence predictions elsewhere, producing long-range coupling and gradient conflict during training.

Method: The paper shows analytically and empirically that mixture-of-experts (MoE) architectures with centered, compact-support routers yield a uniformly banded NTK whose kernel-regression weights decay exponentially with distance, localizing learning. Based on this, they propose Latent-MoE, which interleaves domain-aware MoE blocks within a shared backbone, preserving localization while allowing capacity to flow across regions.

Result: Latent-MoE is competitive with established baselines on standard homogeneous-physics benchmarks. On benchmarks with multi-stage time-variable physics, it improves over global models and rigid domain decompositions by more than an order of magnitude, with markedly reduced gradient conflict during training.

Conclusion: Rigid domain partitioning (as in FB-PINNs or X-PINNs) is not necessary for localization; instead, a shared backbone with domain-aware MoE routing achieves both localization and capacity sharing, significantly improving on multi-stage time-variable physics problems.

Abstract: Physics-informed neural networks (PINNs) struggle on PDEs whose governing physics varies across the domain. We trace this to a structural property of standard coordinate networks: their neural tangent kernel (NTK) is translation-variant and lets training points of large coordinate magnitude disproportionately influence predictions elsewhere, producing long-range coupling and gradient conflict during training. We show analytically and empirically that mixture-of-experts (MoE) architectures with centered, compact-support routers yield a uniformly banded NTK whose kernel-regression weights decay exponentially with distance, localizing the learning. Building on this, we propose \emph{Latent-MoE}, which interleaves domain-aware MoE blocks within a shared backbone. Unlike FB-PINNs or X-PINNs, which rigidly partition both the domain and the parameters so that the parameters on different subdomains are updated independently, Latent-MoE is designed to preserve the localization benefit of domain-aware routing while allowing capacity to flow across regions through the shared backbone. On standard homogeneous-physics benchmarks Latent-MoE is competitive with established baselines; on benchmarks with multi-stage time-variable physics, where global models and rigid domain decompositions both fall into spurious solutions, it improves over them by more than an order of magnitude, with markedly reduced gradient conflict during training.

</details>


### [224] [Learning Metamaterial Eigenmodes with Wavelet-Encoded Fourier Neural Operators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08102)
*Han Zhang,Alexander Ogren,Cynthia Rudin,Johann Guilleminot,L. Catherine Brinson*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper shows that Fourier Neural Operators (FNOs) with wavelet-based input encodings can learn and predict multiple eigenmodes of the elastic wave equation in metamaterial geometries, accelerating simulation by three orders of magnitude compared to finite element analysis while maintaining high fidelity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard operator learning formulations assume a unique input-output map, making eigenvalue problems difficult because an eigenparameter and one of several valid eigenmodes must be solved simultaneously. There is a need for surrogates that can handle multi-mode PDE problems.

Method: Combine Fourier Neural Operators (FNOs) with wavelet-based encodings of PDE inputs to learn and predict multiple eigenmodes of the elastic wave equation in arbitrary metamaterial geometries.

Result: Wavelet encodings enable deterministic mode selection on both continuous-valued and binary-valued geometries within a single model, and accelerate metamaterial design simulation by three orders of magnitude relative to FEM while preserving high fidelity.

Conclusion: Wavelet encodings are well matched to the dual spatial-spectral structure of FNOs, providing a mechanistic explanation for multi-mode prediction. The approach has broader implications for designing input encodings in other multi-mode PDE solvers based on spectral neural operators.

Abstract: Machine learning surrogates based on neural operators have shown broad applicability in solving forward PDE problems. However, eigenvalue problems, in which an eigenparameter and one of several valid eigenmodes must be simultaneously solved, remain difficult because standard operator learning formulations assume a unique input-output map. This work demonstrates that Fourier Neural Operators (FNOs), combined with wavelet-based encodings of PDE inputs, can learn and predict multiple eigenmodes of the elastic wave equation, corresponding to deformation modes of acoustic waves propagating through arbitrary metamaterial geometries. We provide a mechanistic explanation and experimental evidence for why wavelet encodings are well matched to the dual spatial-spectral structure of the FNO, enabling deterministic mode selection on both continuous-valued and binary-valued geometries within a single model, and for why prediction accuracy varies with geometric discontinuities. For metamaterial design, the resulting surrogate accelerates the simulation stage of the design cycle by three orders of magnitude relative to finite element analysis on a consumer-grade CPU, while preserving high fidelity. These results also carry broader implications for designing input encodings in other multi-mode PDE solvers based on spectral neural operators.

</details>


### [225] [Multi-Level-Set-Based Physics-Driven Neural Network to Solve 3-D Inverse Scattering Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08594)
*Yutong Du,Zicheng Liu,Bo Qi,Yali Zong,Peixian Han*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces LSPDNN, a level-set-based physics-driven neural network solver for 3-D electromagnetic inverse scattering that uses multiple neural level-set components with a soft-union multi-material model to reconstruct scatterers with clear boundaries and uniform material regions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous voxel-wise contrast reconstruction methods in electromagnetic inverse scattering suffer from boundary blurring and reconstruction artifacts. The authors aim to leverage the piecewise homogeneity of practical scatterers to improve reconstruction quality.

Method: LSPDNN represents unknown targets using multiple coordinate-dependent neural level-set components. A soft-union multi-material model separates object support (formed by union of level-set components) from material distribution (determined by normalized component weights and learnable complex permittivity candidates). Model-consistent total variation regularization is applied to material-region indicators rather than directly to reconstructed contrast. An adaptive loss balancing strategy reduces reliance on manually selected regularization weights, and parameters are optimized by minimizing a physics-consistent objective function.

Result: Numerical and experimental results show LSPDNN reconstructs scatterers with clear boundaries, more uniform material regions, and substantially reduced background artifacts, particularly effective in challenging 3-D cases with irregular shapes, closely spaced objects, multiple materials, and measurement noise.

Conclusion: Neural level-set parameterization demonstrates significant advantages for 3-D electromagnetic inverse scattering, overcoming limitations of voxel-wise reconstruction approaches.

Abstract: This paper proposes a level-set-based physics-driven neural network solver (LSPDNN) for 3-D electromagnetic inverse scattering. To mitigate boundary blurring and reconstruction artifacts in voxel-wise contrast reconstruction, the proposed solver exploits the piecewise homogeneity of practical scatterers by representing unknown targets with multiple coordinate-dependent neural level-set components. Specifically, a soft-union multi-material model is proposed to separately describe the object support and material distribution. The global support is formed by the union of multiple level-set components, while the local contrast is determined by normalized component weights and learnable complex permittivity candidates. In addition, a model-consistent total variation (TV) regularization is imposed on the material-region indicators, rather than directly on the reconstructed contrast, to suppress fragmented material assignments without excessively smoothing material interfaces. An adaptive loss balancing strategy is further introduced to reduce the dependence on manually selected regularization weights. For each measurement instance, the neural level-set parameters and material candidates are optimized by minimizing a physics-consistent objective function. Numerical and experimental results demonstrate that LSPDNN can reconstruct scatterers with clear boundaries, more uniform material regions, and substantially reduced background artifacts. The results highlight the advantage of the neural level-set parameterization in challenging 3-D inverse scattering cases involving irregular shapes, closely spaced objects, multiple materials, and measurement noise.

</details>


### [226] [Nonlinear elliptic homogenization with the parametric Deep Ritz method](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05778)
*Conor Rowan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper applies the parametric Deep Ritz method to solve parametric cell problems arising in nonlinear elliptic homogenization, providing a continuous and differentiable neural network representation of the cell response over macroscopic states that accelerates macroscale solutions compared to traditional FE² methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Nonlinear homogenization requires solving parametric cell problems because the homogenized constitutive relation depends on macroscopic state and/or its gradient. A differentiable representation of the cell solution is needed for Newton iterations at the macroscale, but traditional approaches are computationally expensive.

Method: The authors exploit the variational structure of the cell problem and use the parametric Deep Ritz method with a neural network that discretizes both spatial coordinates and the macroscopic state. Boundary conditions are enforced strongly, enabling simultaneous solution of the cell problem across a range of macroscopic states.

Result: The method is shown to be accurate and efficient, offering a continuous and differentiable representation of the cell response over the macroscopic state and gradient. This representation significantly expedites macroscale solutions when compared to a traditional FE² scheme.

Conclusion: The parametric Deep Ritz method is a promising approach for nonlinear homogenization, providing computational gains at the macroscale through an efficient, differentiable surrogate for the parametric cell problem.

Abstract: Elliptic homogenization is used to determine coarse-grained properties of materials with features on small scales. When these small scale features have rapid, periodic fluctuations, the solution field corresponding to a homogenized constitutive relation closely resembles the true solution based on the heterogeneous material. This homogenized behavior of the material is computed from a cell problem, where a cell is defined to be one period of the fluctuating material. In the context of linear elliptic partial differential equations, the homogenized constitutive relation is defined simply by a constant coefficient tensor, but for nonlinear problems, the homogenized response depends on the macroscopic state and/or its gradient, thus requiring solutions to parametric cell problems. When computing a numerical solution with the homogenized constitutive relation, it is useful to have a differentiable representation of the solution to the cell problem, as derivatives of the homogenized constitutive relation are required in Newton iterations for the macroscopic state field. In this work, we use the Deep Ritz method to solve the parametric cell problems that arise from nonlinear homogenization. First, we exploit the variational structure of the cell problem, then we discretize the dependence of the cell response on both space and the macroscopic state with a neural network. Enforcing boundary conditions on the cell response strongly, we next use the parametric Deep Ritz method to simultaneously solve the cell problem over a range of macroscopic states. We show that this method is accurate, efficient, and offers a continuous and differentiable representation of the cell response over the macroscopic state and gradient. We then show that our parametric representation of the cell response significantly expedites macroscale solutions when compared to a traditional $\text{FE}^2$ scheme.

</details>


<div id='math-ph'></div>

# math-ph [[Back]](#toc)

### [227] [Asymptotics of Ground States for Helium-Like and Bosonic Atoms in a Two-Cluster Region](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08257)
*Yukimi Goto*

Main category: math-ph

TL;DR: This paper analyzes the asymptotic behavior of positive ground states of N-particle atomic Coulomb Hamiltonians in a two-cluster region, proving precise convergence rates of the N-particle ground state to the product of the one-particle density and (N-1)-particle ground state as a particle approaches infinity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding the asymptotic structure of N-particle atomic ground states in cluster regions is fundamental for rigorous multiscale and multiparticle quantum mechanics, particularly for establishing decay and factorization properties needed in scattering theory and electronic structure analysis.

Method: The authors study the normalized ground state ψ of an N-particle Coulomb Hamiltonian, comparing ψ(x₁,...,x_N)/√ρ(x_N) with the (N-1)-particle ground state φ. They derive asymptotic expansion rates in two regimes: (i) |x_i| ≤ |x_N|^α for 0<α<1, giving O(|x_N|^{-2+α} ln|x_N|) error; and (ii) |x_i| ≤ R₀ ln|x_N|, giving O(|x_N|^{-2}(ln|x_N|)²) error. Results hold both below the essential spectrum and at threshold.

Result: The paper establishes that in the region |x_i| ≤ |x_N|^α (0<α<1), the scaled N-particle ground state converges to φ with rate O(|x_N|^{-2+α} ln|x_N|). In the logarithmic region |x_i| ≤ R₀ ln|x_N|, convergence rate improves to O(|x_N|^{-2}(ln|x_N|)²). An L² error bound of O(|x_N|^{-2}) is also established, valid both below the essential spectrum and at threshold.

Conclusion: The results provide rigorous quantitative control on the cluster decomposition of N-particle Coulomb ground states, demonstrating fast polynomial convergence with explicit error rates in different spatial regimes, covering both energies below the essential spectrum and at threshold.

Abstract: We study the asymptotic behavior of positive ground states of $N$-particle atomic Coulomb Hamiltonians in a two-cluster region. For the $N$-particle ground state $ψ$, its one-particle density $ρ$, and $(N-1)$-particle ground state $φ$, we show that for $0<α<1$, as $|x_N|\to\infty$, $ψ(x_1,\dots,x_N)/\sqrt{ρ(x_N)}= φ(x_1,\dots,x_{N-1})+O(|x_N|^{-2+α} \ln |x_N|)$ uniformly in the region $|x_i|\le |x_N|^α$. In the region $|x_i|\le R_0\ln |x_N|$, the convergence rate is $O(|x_N|^{-2} (\ln |x_N|)^2)$. We also establish an $O(|x_N|^{-2})$ bound on the squared $L^2$ error. These results hold both below the essential spectrum and at threshold.

</details>


<div id='cs.CV'></div>

# cs.CV [[Back]](#toc)

### [228] [Poisson Image Denoising Using Minimax Concave and Reweighted $\ell_1$ Penalties: Nonblind and Blind Approaches](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07916)
*Reza Parvaz*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes nonblind and blind image deblurring methods under Poisson noise using reweighted ℓ1-regularized fractional-order derivatives combined with the Minimax Concave Penalty (MCP), solved via an ADMM-based algorithm with convergence analysis, and validated through extensive experiments on various images.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Images used in medical and astronomical sciences suffer from Poisson noise and blur, reducing their quality. Image restoration becomes challenging especially in blind cases (unknown PSF), and the choice of penalty function critically affects performance of methods like Total Variation. The authors aim to improve edge preservation and address both blind and nonblind deblurring under Poisson noise.

Method: The method employs a reweighted ℓ1-regularization of fractional-order derivatives for better edge preservation, combined with the Minimax Concave Penalty (MCP)—a continuous, sparsity-promoting, nearly unbiased regularizer. This yields a nonconvex optimization model solved via an Alternating Direction Method of Multipliers (ADMM)-based algorithm, with convergence analysis provided.

Result: The effectiveness of the proposed algorithm is demonstrated through extensive experiments on various images, showing improved performance in both blind and nonblind deblurring scenarios under Poisson noise.

Conclusion: The proposed approach successfully addresses image deblurring under Poisson noise with better edge preservation, handling both blind and nonblind cases, supported by an efficient and convergent numerical algorithm.

Abstract: Images are important tools in various sciences. Despite the development of photo-taking tools, creating clear and image without noise remains challenging in practice. In particular, Poisson noise has an effect on medical and astronomical images, and reduces their quality. Additionally, blur is another factor that has an effect on image quality. The problem of image restoration becomes very complicated when we have no information about the Point Spread Function (PSF). These types of problems are known as blind case. However, in some images, such as some astronomical images, the type of PSF can be specified, and these types of problems are known as nonblind problems. Total Variation (TV) is a widely used method for solving such inverse problems, where the selection of the penalty function is the most critical factor that affects the method's performance. In this paper, to improve edge preservation, we employ a reweighted $\ell_1$-regularization of the fractional order derivative. Furthermore, we propose a nonblind and blind image deblurring approach under Poisson noise using the Minimax Concave Penalty (MCP), which is a continuous, sparsity promoting, and nearly unbiased regularizer. This formulation leads to a nonconvex optimization model. To solve the proposed model, we introduce an efficient numerical algorithm based on the Alternating Direction Method of Multipliers (ADMM) and provide an analysis of its convergence. Finally, the effectiveness of the proposed algorithm are demonstrated through extensive experiments on various images.

</details>
