### [229] [Mixed quantum-classical evolution in open molecular systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07272)
*Michael Galperin,Abraham Nitzan*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: The paper develops a mixed quantum-classical formulation for open nonequilibrium molecular systems using pseudoparticle nonequilibrium Green's function (PP-NEGF) as the quantum starting point, from which a density-matrix equation of motion for quantum-classical dynamics is derived. The authors compare with prior formulations, highlight key differences arising from the nonequilibrium/open character, and critically analyze the approximations underlying fewest-switches surface hopping (FSSH), showing they become unreasonable when adiabatic surfaces approach each other.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a unifying quantum-classical framework for open nonequilibrium molecular systems (e.g., molecular junctions) and to rigorously assess the validity of commonly used approximate methods like FSSH when applied to such systems.

Method: The authors start from the fully quantum PP-NEGF formalism and introduce classical nuclear dynamics to derive a density-matrix equation of motion. They compare this formulation to prior quantum-classical approaches and systematically examine the approximations needed to reduce the dynamics to FSSH.

Result: A new quantum-classical density-matrix equation of motion is derived from PP-NEGF, showing important differences from previous formulations due to the nonequilibrium and open nature of the system. The analysis reveals that FSSH relies on approximations that break down when adiabatic surfaces become nearly degenerate.

Conclusion: The derived formulation provides a more rigorous basis for quantum-classical dynamics in open systems, and the analysis cautions against the use of FSSH when adiabatic surfaces are close in energy, motivating the need for more accurate methods in such regimes.

Abstract: We consider a mixed quantum-classical formulation for open nonequilibrium molecular systems. We employ the pseudoparticle nonequilibrium Green's function (PP-NEGF) method as a fully quantum description and a starting point for introducing classical nuclear dynamics for an open system. We use this formulation to derive a density-matrix equation of motion describing the quantum-classical evolution. We compare our results with previous formulations and highlight important differences related to the nonequilibrium and open character of the molecular system. We also analyze the approximations needed to reduce full quantum-classical dynamics to the fewest-switches surface hopping (FSSH) method and show that these approximations are unreasonable when adiabatic surfaces come close to each other.

</details>


<div id='nucl-th'></div>

# nucl-th [[Back]](#toc)

### [230] [Science Of Nuclear Fusion: Insights and Ideas](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01366)
*Johann Rafelski,Andrew Steinmetz*

Main category: nucl-th

TL;DR: This paper surveys both legacy and novel table-top approaches to nuclear fusion, with emphasis on aneutronic fusion reaction chains. It examines proton-boron fusion advances, proposes a new beryllium-helium-3 aneutronic option, and explores plasmonic field-enhancement, muon-catalyzed fusion, and the challenges of harnessing D-T fusion for civilian use and space travel.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore novel paths toward smaller-scale, higher energy density fusion opportunities that could advance small systems for nuclear fusion, addressing both legacy approaches and emerging table-top techniques.

Method: A survey approach covering multiple fusion pathways: solar fusion cycle analogs (aneutronic chains), analysis of natural isotopic abundances for the "limitless energy" claim, laser-driven proton-boron fusion, a proposed proton-boron-nitride cycle, a novel beryllium and helium-3 (3-helium) target mixture, plasmonic opto-electric field enhancement, muon-catalyzed fusion, and D-T fusion challenges.

Result: The survey highlights achievements in laser-driven proton-boron fusion and proposes extensions to self-sustaining nearly fully aneutronic proton-boron-nitride cycles. It introduces helium-3 as a central fusion component, proposes a beryllium-3-helium aneutronic option, and discusses practical challenges in both magnetic and inertial confinement fusion contexts.

Conclusion: Aneutronic fusion pathways offer promising alternatives for advancing small-scale fusion systems, with potential applications extending to space travel, though D-T fusion still presents significant challenges for civilian use.

Abstract: Advances in several physics domains open up novel paths to smaller scale, higher energy density opportunities to advance small systems for nuclear fusion. Here we survey both legacy and several novel ``table-top'' approaches which attract current interest. We furthermore address a few related practical and challenging nuclear science topics arising in the context of magnetic confinement and inertial confinement fusion. The contents emphasis includes: By example of solar fusion cycles we draw attention to aneutronic fusion reaction chains. Considering the natural isotopic abundances we assess more carefully the meaning of the term ``limitless energy'' in the context of actual fusion power realizations. We describe achievements in laser-driven proton-boron fusion, and extensions to a self-sustaining and nearly fully aneutronic proton-boron-nitride reaction cycle. We propose another aneutronic option, where the target is a mix of beryllium and light helium isotope; this 3-helium is arguably the most mentioned fusion component in this article. We look in depth at the plasmonic opto-electric field-enhancement for fusion, and at the particle (muon) catalyzed fusion option. We describe problems in harnessing the dt fusion for civilian use. We introduce space travel as forthcoming application of aneutronic fusion.

</details>


<div id='physics.ins-det'></div>

# physics.ins-det [[Back]](#toc)

### [231] [Real-time blind indexing by cross-frame consensus](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07722)
*Stefano Marchesini,Yuan Ni*

Main category: physics.ins-det

TL;DR: GLINT is a new approach for indexing serial crystallography data that treats the entire dataset (rather than individual frames) as the unit of inference, pooling weak but recurrent lattice hypotheses across many frames to identify a shared unit cell. It excels in the "small-N regime" where individual frames have too few reflections for reliable blind indexing, and can register each frame against the consensus cell via a lower-dimensionality known-cell search. It achieves similar indexing yield to the best blind indexer at ~40x lower latency, runs fully on GPU with on-the-fly cell discovery, and produces crystallographic-quality merged data (CC*=0.90) while rejecting non-crystal images.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Blind indexing of serial crystallography frames is highly unreliable when each frame contains only a sparse subset of reflections (the small-N regime), because multiple incorrect lattices can explain sparse observations. Existing methods index each frame independently, wasting the collective signal across the dataset. There is a need for an approach that leverages ensemble-level information to improve indexing robustness while maintaining throughput.

Method: GLINT treats the dataset as the unit of inference rather than individual frames. It: (1) generates candidate indexing solutions for many frames; (2) pools weak but recurrent lattice hypotheses across the ensemble to identify a shared consensus unit cell; (3) registers each frame against the consensus cell through a known-cell orientation search, which has lower dimensionality than blind indexing. The batched known-cell registration runs entirely on a single GPU, enabling on-the-fly cell discovery during data collection.

Result: In benchmark tests, GLINT matched the strongest blind indexer in the comparison on indexing yield at roughly 40x lower per-frame blind-indexing latency than XGANDALF's fastest configuration. On experimental serial data, frames indexed blind by GLINT merged to crystallographic quality (CC*=0.90). The method successfully rejected non-crystal images rather than forcing unsupported indexing assignments.

Conclusion: The authors demonstrate that indexing performance should be evaluated not only by indexing yield and throughput, but also by the quality of the resulting merged crystallographic data. GLINT's ensemble-based inference paradigm offers a scalable, GPU-accelerated solution for the small-N regime in serial crystallography.

Abstract: Classical crystallography determines orientation and unit-cell geometry from a rotation series collected from a single specimen, whereas serial crystallography acquires one still exposure from each of many randomly oriented microcrystals and must reconstruct a complete dataset by pooling partial measurements across the ensemble. In serial experiments, each frame typically contains only a subset of reflections, and frames with few reliable peaks (the small-N regime) are especially difficult to index blindly, because multiple incorrect lattices or orientations can explain sparse observations. The approach implemented in GLINT addresses this small-N regime by treating the dataset, rather than the individual frame, as the unit of inference. Candidate solutions are generated for many frames, weak but recurrent lattice hypotheses are pooled across the ensemble to identify a shared unit cell, and each frame is then registered against that consensus cell through a known-cell orientation search with lower dimensionality than blind indexing. In benchmark tests, GLINT matched the strongest blind indexer in the comparison on indexing yield at roughly 40x lower per-frame blind-indexing latency against XGANDALF's fastest configuration, and batched known-cell registration ran entirely on one GPU, allowing cell discovery on the fly during serial data collection. On experimental serial data, frames indexed blind by GLINT merged to crystallographic quality (CC* = 0.90), while the method rejected non-crystal images rather than forcing unsupported indexing assignments. These results demonstrate that indexing performance can be evaluated not only by indexing yield and throughput, but also by the quality of the resulting merged crystallographic data.

</details>

### [232] [Smoothed Picard Hamiltonian Monte Carlo](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06906)
*Fan Chen,Sinho Chewi,Jianfeng Lu,Matthew S Zhang*

Main category: math.ST

TL;DR: This paper introduces a smoothed Picard Hamiltonian Monte Carlo sampler for log-concave distributions, achieving improved gradient query complexity, and a recursive warm start framework that upgrades these to stronger divergence guarantees for high-accuracy sampling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to develop more efficient sampling algorithms for log-concave distributions by combining multiple techniques (Gaussian smoothing, Picard iteration, and higher-order discretization) to improve convergence rates, and to bridge the gap between low-accuracy and high-accuracy sampling guarantees.

Method: The method combines three ingredients: Gaussian smoothing, Picard iteration for solving integration problems, and higher-order discretization of Hamiltonian dynamics. The paper also proposes a recursive warm start generator framework that transforms W_q Wasserstein bounds into stronger divergence guarantees, which then provides a warm start for the proximal bouncy particle sampler.

Result: For log-concave targets with condition number κ, the smoothed Picard HMC achieves ε-accuracy in W_2 distance using Õ(κ² + κ^{7/6}d^{1/6}/ε^{1/3}) gradient queries. Stronger W_q bounds are also proven, and the recursive warm start framework yields a high-accuracy sampler with complexity Õ((κ^{7/6}d^{1/6} + κ^{1/2}d^{1/4})polylog(1/ε)).

Conclusion: The proposed framework achieves near-dimension-free and near-condition-number-optimal complexity for high-accuracy log-concave sampling, improving upon previous methods by decoupling low-accuracy and high-accuracy regimes.

Abstract: We develop a new low-accuracy sampler, called \emph{smoothed Picard Hamiltonian Monte Carlo}, which combines Gaussian smoothing, Picard iteration, and higher-order discretization. For a log-concave target $π\propto \exp(-V)$ in dimension $d$ satisfying $0 \prec αI \preceq \nabla^2 V \preceq βI$, with condition number $κ:= β/α$, smoothed Picard HMC returns a sample with $\sqrt α\,W_2(\cdot,π) \le \varepsilon$ using $\widetilde O(κ^2 + κ^{7/6} d^{1/6}/\varepsilon^{1/3})$ gradient queries. We also prove stronger $W_q$ bounds, and then develop an algorithmic framework, the recursive warm start generator, to upgrade these $W_q$ bounds to stronger divergence guarantees. This produces a warm start for the proximal bouncy particle sampler, introduced in a companion work, leading to a high-accuracy log-concave sampler with complexity $\widetilde O((κ^{7/6} d^{1/6} + κ^{1/2} d^{1/4})\mathrm{polylog}(1/\varepsilon))$.

</details>


<div id='math.DG'></div>

# math.DG [[Back]](#toc)

### [233] [Interface foliation near a minimal isoparametric hypersurface for the Allen-Cahn equation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05715)
*Guillermo Henry,Jimmy Petean*

Main category: math.DG

TL;DR: The paper constructs solutions to the Allen-Cahn equation on closed Riemannian manifolds with positive Ricci curvature, using proper isoparametric functions. These solutions are invariant along level sets, have exactly k nodal components, and concentrate near the unique minimal isoparametric hypersurface as the parameter λ tends to infinity, with uniformly bounded energy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To construct multi-nodal solutions to the Allen-Cahn equation that concentrate near minimal hypersurfaces, thereby relating solutions of the semilinear PDE to geometric objects (minimal submanifolds) in the context of positive Ricci curvature manifolds.

Method: The construction exploits the symmetries of isoparametric functions on manifolds of positive Ricci curvature. Since f has a unique minimal level set Γ, the authors reduce the problem to an ODE (radial-type profile equation along f), and build solutions with arbitrary prescribed number k of nodal components by matching a prescribed oscillatory profile across the minimal hypersurface Γ. The parameter λ is taken large to force concentration near Γ, and energy bounds follow from the isoparametric structure.

Result: For every positive integer k and sufficiently large λ, there exist Allen-Cahn solutions that are constant along level sets of f and have exactly k nodal components. These nodal components converge to the minimal isoparametric hypersurface Γ as λ→∞, and the energy remains uniformly bounded.

Conclusion: The work provides explicit multi-end solutions to the Allen-Cahn equation concentrating on a specific minimal hypersurface, confirming a higher-nodal extension of the classical one-interface (solution) concentration phenomenon and contributing to the variational/geometric understanding of the Allen-Cahn equation on curved spaces.

Abstract: Let $(M,g)$ be a closed Riemannian manifold of positive Ricci curvature. Let $f$ be a proper isoparametric function on $M$, and $Γ$ the unique level set of $f$ which is a minimal hypersurface. For any positive integer $k$, and $λ>0$ large enough, we construct a solution of the Allen-Cahn equation $ Δu + λ(u-u^3 ) =0$ which is constant along the level sets of $f$ and has exactly $k$ nodal components. We prove that the nodal components approach the minimal isoparametric hypersurface $Γ$ as $λ\rightarrow \infty$ and the energy is uniformly bounded for all such solutions.

</details>


### [234] [Short-time existence for the Schouten flow](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06503)
*Giovanni Catino,Carlo Mantegazza*

Main category: math.DG

TL;DR: The paper proves short-time existence and uniqueness for the critical Ricci-Bourguignon flow (equivalently the Schouten flow) on closed Riemannian manifolds of dimension n≥3, resolving the degeneracy in the standard DeTurck modification by extending the system with a scalar unknown r representing scalar curvature, which enables application of Vol'pert–Khudyaev theory for parabolic-hyperbolic systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: At the critical parameter ρ=1/(2(n-1)), the principal symbol of the linearized operator after the usual DeTurck modification has a zero eigenvalue, so standard parabolic theory cannot be applied directly. This short-time existence question was explicitly left open by previous Ricci–Bourguignon theory (Catino et al., 2017).

Method: The authors adjoin an independent scalar unknown r (meant to represent scalar curvature) and form an "extended" system coupling a strictly parabolic equation for the metric with a transport-reaction equation for r that has curvature-squared forcing. This extended system fits the framework of nonlinear composite parabolic–hyperbolic systems due to Vol'pert and Khudyaev, and a compact-manifold adaptation of their theorem yields a unique smooth solution. The difference R_g - r is then shown to satisfy a homogeneous linear parabolic equation, so the constraint propagates and the original critical flow is recovered.

Result: Short-time existence and uniqueness are established for the critical Ricci–Bourguignon/Schouten flow on closed smooth Riemannian manifolds with dimension n≥3, settling the open problem left by earlier theory.

Conclusion: The degeneracy at the critical Schouten parameter is successfully resolved through a scalar-extension technique, thereby completing the short-time well-posedness theory for the full range of the Ricci–Bourguignon parameter.

Abstract: Let $(M^n,g_0)$ be a closed smooth Riemannian manifold, with $n\geqslant 3$. We prove short--time existence and uniqueness for the "critical" {\em Ricci--Bourguignon flow} \begin{equation} \partial_t g=-2\operatorname{Ric}_g+\frac{R_g}{n-1}g\,. \end{equation} Up to a constant rescaling of time, this is the {\em Schouten flow}, since the right--hand side of the equation is $-2(n-2)$ times the Schouten tensor. At the critical parameter, however, the principal symbol of the linearization of the operator that we obtain after the usual "DeTurck modification" still has a zero eigenvalue. We resolve this degeneracy by adjoining an independent scalar unknown $r$, intended to represent the scalar curvature, and considering an "extended" system consisting of a strictly parabolic equation for the metric coupled to a transport--reaction equation for $r$ with a curvature--square forcing. The extended system belongs to the class of nonlinear composite parabolic--hyperbolic systems studied by Vol'pert and Khudyaev~\cite{VolpertKhudyaev1972}. Then, a standard compact--manifold adaptation of their theorem yields a unique smooth solution of the extended system. Finally, the difference $R_g-r$ satisfies a homogeneous linear parabolic equation, so the scalar--curvature constraint "propagates" and the original critical Ricci--Bourguignon flow is recovered. This settles the short--time existence and uniqueness problem at the critical ``Schouten'' value $ρ=1/(2(n-1))$, which was left open by the previous Ricci--Bourguignon theory in~\cite{CatinoEtAl2017}.

</details>


### [235] [Classification of Solutions to a Critical Fourth-Order Equation on Complete Non-compact Manifolds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07657)
*Huabin Li,Tian Wu,Xiao Zhou*

Main category: math.DG

TL;DR: This paper proves a rigidity theorem for the critical biharmonic equation on complete non-compact manifolds with non-negative Ricci curvature: if a positive finite energy solution exists, the manifold must be Euclidean space and the solution takes an explicit bubble form.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The study aims to extend rigidity/classification results for nonlinear elliptic PDEs (like the Yamabe problem and Lane-Emden type equations) to the fourth-order biharmonic case on non-compact manifolds, where geometric constraints may force Euclidean structure.

Method: The authors employ Bernstein's method combined with the continuity method to establish an optimal pointwise second-order derivative estimate. Using invariant tensors, they derive a differential inequality that leads to the rigidity result.

Result: The paper establishes that on a complete, connected non-compact manifold of dimension n≥5 with non-negative Ricci curvature, the existence of a positive finite energy solution to the critical biharmonic equation forces the manifold to be isometric to R^n, with the solution given by the explicit bubble formula at the critical exponent (n+4)/(n-4).

Conclusion: The rigidity phenomenon holds for the fourth-order critical biharmonic equation: geometric curvature assumptions (non-negative Ricci) combined with existence of finite energy solutions force the manifold to be Euclidean and pin down the exact solution form.

Abstract: We study the critical biharmonic equation \[ Δ^2 u=u^{\frac{n+4}{n-4}}, \] on a complete, connected, and non-compact Riemannian manifold \((M^n,g)\) of dimension \(n\geq 5\) with non-negative Ricci curvature. We establish an optimal pointwise second-order derivative estimate by Bernstein's method and the continuity method. Using invariant tensors, we derive a differential inequality that yields a rigidity result. More precisely, if there exists a positive finite energy solution, then the manifold is isometric to the Euclidean space \(\mathbb{R}^n\), and the solution is given by \[ u(x)= \frac{ \left[λ^2 n(n-4)(n^2-4)\right]^{\frac{n-4}{8}} }
{\left(1+λ|x-x_0|^2\right)^{\frac{n-4}{2}}}, \qquad λ>0,\quad x_0\in\mathbb{R}^n . \]

</details>


### [236] [Formation and structural stability of nondegenerate neckpinches](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08112)
*Kyeongsu Choi,Panagiota Daskalopoulos,Natasa Sesum*

Main category: math.DG

TL;DR: This paper proves that mean curvature flow hypersurfaces initially close to a generalized cylinder with localized quadratic upward bending must develop neckpinch singularities in finite time, establishes sharp asymptotic expansions showing these singularities are nondegenerate with polynomial profile, and proves structural stability of such singularities under C^2 perturbations—confirming nondegenerate neckpinches are generic in 3D mean curvature flow.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Building on the static rigidity of cylindrical self-shrinkers (Colding-Ilmanen-Minicozzi), the authors seek a dynamical counterpart: understanding when and how neckpinch singularities form, and whether they are stable/generic phenomena in mean curvature flow.

Method: The paper combines rigorous analytical techniques including: (1) a dynamical rigidity theorem proving finite-time neckpinch formation for data graphically close to a generalized cylinder with localized quadratic upward bending; (2) derivation of sharp asymptotic expansions for profile functions of the evolving graphs; (3) analysis showing rescaled graphical radius converges to a specific polynomial profile with quadratic bending (proving nondegeneracy); and (4) an openness theorem establishing C^2 structural stability of nondegenerate neckpinches, combined with Szekelyhidi's density theorems.

Result: The paper proves dynamical rigidity (neckpinch formation in finite time), establishes sharp asymptotic expansions of the singularity profile, demonstrates convergence to a polynomial profile with quadratic bending (nondegenerate singularity), and proves an openness/stability theorem under C^2 perturbations. Together with density results, this establishes that nondegenerate neckpinches form a generic and stable phenomenon in 3D mean curvature flow.

Conclusion: Nondegenerate neckpinch singularities are not just possible but are generic and structurally stable in 3-dimensional mean curvature flow, providing a complete dynamical counterpart to the static rigidity theory of cylindrical self-shrinkers.

Abstract: In this paper, we study the formation, precise asymptotics, and structural stability of neckpinch singularities in mean curvature flow. Motivated by the static rigidity of cylindrical self-shrinkers established by Colding, Ilmanen and Minicozzi, we first prove a dynamical rigidity result: mean curvature flow of hypersurfaces that are initially graphically close to a generalized cylinder on a sufficiently large scale, subject to a localized quadratic upward bending, inevitably develop a neckpinch singularity in finite time. We also establish sharp asymptotic expansions for the profile functions of these locally evolving graphs. We show that the rescaled graphical radius converges to a specific polynomial profile with quadratic bending, proving that the resulting singularities are nondegenerate. Finally, we establish an openness theorem showing that nondegenerate neckpinches are structurally stable under C^2 perturbations of the initial data. Combined with recent density theorems for the 3-dimensional mean curvature flow by Szekelyhidi, our results confirm that nondegenerate neckpinches constitute a generic and stable phenomenon in 3-dimensional mean curvature flow.

</details>


### [237] [Continuation and reduction of steady vortex sheets under metric deformations on a disk](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08361)
*Yuuki Shimizu*

Main category: math.DG

TL;DR: The paper studies local continuation of steady vortex sheets under conformal metric deformations on the unit disk, formulating the problem as a nonlinear residual with differing Sobolev orders. The derivative is Fredholm of index two under non-degeneracy; fixing mean normal displacement and total circulation yields an index-zero problem amenable to Lyapunov-Schmidt reduction. Fourier analysis of rotationally symmetric cases shows all modes k≥2 are nonresonant in constant-curvature disks, while mode k=1 yields a two-dimensional reduced problem linked to Killing fields but not nonconcentric branches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand when and how steady vortex sheet configurations can be continuously continued (branched) as the conformal metric on the disk is deformed, and to classify nondegenerate versus degenerate continuation scenarios including symmetry-breaking behavior.

Method: The moving sheet is represented as a normal graph; streamline and Bernoulli conditions are formulated as a nonlinear residual with two components of different Sobolev orders. Local continuation theory via Fredholm analysis (index two), normalization by mean normal displacement and total circulation, Lyapunov-Schmidt reduction, and Fourier modal decomposition for rotationally symmetric reference data.

Result: A locally unique normalized branch exists in the nondegenerate case, with explicit first- and second-order necessary conditions in the degenerate case. In constant-curvature disks, all modes k≥2 are nonresonant; mode k=1 yields a two-dimensional reduced problem whose kernel profiles are normal traces of Killing fields but do not create fixed-boundary symmetries or nonconcentric branches.

Conclusion: The continuation theory provides a classification of vortex sheet bifurcations under metric deformations, revealing that the first Fourier mode (associated with Killing fields) gives genuine degeneracy but does not generate nonconcentric sheet branches by itself, while higher modes are all stable under perturbation.

Abstract: We study the local continuation of a steady vortex sheet under a prescribed deformation $g_s= e^{2σ_s}g_{\mathrm{e}}$ of a conformal metric on the unit disk. Representing the moving sheet by a normal graph, we formulate the streamline and Bernoulli conditions as a nonlinear residual whose two components have different Sobolev orders. If the two one-sided tangential velocities of the reference sheet do not vanish simultaneously, the derivative of the unnormalized residual is Fredholm of index two. Fixing the mean normal displacement and the total circulation gives an index-zero problem; when the normalization differential restricted to the unnormalized kernel is onto $\mathbb{R}^2$, we obtain a parameter-dependent Lyapunov-Schmidt reduction. This yields a locally unique normalized branch in the nondegenerate case and explicit first- and second-order necessary conditions in the degenerate case. For a rotationally symmetric reference metric and a concentric circular sheet, the linearized problem separates into Fourier blocks and only finitely many modes can be resonant. In the constant-curvature disk family, every mode $k\geq2$ is nonresonant, whereas the first mode produces a two-dimensional reduced problem. The two first-mode kernel profiles are normal traces of ambient Killing fields, but they do not generate fixed-boundary symmetries or, by themselves, a nonconcentric branch.

</details>


### [238] [High-Dimensional Families of Minimal Surfaces of Arbitrary Genus in Round Spheres](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09016)
*Riccardo Caniato*

Main category: math.DG

TL;DR: The paper proves the existence of arbitrarily high-dimensional families of minimal surfaces with any prescribed genus and conformal structure in even-dimensional round spheres, constructed via sequences of degrees leading to complex manifolds of increasing dimension.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To establish that minimal surfaces in even-dimensional spheres exist in families of arbitrarily large dimension, for any genus and conformal structure, addressing a fundamental question about the moduli of minimal surfaces in spherical geometry.

Method: The authors construct a sequence of degrees d_l → +∞ such that for each l, there exists a complex manifold of dimension 2d_l + n^2(1-g) consisting of linearly full branched superminimal immersions from any closed Riemann surface Σ of genus g into S^{2n}.

Result: For every genus g and conformal structure, they produce complex manifolds of branched superminimal immersions whose complex dimensions grow to infinity, yielding parametrized families of linearly full branched minimal immersions with dimensions tending to infinity.

Conclusion: Arbitrarily high-dimensional families of minimal surfaces of any prescribed genus and conformal structure exist in all even-dimensional round spheres, demonstrating the richness of the space of minimal surfaces in spherical geometry.

Abstract: We prove the existence of arbitrarily high-dimensional families of minimal surfaces of any prescribed genus and conformal structure in even-dimensional round spheres. More precisely, let $n\geq2$ and let $Σ$ be any closed Riemann surface of genus $g$. We construct a sequence of degrees $d_\ell\to+\infty$ such that, for every $\ell$, there exists a complex manifold of complex dimension $2d_\ell+n^2(1-g)$ consisting of linearly full branched superminimal immersions of $Σ$ into $\mathbb S^{2n}$ of degree $d_\ell$. In particular, this yields parametrized families of linearly full branched minimal immersions whose dimensions tend to infinity.

</details>


<div id='physics.flu-dyn'></div>

# physics.flu-dyn [[Back]](#toc)

### [239] [Direct Numerical Simulation of Thermoacoustically Unstable Flames Via Non-Drifting Acoustic Delay Characteristic Boundary Conditions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05656)
*Benjamin L. Cookman,Jack R. C. King,Raphaël C. Assier,Steven J. Lind*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: This paper presents Direct Numerical Simulations (DNS) of thermoacoustically unstable premixed flames in a tube, capturing both primary instability (flame flattening) and secondary parametric instability with oscillating flame fingering due to the unsteady Rayleigh-Taylor effect. To reduce computational cost, DNS is limited to the flame region with acoustics modeled via NSCBC-based Acoustic Delay Characteristic Boundary Condition (ADCBC), plus a new Averaged Proportional and Integral Linear Relaxation (APILR) method that maintains time-averaged inflow velocity/outflow pressure for improved flame position control. Both methods are validated in inert and counterflow flame cases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Numerical simulations of thermoacoustic instability in premixed flames are scarce due to their prohibitively high computational expense, yet understanding primary and secondary instabilities is critical for predicting and controlling combustion dynamics.

Method: The authors use Direct Numerical Simulations confined to the region around the flame. Boundary acoustics are handled via the Acoustic Delay Characteristic Boundary Condition (ADCBC), extending NSCBC to model delayed reentry. A new Averaged Proportional and Integral Linear Relaxation (APILR) method adds an integral control term to the Classic Linear Relaxation (CLR) method, ensuring zero time-averaged inflow velocities to maintain control over flame position.

Result: The simulations reproduce nonlinear saturation of the primary instability as the flame flattens, and reveal an oscillating flame fingering behavior characteristic of the unsteady Rayleigh-Taylor effect during secondary instability. Both ADCBC and APILR methods are validated in inert and counterflow flame test cases, demonstrating their effectiveness.

Conclusion: The proposed ADCBC and APILR methods enable numerically efficient simulation of combustion instabilities at drastically reduced computational cost, making DNS of thermoacoustic instability more feasible and opening the door to broader parametric studies.

Abstract: Thermoacoustic instability in premixed flames results from the coupling between the flame's heat release, as determined by combustion parameters, and surrounding acoustics, as determined by combustor geometry. A primary instability results in flame flattening as intrinsic flame instability modes are stabilised. Secondary thermoacoustic instability results in a parametric flame instability and drastic growth of acoustic amplitudes. Due to their relative expense, numerical simulations of these phenomena remain scarce. In this work, Direct Numerical Simulations (DNS) of thermoacoustically unstable idealised premixed flames in a tube with acoustically closed upstream and open downstream ends are presented. Results herein demonstrate nonlinear saturation of the primary instability as the flame flattens as well as an oscillating flame fingering characteristic of the unsteady Rayleigh-Taylor effect. To reduce computational cost, we perform DNS only on the region surrounding the flame. Acoustics at in- and outflows are described using the Navier-Stokes Characteristic Boundary Condition (NSCBC) method to model their delayed reentry into the domain in a formulation referred to as the Acoustic Delay Characteristic Boundary Condition (ADCBC) method. A new Averaged Proportional and Integral Linear Relaxation (APILR) method is also introduced, which modifies the Classic Linear Relaxation (CLR) method to maintain time-averaged values of inflow velocity and outflow pressure. Here, an integral control term is used to remove non-zero equilibrium time-averaged inflow velocities which impinge control over flame position. Both new methods demonstrate their capability in inert and counterflow flames test cases. These methods enable the numerical simulation of combustion instabilities at significantly reduced computational expense.

</details>


### [240] [Physics-informed neural networks for viscoelastic fluid flows around a cylinder in a two-dimensional channel](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07861)
*Midhuna Suresh,Akanksha Gupta*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: This paper presents a Cholesky-PINN framework to solve Oldroyd-B viscoelastic fluid flow problems, overcoming the High Weissenberg Number Problem via Cholesky decomposition of the conformation tensor to ensure positive-definite stress, combined with sparse data assimilation and transfer learning to escape non-physical local minima. The method is validated on single-cylinder and 3-cylinder array geometries, demonstrating stability and bypassing traditional mesh requirements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Grid-based fluid dynamics solvers suffer from exhaustive meshing demands and ill-posed inverse problems. While PINNs offer a mesh-free alternative, they fail when applied to highly elastic Oldroyd-B fluids due to the High Weissenberg Number Problem (HWNP), where exponential stress growth near stagnation points causes training divergence.

Method: (1) Apply Cholesky decomposition to the conformation tensor to guarantee a positive-definite stress field, stabilizing gradients and preventing network crashes. (2) Use sparse data assimilation with targeted CFD data points to force the model toward the actual physical solution. (3) Accelerate training via transfer learning to push the network past non-physical local minima. (4) Use a constructive solid geometry approach to handle complex geometries without re-meshing.

Result: Validated on flow past cylindrical geometries for Reynolds numbers (Re) between 5-25 (single-cylinder setup), with relaxation time (λ) increased from 0.1 to 0.5. Successfully scaled to a complex 3-cylinder array, accurately capturing sharp viscoelastic wakes while bypassing tedious re-meshing steps of traditional CFD.

Conclusion: The combined Cholesky-PINN framework accurately captures sharp viscoelastic wakes and provides a stable computational tool for complex rheological modeling, offering a mesh-free alternative that overcomes both the HWNP and spatial bias limitations of standard PINNs.

Abstract: Grid-based fluid dynamics solvers routinely struggle with exhaustive meshing demands and ill-posed inverse problems. A practical mesh-free alternative is given by Physics-informed neural networks (PINNs) but, applying them to highly elastic Oldroyd-B fluids results in many training failures. The High Weissenberg Number Problem (HWNP), driven by the exponential stress growth near stagnation points is a major issue, which causes standard PINN optimizers to diverge. To prevent the network from crashing, we apply a Cholesky decomposition to the conformation tensor. This mathematical constraint stabilizes the gradients by guaranteeing a positive-definite stress field. Beyond mathematical stability, the inherent spectral bias of deep learning models can hinder the network from accurately capturing the highly elastic wake structures. Therefore, we used sparse data assimilation to force the model toward the actual physical solution. By anchoring the physics loss with targeted CFD data points and accelerating training via transfer learning, we successfully pushed the network past non-physical local minima. We validated this Cholesky-PINN approach on flow past cylindrical geometries for Reynolds numbers (Re) between 5-25, for the single-cylinder setup. In addition, the relaxation time (λ) is increased from 0.1 to 0.5 to test the stability of the network. Finally, we scale the framework to a complex 3-cylinder array which proved our constructive solid geometry approach completely bypasses the tedious re-meshing steps of traditional CFD. The combined framework accurately captured sharp viscoelastic wakes, providing a stable computational tool for complex rheological modeling.

</details>


### [241] [Sloshing reduction in a swaying tank with porous baffles using scaled boundary finite element method](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06141)
*Pramod,Aln,Choudhury,Aditi,Vijay,KG,Natarajan,S*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: This work investigates sloshing mitigation in a partially filled swaying tank using multiple thin porous baffles of various configurations, solved via linearized potential flow theory and the scaled boundary finite element method (SBFEM). Parametric studies show top-mounted baffles improve sloshing suppression by 50% over bottom-mounted vertical baffles, with top-mounted convex baffles being the most efficient configuration overall.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sloshing is an inevitable and adverse phenomenon in ocean-going vessels, motivating the need for effective mitigation strategies using porous baffles.

Method: The boundary value problem is solved using linearized potential flow theory with the scaled boundary finite element method (SBFEM). Flow through thin porous baffles follows Darcy's law, the domain is divided into minimal subdomains for star convexity, and higher-order polynomials represent the velocity potential along subdomain edges.

Result: Top-mounted baffles achieve 50% better sloshing suppression than bottom-mounted vertical baffles across all modes. A parametric study examined effects of configuration, porosity, tank width, baffle submergence depth, and spacing between baffles. Top-mounted convex baffles emerged as the most efficient configuration with balanced reduction across all sloshing modes.

Conclusion: The SBFEM-based model is validated against literature and effectively demonstrates that top-mounted convex porous baffle configurations provide the most balanced and efficient sloshing suppression.

Abstract: Sloshing is an inevitable phenomenon in an ocean-going vessel that can have adverse effects. In this work, the mitigation of sloshing is investigated using multiple thin porous baffles of various configurations in a partially filled swaying tank. The boundary value problem is solved within the framework of a linearized potential flow theory using the scaled boundary finite element method (SBFEM). The flow through the thin porous baffles is assumed to follow Darcy's law. The computational domain is divided into a minimum number of subdomains due to the presence of porous baffles and to ensure star convexity. Higher-order polynomials are used along each subdomain edge to represent the unknown field, i.e., velocity potential. The developed numerical model is validated with the known results in the literature. Subsequently, various results, such as the amplification factor and the forces of the tank wall, are presented and discussed for the effect of configuration, porosity, slosh tank width, depth of baffle submergence and the space between adjacent baffles. From the parametric study, it is observed that top-mounted baffles enhance sloshing suppression by $50\%$ compared to bottom-mounted vertical baffles, considering all sloshing modes. Assessing the overall effectiveness, top-mounted convex baffle configuration emerges as the most efficient configuration for sloshing suppression, achieving a well-balanced reduction across all modes.

</details>


### [242] [A Comparative Study of Finite-Volume-based Coupled and Segregated Reduced-Order Models for Incompressible Flows in Parametrized Domains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07472)
*Andrea Buffolini,Davide Oberto,Gianluigi Rozza*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: This paper compares two Reduced-Order Model strategies—a monolithic coupled solver and a segregated SIMPLE-based algorithm—for incompressible fluid dynamics in geometrically parametrized domains. Tested on two 2D steady benchmarks (lid-driven cavity and flow past a cylinder), the coupled approach converges faster after supremizer enrichment but needs more DOFs, while the segregated SIMPLE method provides better reconstruction accuracy in lower-dimensional spaces at the cost of slower convergence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the trade-offs between two prominent reduced-order solution strategies (coupled vs. segregated) for parametrized incompressible flow problems, particularly regarding accuracy and numerical efficiency.

Method: Comparative assessment of a monolithic coupled reduced-order solver and a segregated SIMPLE-based reduced-order algorithm on two steady 2D benchmark cases: lid-driven cavity flow and flow past a cylindrical obstacle. Both are evaluated on field reconstruction and aerodynamic coefficient prediction, with supremizer enrichment applied where appropriate.

Result: The coupled approach converges faster with supremizer enrichment but requires more degrees of freedom. The segregated SIMPLE algorithm achieves better reconstruction accuracy, especially for lower-dimensional reduced spaces, at the price of slower convergence.

Conclusion: A fundamental trade-off exists between the two ROM strategies: coupled solvers favor numerical efficiency (convergence speed), while segregated SIMPLE-based solvers favor accuracy in low-dimensional reduced spaces—suggesting algorithm selection should depend on problem-scale and accuracy requirements.

Abstract: This work presents a comparative analysis of Reduced-Order Models (ROMs) applied to incompressible fluid dynamics within geometrically parametrized domains. Two distinct reduced-order solution strategies are investigated and compared: a monolithic coupled solver and a segregated SIMPLE-based algorithm. Their performance is assessed on two steady, two-dimensional benchmark cases: a lid-driven cavity flow and a flow past a cylindrical obstacle. The two algorithms are compared in terms of fields evaluation and aerodynamic coefficients prediction. The computational results highlight a fundamental trade-off between accuracy and numerical efficiency. On the one hand, after an opportune supremizers enrichment, the coupled approach guarantees a faster convergence, despite the need of a larger number of degrees of freedom. On the other hand, the segregated SIMPLE algorithm yields superior reconstruction accuracy, particularly for lower-dimensional reduced spaces, at the cost of a slower convergence rate.

</details>

### [243] [Formation of grain boundaries in ductile single crystals under plane-strain simple shear: a block-coordinate finite element method](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07016)
*Khanh Chau Le,Thanh Danh Nguyen*

Main category: physics.class-ph

TL;DR: The paper studies spontaneous grain subdivision in ductile crystals under large plastic deformation, using continuum dislocation theory with a polyconvex elastic energy. A double-well condensed energy favors lamellar microstructures, regularized by dislocation density gradients. A block-coordinate FEM scheme resolves the microstructure numerically, matching closed-form analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand and model the pattern-forming instability where uniform single crystals spontaneously subdivide into misoriented grains separated by dislocation walls under large deformation, rooted in loss of convexity of elastic energy.

Method: Uses continuum dislocation theory with a polyconvex (Ciarlet-Geymonat) elastic energy for plane-strain simple shear. Minimizes over plastic slip to obtain a double-well condensed energy, with dislocation density gradients regularizing grain boundaries. A block-coordinate FEM (alternating convex non-smooth slip solve with Levenberg-regularized Newton deformation solve) resolves the microstructure numerically.

Result: The numerical scheme reproduces spontaneous onset of lamellar grain microstructure in agreement with closed-form analysis; grain boundaries have finite thickness and energy as functions of misorientation angle.

Conclusion: The polyconvex energy framework guarantees existence of minimizers, and the coupled theoretical-numerical approach successfully captures spontaneous grain subdivision as a microstructure pattern, demonstrating consistency between analytical predictions and numerical simulations.

Abstract: Large plastic deformation can drive an initially uniform single crystal to spontaneously subdivide into misoriented grains separated by thin dislocation walls -- a pattern-forming instability rooted in the loss of convexity of the crystal's elastic energy at large strain. We study this phenomenon for a ductile crystal in plane-strain simple shear within continuum dislocation theory, using a polyconvex (Ciarlet--Geymonat) elastic energy that guarantees existence of minimizers for the coupled deformation--slip problem. Minimizing over the plastic slip yields a condensed energy of double-well form whose non-quasiconvexity favours a lamellar microstructure; the gradient of the geometrically necessary dislocation density regularizes it, giving the grain boundaries a finite thickness and energy as functions of the misorientation angle. A block-coordinate finite element scheme -- alternating a convex non-smooth solve for the slip with a Levenberg-regularized Newton solve for the deformation -- resolves this microstructure numerically and detects its spontaneous onset, reproducing the lamellar grain structure in agreement with the closed-form analysis.

</details>

### [244] [Accelerating Fourier--Motzkin elimination: redundancy removal and the choice of variable elimination order](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07960)
*Shashaank Khanna*

Main category: cs.CC

TL;DR: This paper addresses computational bottlenecks in Fourier-Motzkin elimination (FME), a method for projecting polyhedra by eliminating variables one at a time. The authors show that Imbert's redundancy test cannot be naively combined with LP-based redundancy removal, but both methods can be soundly interleaved if Imbert's derivation records are re-initialised after each LP step. They also propose a heuristic for choosing the elimination order that yields 6–25× speedups on random polytopes and reduces intermediate inequalities by 1–2 orders of magnitude in entropic causal-structure descriptions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fourier-Motzkin elimination suffers from potentially doubly-exponential growth of intermediate inequalities, even though the final projection grows only exponentially. This practical bottleneck depends on two choices—redundancy removal strategy and variable elimination order—and current combinations of redundancy tests are not always sound, limiting scalability for applications such as entropic constraints of causal structures.

Method: The paper (1) shows via a counterexample that Imbert's redundancy test cannot be interleaved with LP-based redundancy removal, (2) proves the two methods can be soundly combined if Imbert's derivation records are re-initialised after each LP-based reduction step, and (3) proposes a variable-elimination ordering rule that trades increased resource usage for significant computational efficiency, validated empirically.

Result: On random polytopes, the proposed elimination-order rule reduces running time by a factor of 6 to 25 compared to a fixed order. For entropic descriptions of causal structures (250+ inequalities, 100+ eliminated variables), the rule keeps the number of handled inequalities at each step 1–2 orders of magnitude lower than with a fixed order.

Conclusion: Both redundancy-removal interleaving and elimination-order heuristics are important levers for making Fourier-Motzkin elimination practical. A sound combination of Imbert's test with LP-based removal is achievable with re-initialisation, and a principled variable-ordering rule yields large practical gains in the considered problem classes.

Abstract: Fourier-Motzkin elimination computes an inequality description of the projection of a polyhedron onto a subset of its coordinates by eliminating one variable at a time. It is used in several areas of optimisation and computer science, and it is a standard way of obtaining the entropic constraints of a causal structure, where the marginalisation over the latent variables produces such a projection. Its limitation is the growth of the intermediate systems of inequalities, which can be doubly exponential in the number of eliminated variables even though the projection itself grows only as a single exponential. In practice the computational overload of the method therefore depends on two choices: how the redundant inequalities are removed after each step, and the order in which the variables are eliminated. We consider both. We first show, by an explicit example, that Imbert's redundancy test cannot be interleaved with redundancy removal by linear programming. We show that the two methods, however, can be combined soundly if the derivation records used by Imbert's test are re-initialised after every step at which linear programming is used. We then propose a rule for choosing the elimination order of the variables that gives a significant computational advantage, however, at the cost of increased resource usage. We demonstrate this advantage on some random polytopes, where the rule reduces the running time by factors of between 6 and 25 compared with the same elimination under a fixed order. For entropic descriptions of causal structures, with more than 250 inequalities and more than 100 variables to eliminate, our rule keeps the number of inequalities handled at each step one to two orders of magnitude lower than a fixed order.

</details>


<div id='gr-qc'></div>

# gr-qc [[Back]](#toc)

### [245] [Picard Iteration for the Characteristic Initial Value Problem in Einstein Equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08421)
*Shengrong Wu*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: Presents an iteration algorithm that converts vacuum and Einstein scalar-field PDEs in double-null gauge into systems of ODEs, using spectral elements and projection methods for numerical solution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To find a way to transform nonlinear PDEs in general relativity (vacuum and Einstein scalar-field equations) into more tractable ODE systems, enabling efficient and accurate numerical simulation.

Method: Iteration algorithm in double-null gauge that reformulates nonlinear PDEs as ODE systems, numerically implemented via characteristic constraint solves, LGL spectral elements, pole-free spherical operators, Galerkin projection, along with independent first-order residual and consistency verification.

Result: A numerical method combining multiple advanced techniques (characteristic constraint solving, spectral elements, Galerkin projection) with built-in residual and consistency checks for validation.

Conclusion: The approach offers a viable numerical framework for solving Einstein equations in double-null coordinates, with built-in error verification mechanisms.

Abstract: We present an iteration algorithm for vacuum and Einstein scalar-field equations in double-null gauge, which transform the non-linear PDE into systems of ODE. The numerical realization combines characteristic constraint solves, LGL spectral elements, pole-free spherical operators, Galerkin projection, and independent first-order residual and consistency checks.

</details>


### [246] [Semilinear wave equations on the Witten bubble spacetime](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06609)
*Onyx Gautam*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: The paper studies nonlinear wave equations on the Witten bubble spacetime (a 4+1 dimensional SO(3,1)×U(1)-symmetric solution to Einstein vacuum equations), proving small-data global existence without symmetry assumptions and improved decay for U(1)-symmetric solutions to semilinear equations with a null condition, via novel linear wave equation estimates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The Witten bubble spacetime models the semiclassical instability of the Kaluza-Klein spacetime R^{3+1}×S^1, which is classically stable. Understanding nonlinear wave dynamics in this setting is important for probing the decay/instability structure of this geometric background, and no prior global existence results existed for nonlinear wave equations on this spacetime.

Method: The proof relies on novel estimates for the linear wave equation on the Witten bubble spacetime (previously studied by Bhawal-Viveshwara and Bachelot). Using these linear estimates, the authors establish small-data global existence for general semilinear equations (no symmetry assumptions) and improve decay rates for U(1)-symmetric solutions to equations satisfying a null condition.

Result: Small-data global existence of solutions to nonlinear wave equations on the Witten bubble spacetime is proven without symmetry assumptions, and improved decay is demonstrated for U(1)-symmetric solutions to semilinear equations satisfying a null condition.

Conclusion: This initiates the study of nonlinear wave equations on the Witten bubble spacetime and provides the first global existence results in this setting, establishing a framework based on novel linear estimates that could be extended to other nonlinear problems on this geometry.

Abstract: We initiate the study of nonlinear wave equations on the Witten bubble spacetime (also known as the "bubble of nothing"), which is an $\mathrm{SO}(3,1)\times \mathrm{U}(1)$-symmetric solution to the Einstein vacuum equations in $(4 + 1)$ dimensions. This spacetime was introduced by Witten to model the semiclassical instability of the Kaluza--Klein spacetime $\mathbf{R}^{3+1}\times S^1$ (which is classically stable by work of Huneau--Stingo--Wyatt (arXiv:2307.15267)). We prove a small-data global existence result without symmetry assumptions and an improved decay result for $\mathrm{U}(1)$-symmetric solutions to a class of semilinear equations satisfying a version of the null condition. Key to the proof are novel estimates for the linear wave equation, which has been studied in the physics literature by Bhawal and Viveshwara and in the mathematics literature by Bachelot (arXiv:1601.03682).

</details>

# cond-mat.soft [[Back]](#toc)

### [247] [A Reduced-Order Dynamical Model for the Ignition of Diver-Induced Cohesive Silt-Out on Sloping Beds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05479)
*Sandy Hardian Susanto Herho,Iwan Pramesti Anwar,Umar Abdurrahman,Faruq Khadami,Alfita Puspa Handayani,Karina Aprilia Sujatmiko,Dasapta Erwin Irawan*

Main category: cond-mat.soft

TL;DR: A layer-averaged reduced model of a downward fin thrust near a cohesive seabed predicts sediment lofting and whether disturbances self-limit or ignite into self-sustaining down-slope currents, with a closed-form critical bed slope threshold (~17° for silt) partitioning decay and growth basins.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether sediment lofted by a fin thrust near a cohesive seabed settles benignly or triggers a catastrophic self-sustaining turbidity current, which has practical consequences for diver safety (brief vs. catastrophic visibility loss).

Method: The authors reduce layer-averaged balances of fluid mass, sediment mass, and momentum to a temporal slab of fixed thickness in the weakly entraining limit, yielding an autonomous planar vector field governed by three dimensionless groups (drag against settling, erosion strength, near-bed concentration). Stability analysis via phase-plane methods identifies equilibria and basins.

Result: A quiescent equilibrium exists at the origin. Above a critical bed slope (closed-form, ratio of near-bed concentration × drag-settling number to erosion-strength number), an interior saddle equilibrium appears whose stable manifold partitions state space into decay and unbounded-growth basins. For silt, the ignition threshold is near 17°. Results confirmed numerically to machine precision; closure study shows critical slope is robust in existence but closure-dependent in value, with the linear erosion law being conservative.

Conclusion: The model isolates bed slope as the controlling parameter for ignition of self-sustaining down-slope sediment currents, providing an exact closed-form threshold and identifying conservative modeling choices for erosion closure.

Abstract: A downward fin thrust near a cohesive seabed lofts sediment. Whether this cloud settles or organizes into a self-sustaining down-slope current determines if a diver loses visibility briefly or catastrophically. We reduce the layer-averaged balances of fluid mass, sediment mass, and momentum to a temporal slab of fixed thickness in the weakly entraining limit. This yields an autonomous planar vector field in dimensionless near-bed speed and suspended load, governed by three dimensionless groups: drag against settling, erosion strength, and near-bed concentration. The field possesses a quiescent equilibrium at the origin and, above a critical bed slope obtained in closed form as the ratio of near-bed concentration times drag-settling number to erosion-strength number, an interior saddle equilibrium. The saddle's stable manifold partitions the state space into basins of decay and unbounded growth, establishing the exact threshold between self-limiting and igniting disturbances. On a flat bed, the origin attracts globally for all admissible erosion laws. For representative silt, the ignition threshold is near a 17-degree bed angle. The closed-form structure is confirmed numerically to machine precision. A closure study demonstrates the critical slope is robust in existence but closure-dependent in value, identifying the linear erosion law as the conservative choice. The model thus isolates bed slope as the controlling parameter for ignition.

</details>


### [248] [Valency-bounding correction potential for coarse-grained molecular dynamics simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07842)
*Vladimir Dmitriev,Ankit Gupta,Anton Goloborodko*

Main category: cond-mat.soft

TL;DR: Introduces the valency-bounding correction (VBC), a many-body modification of generic short-range pairwise potentials that smoothly suppresses attraction based on neighbor count, enabling simulations of valency-limited binding without angular degrees of freedom, patchy particles, or fixed binding-site geometry.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Valency-limited binding is crucial in biological and soft matter systems, but existing simulation approaches rely on patchy particles with anisotropic potentials that require rotational integration, small timesteps, and fixed binding-site geometry—limitations that are computationally expensive and physically inflexible.

Method: The VBC modifies generic short-range pairwise potentials with a many-body term that suppresses attraction when either interacting particle exceeds a prescribed valency. It uses no angular degrees of freedom, works with soft repulsive cores, and evaluates in just two passes over the neighbor list at standard pairwise cost. A tuned variant enables bonded-partner exchange via ordinary molecular dynamics.

Result: The VBC drives coordination numbers to prescribed valency with low error, departs from Wertheim and Flory-Stockmayer predictions via unrestricted ring formation, achieves nearly valency-independent cost on GPUs with almost 10x speedup over patchy-particle references, reproduces reentrant aggregation of repeat-expanded RNA, and remedies the Fisher-Ruelle thermodynamic instability of soft-core potentials with attraction.

Conclusion: The VBC provides an efficient, general, and physically flexible alternative to patchy-particle models for simulating valency-limited binding, released as an open-source GPU plugin for HOOMD-blue.

Abstract: Many systems in soft and living matter bind through a limited number of bonds per particle: proteins associate via discrete surface patches, nucleic acids form one-to-one contacts, and the phase behaviour of multivalent biomolecules is governed by the number of binding sites they carry. In simulations, valency limits are typically enforced with patchy particles, whose anisotropic potentials require integration of rotational degrees of freedom and combine hard cores with narrow patches, which forces small timesteps and commits the model to a fixed binding-site geometry that is often unknown, flexible or mobile. We introduce the valency-bounding correction (VBC), a many-body modification of generic short-range pairwise potentials that smoothly suppresses attraction once the neighbour count of either interacting particle exceeds a prescribed valency. The correction carries no angular degrees of freedom, applies on top of soft repulsive cores and evaluates in two passes over the neighbour list at the cost of a standard pairwise potential. The VBC drives the coordination number to the prescribed valency with low error, while its cluster statistics depart from Wertheim and Flory-Stockmayer predictions through unrestricted ring formation. A tuned variant exchanges bonded partners through ordinary molecular dynamics, reducing bond lifetimes at high saturation by an order of magnitude. On GPUs the cost of the correction is nearly independent of valency, reaching an almost tenfold advantage over a patchy-particle reference. We illustrate large-scale applications by reproducing the reentrant aggregation of repeat-expanded RNA, and show that the VBC also remedies the Fisher-Ruelle thermodynamic instability of soft-core potentials with attraction. The VBC is available as an open-source GPU plugin for HOOMD-blue.

</details>


### [249] [Exact Analytic Solution for the Time-Fractional Hunter-Saxton Equation with Caputo derivative](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06513)
*Weiguang Huang*

Main category: cond-mat.soft

TL;DR: This paper presents the first exact closed-form solution to the Caputo time-fractional Hunter-Saxton equation by using fractional separation of variables with an exponential spatial profile that cancels the nonlinear terms, reducing the PDE to a fractional relaxation ODE solved via the one-parameter Mittag-Leffler function.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors aim to extend the classical Hunter-Saxton equation — a model for director field dynamics in nematic liquid crystals — to the time-fractional regime, introducing memory effects, and to obtain an exact analytical solution that can serve as a benchmark for numerical methods and clarify the impact of fractional dynamics on nonlinear wave propagation.

Method: The method employs a fractional separation of variables strategy combined with an exponential spatial ansatz φ(x) = exp(b+x), which exactly cancels the nonlinear structure of the equation. This reduces the governing nonlinear fractional PDE to a fractional relaxation ODE, whose analytic solution is expressed via the one-parameter Mittag-Leffler function. Results are validated symbolically using the MathHandbook computer-algebra system.

Result: The paper derives the first exact closed-form solution of the Caputo time-fractional Hunter-Saxton equation. Quantitative comparisons in the fractional (0 < α < 1) and classical (α → 1) regimes demonstrate that fractional-order memory slows temporal relaxation relative to the exponential baseline.

Conclusion: The exact solution provides a reliable benchmark for numerical schemes and offers insight into how fractional dynamics influence nonlinear wave propagation in orientationally ordered fluids.

Abstract: A time fractional extension of the Hunter Saxton equation is examined, in which the temporal derivative of u_x is replaced by a Caputo derivative of order 0 < alpha <= 1. This modification introduces memory effects into a model traditionally associated with director field dynamics in nematic liquid crystals. By employing a fractional separation of variables strategy together with the exponential spatial profile phi(x) = exp(b+x), which cancels the nonlinear structure exactly, the governing nonlinear partial differential equation is reduced to a fractional relaxation ODE, whose closed form solution is the one-parameter Mittag Leffler function. The exact analytic solution is derived algebraically from the separation process. This appears to be the first exact closed form solution of the Caputo time fractional Hunter Saxton equation. The result is validated symbolically using the MathHandbook computer-algebra system. Quantitative analysis in both the fractional (0 < alpha < 1) and classical (alpha -> 1) limits demonstrates how fractional-order memory slows temporal relaxation relative to the exponential baseline. The solution provides a reliable benchmark for numerical schemes and clarifies how fractional dynamics influence nonlinear wave propagation in orientationally ordered fluids.

</details>


<div id='cs.SC'></div>

# cs.SC [[Back]](#toc)

### [250] [Physical Law Ecology: mapping multi-mechanism ecologies as the zeroth step of data-driven scientific discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08536)
*Xiongheng Bian,Xiangyu Cui,Ma Feng,Xiaoyan Shen*

Main category: cs.SC

TL;DR: This paper challenges the implicit assumption in equation discovery that systems obey a single governing law (K=1), and introduces Physical Law Ecology—a framework that first determines K* (the number of coexisting independent mechanisms) from data before searching for equations. Across four unrelated systems and 163 SPARC galaxies, it identifies K*=3 governing topologies, providing evidence against MOND's single-universal-acceleration hypothesis and reducing prediction error by 67-72% in engineering applications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing equation discovery methods assume systems follow a single governing law without verification, which is a primary bottleneck for scientific discovery in complex multi-mechanism systems. The paper argues that determining the number of coexisting independent mechanisms should be the first step, rather than an afterthought or assumption.

Method: The framework mines a pool of topologically distinct candidate equations, constructs a continuous dominance weight field across parameter space to describe how mechanisms coexist and succeed one another, and discovers analytic evolution laws governing mechanism succession with optional monotonicity constraints for irreversible physics.

Result: BIC consistently identifies K*=3 independent governing topologies across elastomer mechanics, pool boiling, galactic dynamics, and droplet evaporation. On 163 SPARC galaxies with 3,269 spatially resolved measurements, it recovers three gravitational laws providing evidence against MOND's single-universal-acceleration hypothesis (p<10^-34). Multi-law weighted prediction achieves 67-72% error reduction over single-equation baselines while maintaining interpretability.

Conclusion: Determining K* should be established as the zeroth step of scientific discovery, preceding and independent of equation search. This opens an orthogonal direction to symbolic regression—not finding better equations, but mapping the ecology of mechanisms governing complex systems.

Abstract: Every data-driven equation discovery method assumes (implicitly and without verification) that the target system obeys a single governing law ($K{=}1$). Here we show that this assumption is the primary bottleneck limiting scientific discovery in multi-mechanism systems, and introduce Physical Law Ecology, a framework that makes $K^*$ (the number of coexisting independent mechanisms) itself the first quantity to be determined from data. The framework automatically mines a pool of topologically distinct candidate equations, constructs a continuous dominance weight field across parameter space, and discovers analytic evolution laws governing mechanism succession---with optional monotonicity constraints encoding irreversible physics. Across four unrelated systems (elastomer mechanics, pool boiling, galactic dynamics, and droplet evaporation), BIC consistently identifies $K^*{=}3$ independent governing topologies. Applied to 163 SPARC galaxies (3,269 spatially resolved measurements), the framework autonomously recovers three gravitational laws whose coexistence provides evidence against the single-universal-acceleration hypothesis of MOND ($p<10^{-34}$). In engineering applications, multi-law weighted prediction reduces error by 67-72\% over single-equation baselines while retaining full interpretability. By establishing the determination of $K^*$ as the zeroth step of scientific discovery-prior to and independent of equation search---this work opens a direction orthogonal to existing symbolic regression: not finding better equations, but mapping the ecology of mechanisms that govern complex systems.

</details>
