# cs.SE [[Back]](#toc)

### [251] [FPScan: An Automated Constraint-Based Analyzer for Floating-Point Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07492)
*Julien Bortolussi,Dorra Ben Khalifa,Pierre-Loïc Garoche*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: FPScan is a novel tool that formally detects catastrophic cancellation and absorption in floating-point programs by combining static analysis via abstract interpretation, first-order constraint modeling of error propagation, and SMT solving, validated on the FPBench benchmark suite.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Writing error-free floating-point programs is challenging, and existing techniques for bounding rounding errors rarely address critical floating-point pitfalls like absorption and catastrophic cancellation, which can cause finite-precision semantics to deviate significantly from real-number semantics.

Method: FPScan uses (1) a custom static analyzer based on abstract interpretation to infer the order of magnitude of all program variables, (2) builds first-order constraints that model error propagation and numerical precision from this magnitude information, and (3) employs an off-the-shelf SMT solver to determine whether the program exhibits catastrophic cancellation or absorption.

Result: Experiments on the FPBench benchmark suite evaluated the effectiveness of FPScan, with comparisons against state-of-the-art tools in terms of soundness and analysis time.

Conclusion: FPScan provides a formal, sound approach to detecting critical numerical pitfalls in floating-point programs, offering a practical tool for programmers lacking deep numerical analysis expertise.

Abstract: Writing error-free floating-point programs is a challenging task, especially for programmers who lack a strong background in numerical analysis and rounding-error propagation. State-of-the-art techniques typically aim to bound such errors using static or dynamic analysis. However, only a few tools explicitly address critical floating-point pitfalls such as absorption and catastrophic cancellation. These anomalies represent situations in which rounding errors are significantly amplified, causing the semantics of the finite-precision computation to deviate substantially from the real-number semantics. In this article, we present FPScan, a novel tool to formally define and detect both catastrophic cancellation and absorption in floating-point programs. Our approach starts with a custom static analyzer based on abstract interpretation to infer the order of magnitude of all program variables. This magnitude information is then used to build a set of first-order constraints that model error propagation and numerical precision within the program. Finally, we employ an off-the-shelf SMT solver to determine whether the program exhibits any of these critical numerical pitfalls. Experiments were conducted on FPBench, a well-known benchmark suite of floating-point programs, to evaluate the effectiveness of our tool. We also present a comparison with state-of-the-art tools regarding soundness and analysis time.

</details>


<div id='cs.IT'></div>

# cs.IT [[Back]](#toc)

### [252] [A Nuclear-Norm Lower Bound for Dithered Scalar Quantization of Matrix Products](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05641)
*Piyush Sao,Narasinga Miniskar,Pedro Valero-Lara,Keita Teranishi,Sudip Seal*

Main category: cs.IT

TL;DR: The paper analyzes how to minimize rounding error in quantized matrix multiplication C=AB by optimizing over product-preserving transformations that change factor ranges and quantization grid steps without altering C. They prove a tight lower bound on expected squared error and show SVD-aligned Hadamard/DCT constructions achieve or nearly achieve this bound.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantized matrix multiplication introduces rounding errors whose scale depends on the ranges (maximum absolute entries) of the factor matrices' rows and columns, which determine quantization grid steps. The authors seek to understand the fundamental limits of error reduction by exploiting the gauge freedom — transformations that preserve the product AB but can improve conditioning for quantization.

Method: The authors optimize over product-preserving transformations: invertible inner changes of basis and orthogonal outer rotations. They model quantization noise as independent, zero-mean subtractive dither on an unbounded lattice, analyze leading-order expected squared error, and construct near-optimal schemes using SVD-aligned Hadamard and DCT transforms, plus Gram-matrix balancing and finite-set flattening alternatives.

Result: They prove the output-only bound E_lead >= (c_A+c_B)/K * ||AB||_*^2 (nuclear norm bound), which is tight via an SVD-aligned Hadamard construction when a Hadamard matrix of order K exists (including all powers of two). An SVD-aligned DCT construction is within a factor of two for any K. Gram-matrix balancing and finite-set flattening achieve the bound within C log(K(m+n)). For power-of-two K, conditional expectations deterministically select Hadamard signs in O((m+n)K^2) operations.

Conclusion: The results fully characterize the full-gauge (inner basis + outer rotation) optimum for quantized matrix multiplication error, and quantify the cost of restricting to transformations that preserve row and column indices versus full gauge freedom. Synthetic experiments confirm the theoretical constructions and illustrate the tradeoff between regularization and conditioning.

Abstract: We consider the problem of minimizing error in quantized matrix multiplication $C=AB$. Scalar quantization of the factors introduces rounding errors whose scale depends on the maximum absolute entries -- the ranges -- of their rows and columns. These ranges determine the quantization grid steps. To reduce the error, we optimize over product-preserving transformations that alter the factor ranges and grid steps without changing $C$. Specifically, we seek the smallest leading expected squared error over invertible inner changes of basis and orthogonal outer rotations. Under independent, zero-mean subtractive dither noise on an unbounded lattice, we prove the output-only bound $E_{\rm lead} \ge (c_A+c_B)/K \Vert AB\Vert_*^2$, where $K$ is the inner dimension, $c_A$ and $c_B$ are normalized noise variances, and $\Vert AB\Vert_*$ is the nuclear norm. The bound is tight: an SVD-aligned Hadamard construction attains the infimum whenever a Hadamard matrix of order $K$ exists, including every power of two, while an SVD-aligned DCT construction is within a factor of two for every $K$. Without outer rotations, Gram-matrix balancing minimizes factorization energy, and finite-set flattening achieves the bound within $C\log(K(m+n))$. For power-of-two $K$, conditional expectations deterministically select the Hadamard signs in $O((m+n)K^2)$ exact-real operations. Synthetic experiments verify both constructions and illustrate the tradeoff between regularization and conditioning. These results characterize the full-gauge optimum and quantify the cost of preserving row and column indices.

</details>


<div id='math.SP'></div>

# math.SP [[Back]](#toc)

### [253] [Neumann--Dirichlet eigenvalue comparison at the first Dirichlet threshold in the plane](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05893)
*Quanyu Tang,Haiqi Zhang*

Main category: math.SP

TL;DR: The paper proves that for any bounded connected Lipschitz domain in the plane, the third Neumann Laplacian eigenvalue is strictly less than the first Dirichlet eigenvalue, removing the simple-connectivity assumption from prior work. It also establishes a three-spectrum counting inequality involving Dirichlet, Neumann, and conductivity spectra.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To extend the previously known planar result μ_3(Ω) < λ_1(Ω) beyond simply connected domains to all bounded connected Lipschitz domains in ℝ², and to develop a broader comparative framework relating different spectral problems.

Method: The proof removes the simple-connectivity assumption by developing new analytical techniques. A three-spectrum counting inequality is also established relating the Dirichlet, Neumann, and conductivity spectra.

Result: The strict inequality μ_3(Ω) < λ_1(Ω) holds for all bounded connected Lipschitz domains in the plane (without simple-connectivity). A three-spectrum counting inequality is also proven.

Conclusion: The result fully generalizes the planar comparison of the third Neumann eigenvalue to the first Dirichlet eigenvalue, and the new counting inequality offers a unified perspective across three related spectral problems.

Abstract: Let $Ω\subset\mathbb R^2$ be a bounded connected Lipschitz domain, and let $\{μ_j(Ω)\}_{j\geq1}$ and $\{λ_j(Ω)\}_{j\geq1}$ denote the Neumann and Dirichlet Laplacian eigenvalues, respectively, counted with multiplicity. We prove that $$ μ_3(Ω)<λ_1(Ω), $$ thereby removing the simple-connectivity assumption from the previously known planar result at the first Dirichlet threshold. We also establish a three-spectrum counting inequality relating the Dirichlet, Neumann, and conductivity spectra.

</details>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [254] [Simulating the Marginal Green Contribution of AI Modules in a Smart-Agriculture Platform: Evidence from Two Monte Carlo Experiments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06740)
*Zhaoyang Li,Ruijie Zhang,Zhaoji Sun,Lu Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper decomposes smart agriculture platforms into explicit components (AI diagnosis, IoT sensing, decision push) and uses two controlled simulation experiments to quantify the green benefit attributable to each component, finding that farmer adoption—not algorithm accuracy—is the primary bottleneck for meeting green targets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Smart agriculture platforms bundle AI diagnosis, IoT sensing, and decision push into a single package, making it unclear what green benefit each component contributes. This ambiguity prevents quantitative evidence for resource-allocation decisions.

Method: Building on a prior platform-level Monte Carlo assessment, the paper makes individual components explicit and runs two controlled simulation experiments. Experiment 1 models a causal chain from AI capability to farmer behavior to agrochemical reduction, using a formula of avoidable blind-application share × prescription effectiveness × decision-touch coverage × adoption rate, comparing extension mode with AI mode. Experiment 2 compares three scenarios: current practice (P0), IoT engineering retrofit (P1), and P1 plus AI irrigation scheduling (P2). Sensitivity analyses test adoption rates, diagnosis accuracy, and soil-moisture sensing errors.

Result: In Experiment 1, the probability of reaching 20% pesticide reduction is near zero under extension mode but 20.7% at baseline under AI, rising to 49% with 0.95 diagnosis accuracy and 0.85 adoption; probability of 15% fertilizer reduction rises from near zero to 52.0%. In Experiment 2, median water savings rise from 7.8% (P0) to 11.0% (P1) and 16.0% (P2); AI adds 5.0 percentage points over engineering alone; paddy CH4 reduction reaches 30.5% under AI scheduling vs 19.8% manual; rice irrigation-methane subsystem carbon intensity declines 27.9%. Sensitivity analyses show the primary bottleneck is farmer adoption rather than algorithm accuracy, and AI data fusion is robust to soil-moisture sensing errors.

Conclusion: The work provides a reproducible simulation framework for component-level green-value evaluation and promotion-strategy optimization of smart agriculture platforms, enabling quantitative resource-allocation decisions.

Abstract: Smart agriculture platforms usually bundle AI diagnosis, IoT sensing and decision push into a single package, so the green benefit attributable to each component remains unclear and resource-allocation decisions lack quantitative evidence. Building on a previous platform-level Monte Carlo assessment, this paper makes the components explicit and runs two controlled simulation experiments. Experiment 1 follows the chain from AI capability to farmer behavior to agrochemical input reduction, modeling pesticide/fertilizer reduction as avoidable blind-application share times prescription effectiveness times decision-touch coverage times adoption rate, and compares an experienced-extension mode with the AI mode: the probability of reaching 20% pesticide reduction is essentially zero in the extension mode but 20.7% at baseline, up to 49% with diagnosis accuracy 0.95 and adoption 0.85 under AI; the probability of 15% fertilizer reduction rises from near zero to 52.0%. Experiment 2 compares current practice (P0), IoT engineering retrofit (P1), and P1 plus AI irrigation scheduling (P2): median aggregate water saving rises from 7.8% (P0) to 11.0% (P1) and 16.0% (P2), with AI adding 5.0 percentage points beyond engineering; paddy CH4 reduction reaches 30.5% under AI scheduling versus 19.8% under manual operation, and the rice irrigation-methane subsystem carbon intensity declines 27.9%. Sensitivity analyses of both experiments consistently indicate that the primary bottleneck for meeting green targets is farmer adoption rather than algorithm accuracy, and that AI data fusion is robust to soil-moisture sensing errors. This work provides a reproducible simulation framework for component-level green-value evaluation and promotion-strategy optimization of smart agriculture platforms.

</details>


<div id='quant-ph'></div>

# quant-ph [[Back]](#toc)

### [255] [Matrix Product Belief Propagation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05598)
*Gabriel Woolls,Shahin Jahanbani,Adarsh Pashikanti,Matthew T. Fishman,Joseph Tindall,Michael P. Zaletel*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: MP-BP is a controlled method for approximate contraction of 2D tensor networks that doubles the number of accurate digits at similar computational cost by generalizing belief propagation to matrix-product messages on surfaces.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods for contracting 2D tensor networks and graphical models suffer from limited accuracy at given computational effort. The authors seek a principled, controlled approach that systematically reduces errors, ideally quadratically relative to current techniques.

Method: The paper introduces matrix product belief propagation (MP-BP), where messages live on surfaces and are assumed to have a matrix-product factorization. With unit matrix-product rank it reduces to standard BP. For infinite systems it implements Baxter's corner-transfer-matrix method, and for reflection-symmetric networks it coincides with boundary MPS and CTRG algorithms. The quadratic error improvement is extended to expectation value computation.

Result: MP-BP achieves a quadratic reduction of errors relative to existing methods: at similar computational effort, the number of accurate digits is asymptotically doubled. Numerical demonstrations across classical and quantum 2D tensor network problems show qualitatively improved convergence.

Conclusion: MP-BP provides a unifying and controlled framework that generalizes belief propagation and connects to established tensor network renormalization methods, offering systematically improved accuracy for 2D tensor network contraction and expectation value evaluation.

Abstract: We introduce "matrix product belief propagation" (MP-BP) as a controlled method for the approximate contraction of two-dimensional tensor networks and graphical models, which generically leads to a quadratic reduction of errors relative to existing methods: at similar computational effort, the number of accurate digits is asymptotically doubled. For infinite systems, MP-BP can be understood as a practical implementation of Baxter's corner-transfer-matrix method; for reflection-symmetric networks, it further coincides with the boundary matrix-product-state and corner-transfer-matrix renormalization group algorithms as conventionally implemented. In the limit of unit matrix-product rank, MP-BP is equivalent to belief propagation, and more generally, can be understood as a generalization of BP in which the "messages" live on surfaces and are assumed to have a matrix-product factorization. We further extend the quadratic improvement to the computation of expectation values, and numerically demonstrate the qualitatively improved convergence for a variety of classical and quantum 2D tensor network problems.

</details>


### [256] [Propagating sparsely supported states with time-dependent neural quantum states](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06054)
*Lexin Ding,Markus Reiher*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper introduces interpolation sampling, a novel importance sampling method for time-dependent neural quantum states (NQSs), where samples are drawn from a distribution interpolating between the wave function and its time derivative. This addresses the support mismatch problem when propagating sparsely supported initial states. Combined with a global-in-time variational principle, this enables accurate propagation of extremely peaked states in the 2D transverse-field Ising model, with additional improvements via configuration-time joint sampling and Krylov-informed hybrid proposal strategies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing time-dependent NQS methods fail to propagate sparsely supported initial states because of the inherent mismatch between the support of such states and their time derivatives, creating an entanglement barrier that these methods cannot overcome despite NQSs' high entanglement capacity.

Method: The authors propose: (1) interpolation sampling, a novel importance sampling method drawing samples from a distribution that interpolates between the wave function and its time derivative; (2) combination with a global-in-time variational principle; (3) a configuration-time joint sampling scheme treating spin configurations and time as random variables; and (4) a hybrid sample proposal strategy incorporating Krylov subspace knowledge.

Result: Using the two-dimensional transverse-field Ising model, interpolation sampling enables accurate propagation of extremely peaked states that standard wave function sampling could not handle, while the additional sampling efficiency improvements further enhance performance.

Conclusion: This work extends time-dependent NQS methods to a wider range of physically relevant scenarios and challenges the prevailing reliance on the Born distribution and its close variants as the default sampling basis.

Abstract: Neural quantum states (NQSs) have emerged as a powerful ansatz for quantum dynamics. Their high entanglement capacity promises to overcome the entanglement barrier. However, existing time-dependent NQS methods are ill-equipped to propagate sparsely supported initial states due to the inherent mismatch between the supports of such states and their time derivatives. To address this issue, we introduce the interpolation sampling method as a novel form of importance sampling, where samples are drawn from a distribution that interpolates between the wave function and its time derivative. Combined with a global-in-time variational principle, we demonstrate with the example of the two-dimensional transverse-field Ising model that interpolation sampling allows for accurate propagation of extremely peaked states that standard wave function sampling could not tackle. We further improve sampling efficiency by introducing (i) a configuration-time joint sampling scheme where spin configurations and time are both treated as random variables, and (ii) a hybrid strategy for sample proposal that incorporates the knowledge of a Krylov subspace. Our work extends the scope of time-dependent NQS methods to a wider range of physically relevant scenarios, while challenging the prevailing reliance on the Born distribution and its close variants as the default basis for sampling.

</details>


### [257] [Geometric inflation of deviations challenges neural quantum states in dynamics of quantum Ising models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07645)
*Wladislaw Krinitsin,Jonas B. Rigo,Mohammad Abedi,Markus Schmitt*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper investigates why neural quantum state (NQS) simulations of quench dynamics in a 1D quantum Ising model are surprisingly challenging. Using supervised state reconstruction, they show representational capacity is not the bottleneck. Instead, they identify a geometric effect—rotation of the quantum geometric tensor (QGT) null space—that amplifies small parameter deviations, causing instability in the time-dependent variational principle (TDVP). This sensitivity originates from the non-linearity of the neural network ansatz, and they propose QGT-null-space rotation as a diagnostic and target for improved TDVP algorithms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand why NQS-based simulations of non-equilibrium quantum dynamics fail in certain regimes despite NQS being a powerful representation, and to identify the true source of the difficulty rather than attributing it to representational limitations.

Method: They benchmark quench dynamics in a 1D quantum Ising model, use supervised state reconstruction to establish tighter empirical bounds on required parameters, and analyze the TDVP dynamics focusing on the geometric structure of the quantum geometric tensor (QGT), comparing neural network and matrix product state (MPS) solvers.

Result: They establish substantially tighter upper bounds on required parameter counts than previous estimates, ruling out representational capacity as the obstruction. They discover that dynamical rotation of the QGT kernel can suddenly amplify previously irrelevant small parameter deviations—a geometric inflation effect. Stable MPS solutions to the same TDVP suggest neural network non-linearity underlies this sensitivity.

Conclusion: QGT-null-space rotation is identified as a geometric diagnostic for sensitive NQS dynamics and offers a concrete target for improving TDVP algorithms in neural quantum state simulations.

Abstract: Neural quantum states (NQS) have emerged as a powerful framework for simulating non-equilibrium dynamics in strongly correlated quantum systems, offering scalable variational representations of highly entangled states. Yet, accurate NQS simulations have been found to be surprisingly challenging in some physical regimes of limited complexity. Here, we address paradigmatic quench dynamics of a one-dimensional quantum Ising model as a controlled benchmark. Through supervised state reconstruction we establish substantially tighter empirical upper bounds on the required parameter count than previous estimates, ruling out representational limitations as the key obstruction. Instead, we uncover a geometric inflation of small deviations as a hitherto overlooked challenge for accurate solutions of the infinitesimal time-dependent variational principle (TDVP): the dynamical rotation of the kernel of the quantum geometric tensor (QGT) can suddenly lend physical significance to previously irrelevant parameter deviations. The stability of matrix product state solutions of the same TDVP suggests that the non-linearity of the neural network ansatz is the origin of the sensitivity. These results identify QGT-null-space rotation as a geometric diagnostic of sensitive NQS dynamics and as a concrete target for improving TDVP algorithms

</details>


### [258] [From Bits to Qubits: The Theory and Practice of Quantum Data Encoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08058)
*Xiao-Ming Zhang,Arthur G. Rattew,Bujiao Wu,Georgios Styliaris,Xiaoming Sun,Bálint Koczor,Xiao Yuan*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A comprehensive review of quantum data encoding, covering access models (state preparation, unitary synthesis, QRAM, block encoding), resource requirements (circuit size, depth, non-Clifford gates), structured data handling, and the role of encoding in enabling practical quantum advantage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum data encoding is a foundational bottleneck in realizing practical quantum advantage, since classical data must be efficiently loaded into quantum systems before nearly all quantum algorithms can execute.

Method: The paper provides a systematic survey of quantum data encoding concepts, tracing development from early foundations to recent advances, analyzing access models (quantum state preparation, unitary synthesis, QRAM, block encoding), evaluating fault-tolerant resource requirements (circuit size, depth, space-time tradeoffs, non-Clifford resources), and examining structured data formats such as sparse data, Boolean functions, and tensor network representations.

Result: A comprehensive pedagogical guide and reference that bridges theory and applications, mapping the resource costs and tradeoffs across different encoding approaches and access models, while clarifying their respective roles in quantum algorithms.

Conclusion: Quantum data encoding plays a pivotal role in quantum computing, and understanding the tradeoffs across encoding methods is essential for identifying future directions that will enable practical quantum advantage.

Abstract: Encoding classical data into quantum systems is a foundational step in the execution of nearly all quantum algorithms, and a critical bottleneck in realizing practical quantum advantage. This review provides a comprehensive account of the concepts, algorithms, and practical considerations associated with quantum data encoding. We trace the development from its early conceptual foundations to recent advances, considering commonly used access models, such as quantum state preparation, unitary synthesis, QRAM and block encoding. We survey the circuit size, depth, space-time tradeoffs, as well as non-Clifford resources required for fault-tolerant implementation. We also discuss the roles of different access models in quantum algorithms. Special attention is given to structured data, such as sparse data, Boolean functions and data represented by tensor networks. This review bridges theory and applications, serving both as a pedagogical guide for newcomers and as a reference for active researchers. We also highlight the pivotal role of quantum data encoding in quantum computing and provide insights into future directions that will enable quantum advantage.

</details>


### [259] [Effects of Trotter Error, Digitization Error, and Initial State Overlap on Tapered Quantum Phase Estimation for Minimum Eigenvalue Computation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06249)
*Elijah Pelofske,Stephan Eidenbenz*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper numerically implements full quantum circuits of optimal tapered Quantum Phase Estimation (tQPE) using an approximate bandwidth-limited DPSS taper for minimum eigenvalue computation on quantum Hamiltonians, with time-evolution via Trotterization. Through exact classical simulations on a 4-qubit Heisenberg model (up to 10 phase qubits, 12th-order Trotterization), the authors investigate how algorithm parameters affect performance, finding that optimal phase sampling rate depends on ground-state overlap, evolution time has weak dependence absent high Trotter error, and Trotterization-induced ringing can produce non-physical eigenvalue estimates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to understand the practical performance of tapered Quantum Phase Estimation (tQPE) under realistic conditions—including Trotterization, finite sampling, imperfect initial states, and digitization—which are typically present in actual quantum computations but often idealized away in theoretical analyses of QPE.

Method: The authors implement full quantum circuits of approximate bandwidth-limited DPSS-tapered QPE using exact classical numerical circuit simulations. They apply standard Trotterization for Hamiltonian time-evolution and systematically vary parameters (Trotter error, sampling, initial state quality, total evolution time, number of phase qubits) while measuring phase sampling rate, absolute error, and Shannon entropy. A 4-qubit Heisenberg magnet model is used as the test case with up to 10 phase qubits and 12th-order Trotterization.

Result: Three key findings emerge: (i) the steady-state optimal phase sampling rate is governed by the initial state overlap with the ground state; (ii) evolution time has minimal impact on phase sampling probability or eigenvalue error unless Trotter error is large; (iii) approximated time-evolution unitaries in tQPE can produce substantial ringing (Gibbs-like oscillations), yielding non-physical eigenvalue estimates similar to standard QPE.

Conclusion: Tapered QPE performs robustly across many parameter regimes, but practical implementations with finite Trotterization steps can introduce artifacts such as ringing that lead to unphysical results. These numerical insights provide guidance for choosing tQPE parameters and understanding its limitations in near-term quantum hardware applications.

Abstract: We numerically implement full quantum circuits of optimal tapered Quantum Phase Estimation (in the form of an implementable approximate bandwidth-limited DPSS taper) for the purpose of minimum eigenvalue computation, where the Hamiltonian time-evolution unitary is implemented using standard Trotterization, and the taper state is constructed using an optimized bandwidth-limited taper. We illustrate, on representative quantum Hamiltonians, with tractable exact classical numerical quantum circuit simulations, how tapered QPE performs with respect to optimal phase sampling rate, absolute error, Shannon entropy of the optimal phase distribution, when the tQPE algorithm parameters are changed. Those algorithm parameters are Trotter error, finite sampling, imperfect initial states, total evolution time, and digitization error (number of phase qubits). The reported numerical experiments include up to $\ell=10$ phase qubits of precision with $m=3$ additional phase-register error suppression qubits, on a $4$-qubit Heisenberg quantum magnet model Hamiltonian, using up to 12th-order Trotterization. We numerically show i) the steady-state optimal phase sampling rate is determined by the initial state overlap with the ground-state, ii) with respect to phase sampling probability, or overall eigenvalue sampling error rate, there is no strong evolution time dependence for tQPE unless there is high Trotter error, iii) the approximated time-evolution unitaries in tQPE, like in standard QPE, can result in substantial ringing, which leads to non-physical eigenvalue estimates.

</details>
